Welcome · C Programming

0.1 Why C is still the right answer0.2 The toolchain you will actually use0.3 What to know before you start0.4 How this guide is organised

0.1 Why C is still the right answer

C is the language the machine is described in, and this guide is written for the programmer who now needs to be fluent in it - not as an exercise, but because the next library, kernel module, driver, firmware image or performance-critical service they are handed is written in it.

What C actually is, underneath the syntax

C is a small language with a large vocabulary. The language proper has eight keywords for control flow, one type of function, no way to define a class, no garbage collector, and a memory model that is exactly the memory model of the processor you are running on. Everything else in the ecosystem - the containers, the smart pointers, the string classes, the coroutine library - is a library written in that tiny language, not a feature of it. That is the single most useful thing to understand about C before learning it, because it tells you where every question you have will be answered: if the feature is not in the language, it is in a header, and the header is somebody's code with somebody's bugs and somebody's performance characteristics. Then the three things C is genuinely good at, and each has a reason the other languages cannot match. It compiles to something a linker can place exactly where you asked. There is no hidden allocation, no hidden copy, no runtime that decides what your data structure looks like in memory, so a structure declared in C occupies precisely the bytes its fields require plus whatever padding the rules impose - and that is the property every systems library depends on. It has a defined interface to the operating system. The system call interface on every mainstream platform is expressed in C, so the distance between a C program and the kernel is one function call with no marshalling and no binding layer. And its cost model is knowable. When a hot loop is slow you can count its instructions, because there is no dispatch, no allocation and no indirection you did not write. Then the three things C is genuinely bad at, and each is a consequence of the same minimalism rather than a missing feature. There is no ownership model, so every pointer you pass is an assertion about lifetime that nothing checks. There is no bounds checking on arrays, so an out-of-range index is a silent read or write of whatever happens to be adjacent. And there is no type system that tracks what is in a buffer, so the compiler cannot tell a length from a pointer. Then the two things this guide does, and both are deliberate. It teaches the language as the standard defines it, - the current standard, not the historical dialect - so that what you learn is what a modern compiler accepts and what a code reviewer expects. And it spends most of its length on the four areas where beginners actually lose programs: memory, the preprocessor, the boundary with the operating system, and the set of rules that distinguish C that ships from C that merely compiles.

/* The eight keywords that are control flow, and nothing else.
   Every other construct in a C library - a list, a map, a string,
   a smart pointer, a thread - is built out of these plus a struct
   and a pointer. That is what "C is small" actually means. */

enum kind {
  KIND_UNSET   = 0,
  KIND_COUNT   = 1,   /* unsigned, no sign bit: 0 .. 2^64-1        */
  KIND_SIGNED  = 2,   /* int, two's complement: -2^63 .. 2^63-1   */
  KIND_REAL    = 3,   /* IEEE-754 binary64 by default, NOT decimal */
  KIND_POINTER = 4,   /* an address. Not an integer, despite what
                         the implicit conversions let you do.      */
  KIND_ARRAY   = 5,   /* an address plus a length known only to
                         the type that holds it. Never passed bare. */
  KIND_STRUCT  = 6,   /* the only abstraction C has. No behaviour,
                         no invariants, no constructor - just a
                         layout the compiler will reason about.      */
  KIND_VOID    = 7,   /* "no value" for a return; "unspecified" for
                         a parameter. Not a thing you can hold.     */
};

/* A struct is the whole of C's abstraction. Compare its cost with
   an equivalent object model: no vtable, no hidden base, no
   destructor, no allocation unless you ask for one. */

struct point {
  double x;
  double y;
};

/* sizeof is a compile-time constant, and it is the number that decides
   whether a struct is worth passing by value. 16 bytes here: two
   doubles, no padding, and nothing the compiler had to invent. */
static_assert(sizeof(struct point) == 16, "layout changed");

/* What the language does NOT give you, stated as what it does give
   you instead, which is the only useful way to learn it:
     no exceptions        -> return values, and setjmp/longjmp
     no generics          -> void* plus a cast, or _Generic (C11)
     no operator overloading -> macros, or a function
     no namespaces        -> prefixed identifiers
     no hidden copy       -> memcpy, which you write
     no bounds check      -> a length you thread by hand
     no RAII              -> free(), and discipline about where
*/
/* The cost, stated as code: a leak that ASan finds and the same
   program built without it. This is the difference between a bug and
   a crash. */
#include <stdlib.h>

static char *make_leak(void)
{
    char *p = malloc(64);
    p[0] = 'x';
    return NULL;          /* forgot to free(p) */
}

int main(void)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
        make_leak();
    }
    return 0;
}
/* $ cc -fsanitize=address -g leak.c -o leak && ./leak
   ==12345==ERROR: LeakSanitizer: detected memory leaks
   Direct leak of 64 byte(s) in 1 object(s) allocated from:
     0x55d3 in make_leak leak.c:5
   SUMMARY: AddressSanitizer: 64000 byte(s) leaked in 1000 allocation(s).

   Without the sanitizer this program runs "fine" for 64000 bytes and
   then keeps doing it, forever, until the machine swaps. That is the
   whole argument for building with the sanitizer on: the bug is
   silent, and the tool is the only thing that makes it loud. */

0.2 The toolchain you will actually use

A C toolchain is six programs and a file format, and knowing which program does what turns "my code does not compile" from a mystery into a question with an answer.

Six programs, and what each one actually decides

The pipeline has more stages than most people expect, and each stage makes a decision that the next one cannot revisit. The preprocessor turns text into text: it includes files, expands macros, and evaluates conditional compilation. It knows nothing about types, which is why a macro can produce something that does not compile. The compiler parses, type-checks and emits assembly, and it makes the two decisions that dominate performance: how to lay out your structures in memory, and when a variable can live in a register. The assembler turns assembly into machine code and resolves the symbols that refer to storage or to code. The linker combines object files, resolves the remaining symbols, and applies two policies that decide whether your program starts at all: where things are placed in memory, and which symbols are visible outside your translation unit. The archiver packages object files into a library for reuse. And the build system decides, from the source, which of those five run, with which flags, and in what order — which is where most "works on my machine" problems live. Then the three flags that change what you get, and each is a different optimisation class. The warning level, and the highest practical level is worth enabling permanently because it finds real defects; the two that matter most are the one for implicit conversions between signed and unsigned and the one for a comparison between a pointer and an integer, both of which are almost always a bug. The optimisation level, where the default is appropriate for development and inappropriate for measurement — a build that has been optimised can inline away a variable, which changes the timing of everything around it. And the standard level, which is the flag that decides whether a construct compiles at all; it should be pinned in the build file rather than chosen by whoever is compiling. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Compiling with the compiler as the linker. Some drivers accept both compile and link in one step, and a build that relies on that loses the ability to link two separately-compiled files — so the output of a build is a linked binary, not an object file that a driver happened to finish. Mixing objects built by different compilers or standard levels, which produces link errors whose messages name the linker rather than the cause. And not pinning the standard level in the build file, which means the code compiles for whoever is building it and not for the project — and the first person to notice is a user on an older toolchain. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Turn the warning level up and treat each new warning as a defect, because the warnings a compiler emits are almost all real bugs and the ones that are not are still worth knowing about. And build with a warning list in version control, so that a new warning is a build failure rather than a line in a log — that is what stops a codebase's warning count from growing quietly over a year.

/* The pipeline, as it actually runs. The compile step does NOT
   resolve anything that lives in another translation unit, which
   is why "undefined reference" is a LINKER message and not a
   compiler message - and why a declaration mismatch across two
   files compiles cleanly and fails at link time or, worse, not. */

typedef struct point_s {
  double x;
  double y;
} point_t;

/* Two functions, one declaration. The declaration is a promise
   about a definition in another file; the compiler believes the
   promise and does not check it. A mismatch is undefined
   behaviour, not a diagnostic. */

double  dist(const point_t *a, const point_t *b);   /* a.c   */
double  area(const point_t *a, const point_t *b);   /* b.c   */

/* Compiling a.c produces an object file with dist() UNDEFINED.
   Compiling b.c produces one with dist() referenced. The linker
   is what pairs them - so a.c alone links into an executable only
   if something else defines dist(). */

/* What a compiler flag actually changes:

   -std=c11          the LANGUAGE. VLA, _Generic, anonymous
                     structs and _Atomic appear or vanish.
   -Wall -Wextra     what is REPORTED. Not behaviour.
   -O0 / -O2         what is GENERATED. At -O0 every variable is
                     a real memory location; at -O2 it may not
                     exist at all. Never measure at -O0.
   -fPIC             position-independent code: every absolute
                     address becomes a load from the global offset
                     table. Required for anything in a shared
                     library. Not "portable", despite the name.
   -DDEBUG           a name the preprocessor can test with #ifdef.
                     This is how debug/release differ, and it is a
                     feature, not a hack. */

/* A function that is safe to call with either order of arguments,
   which is worth noticing because none of the operations require it:
   no temporary, no allocation, and the compiler can emit the same
   instructions regardless of which argument is which. */
static double dist_fast(const point_t *a, const point_t *b)
{
  double dx = a->x - b->x;
  double dy = a->y - b->y;
  return dx * dx + dy * dy;
}

/* With real maths you would call hypot() rather than multiplying,
   because the multiplication form overflows for large coordinates
   and for small ones underflows to zero. Written this way to make
   the point: the fast form and the correct form differ, and only
   one of them is right at the ends of the range. */
/* A real Makefile fragment: the three targets that actually matter
   and the one flag combination worth never shipping. */
CC      = cc
CFLAGS  = -std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic \
          -Wshadow -Wconversion -Wsign-conversion \
          -Wstrict-prototypes -Wmissing-prototypes \
          -Wvla -Wcast-qual -Wpointer-arith

all: app

# -O2 is what ships. -g costs nothing in the release build and is
# what makes a production crash symbolicate.
app: app.o
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -O2 -g -o $@ $^

app.o: app.c app.h
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -O2 -c -o $@ $<

# A separate sanitizer build, not a flag on the release build: the
# instrumentation changes timing and code layout, so a sanitizer run
# is not evidence that the shipped binary is correct.
.PHONY: sanitize
sanitize:
	$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -g -fsanitize=address,undefined -o app-san app.c
	./app-san

.PHONY: clean
clean:
	rm -f app app.o app-san

/* Why -Wconversion and not just -Wall: implicit narrowing is the
   mechanism behind a whole class of truncation bugs, and GCC and
   Clang both hide it by default. Enable it and it finds the two
   hundred warnings in a typical codebase on day one. It is noisy,
   and that is the point — each warning is a place where a value
   changed magnitude without anyone deciding it should. */

0.3 What to know before you start

You need almost nothing from other languages to read C well, and two things that are easy to skip are the ones that matter.

What you need, and what you can skip

The prerequisites for C are unusually low, which is one of its virtues and one of its traps: it lets you write a program that compiles in an afternoon and is wrong in a way that takes a week to find. You need binary arithmetic. Not mathematics — arithmetic. That means knowing what two's complement is, what a shift does to a sign bit, why multiplying by a power of two is not the same as shifting for negative numbers, and what happens when a signed value overflows. Every one of those is a mechanism behind a class of C bug, and none of them is visible in the source. You need to know what a pointer is. Not as a metaphor, but concretely: that it holds an address, that the address has a width, that dereferencing is a memory access the compiler will assume is valid, and that nothing in the language connects an address to the size of the thing at it. You do not need to know object-oriented programming, and reading C as if you did is the single most common obstacle — there are no classes, no inheritance, and no virtual dispatch, and every pattern you know that relies on one of those has a different, usually more explicit, spelling here. You do not need to know the standard library yet, beyond knowing that headers declare things and that you must include the right one. Then the three things worth knowing before the first chapter, and each is a habit that will be worth more than any single piece of syntax. Compile with warnings on from the first line you write, because the compiler's opinion of your code is more informed than yours on day one and less informed than yours by month three — which means you want to hear it now. Read the declaration before you read the body, since in C the declaration tells you what a thing is and how it is meant to be used, and the body tells you only what it does. And know your integer sizes before you use them, because the standard guarantees a minimum width for each type and almost nothing else — int can be sixteen bits on one platform and thirty-two on another, and code that assumes thirty-two is code that works on your machine. Then the two things that catch people out, and each is worth stating before the first chapter rather than after the tenth. Reading a declaration from the inside out. The rule is to start at the identifier and move outward: char *const p[] is an array of pointers to constant characters, and reading it left to right produces a completely different and equally valid-looking answer. And believing that a declaration is documentation. A declaration tells the compiler the shape; it says nothing about units, ownership, lifetime or whether a pointer may be null, and in C those four are the actual content of a function's interface. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Keep a scratch program open — one file you compile to check what a construct does, because the preprocessor and the compiler will answer questions faster and more reliably than reasoning will. And turn on every diagnostic your compiler offers, including the optional ones, because the optional warnings are where the pointer and integer categories live and those are the ones that cost a day.

/* Reading a declaration from the inside out. The identifier is the
   fixed point; everything to its LEFT binds tighter, everything to
   its RIGHT binds looser. This is the whole rule. */

/*   char *const p[]      */
   char *const p[];      /* p is an array (on the right)            */
                         /* of pointers (the * to the left of p)    */
                         /* to const char (the const, left of p)    */
                         /* -> array of (pointer to const char)     */

/* The same shape, three ways, and they are different declarations. */
const char *q1;   /* q1 points to a const char. q1 itself is mutable. */
char * const q2;  /* q2 is a const pointer to a mutable char.         */
const char *const q3; /* both. You can only read through it.          */

/* A function declaration, read the same way. */
void fill(char *dst, unsigned long n, int value);
/* fill is a function. Its return type is void.
   Parameters, in order:
     dst      -> char *          a mutable buffer, by pointer
     n        -> unsigned long    a COUNT, unsigned, no sign bit
     value    -> int              any int, converted on entry
   Returns void, so there is nothing to ignore on purpose. */

/* Why "n" is unsigned long and not int: a count cannot be
   negative, and an unsigned type documents that. It also removes
   a whole class of bug: code that computes n - 1 on a signed int
   and compares the result against zero is one refactor away from
   an out-of-bounds access on n == 0. */

/* The four questions a declaration does NOT answer:
     1. units        - is n bytes, elements, or bytes after value?
     2. ownership    - does the caller keep dst after the return?
     3. lifetime     - may dst alias value? may it be NULL? may it be
                       the same buffer as one passed to another call?
     4. failure      - what does this do on a short buffer, a NULL
                       pointer, or a n of zero?
   In C those four ARE the interface. Everything else is a
   convention that a comment might get wrong and cannot enforce. */
/* The four things to know before chapter 1, in code you will
   recognise on your first day. */

/* 1. A pointer is an address. Arithmetic on it is byte arithmetic. */
int a[5] = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
int *p = a;
p += 2;
printf("%d\n", *p);            /* 2 */
printf("%ld\n", (long)(p - a)); /* 2: the distance in ELEMENTS */

/* 2. An array is not a pointer, though it decays to one. */
printf("%zu\n", sizeof a);      /* 20: 5 x 4, the compiler knows */
printf("%zu\n", sizeof p);      /*  8: it is a pointer */

/* 3. Ownership is a convention the compiler does not check. */
char *leak(void) { char *p = malloc(64); p[0]=1; return NULL; }

/* 4. Undefined behaviour is not "unspecified". It is a licence. */
int x = 1;
if (x == 1) { }
printf("%d\n", x / 0);         /* undefined: the optimiser may
                                    assume x != 1 and delete the
                                    branch entirely, and the
                                    printed number may be anything */

0.4 How this guide is organised

The guide moves from the language to the machine, then to the interfaces C sits on, and it is ordered so that each chapter's assumptions are the previous chapter's conclusions.

Why the order is what it is

Most C material teaches syntax first and system programming second, and the result is a reader who can write a loop but cannot write a function that takes a pointer safely. This guide inverts that, deliberately, and the ordering argument is short enough to state. Declarations and types come before everything, because a declaration is how C states an interface and an interface is the only thing you actually write. Pointers and memory come next, because every later chapter assumes you can reason about an address and a lifetime. Structures, arrays and strings come after pointers, because each of them is a pointer plus a rule about the rule. Functions, linkage and the preprocessor come after the data model, because a function signature is a data structure and translation-unit visibility is a naming problem. The standard library chapter comes after all of that, because a library function is only understandable once you know what it does with a pointer. Then the three parts, and each has a character. The language half — types, memory, data structures, functions, the preprocessor, the library — is the part that makes C C. The systems half — files, processes, signals, concurrency, the boundary with the operating system — is the part C was designed for and the part that is most useful in practice. And the craft half — testing, debugging, building, performance, portability, security — is the part that decides whether the first two halves are usable. Then the three things every chapter has, and knowing the shape makes the guide faster to read. A worked example, in the sense of code that compiles and runs rather than fragments chosen to illustrate a syntax point. A list of the things that go wrong, with the mechanism for each, because a C bug is almost never a mystery — it is one of a short set of mechanisms, and naming the mechanism is what makes the fix obvious. And a list of practices, which are the habits that separate C that ships from C that compiles. Then the two things about the code in this guide, and both are stated so you do not copy the wrong things. Security decisions are real, — every buffer operation, every formatting call and every ownership transfer in a sample is written the way it should be written in code that ships, because an example that omits a length check teaches a pattern that produces a vulnerability. And the examples are compact rather than complete, so the shape is visible; where a sample elides something, the surrounding text says what is elided. Then the two practices, and both are about reading rather than writing. Read the "goes wrong" list of every chapter before the chapter itself, because it is the list of the errors you are about to make, and recognising one as it happens is worth more than understanding it afterwards. And compile every sample you read, because in C a sample that you have compiled is a fact and a sample you have read is a claim — and the difference shows up in the first twenty lines of anything involving pointers.

/* The shape of a chapter in this guide, and why each part is there.

   Chapter N:
     1. WHAT IT IS        - the model, and why it is shaped that way.
                             Read this even if you skip the rest.
     2. THE MECHANISM      - what the platform or the compiler actually
                             does, in enough detail that the next
                             section's consequences follow from it.
     3. THE CONSEQUENCES   - the rules that follow, and the rule that
                             follows from each rule.
     4. WHAT GOES WRONG    - the specific defects, each with the
                             mechanism that produces it, because an
                             unmechanised bad-advice list is folklore.
     5. PRACTICES          - the two or three habits that make the
                             difference, stated as habits rather
                             than as rules so they survive contact
                             with a deadline.

   The order matters in one specific way: section 4 is derivable from
   section 2. Every entry in "what goes wrong" traces back to a
   specific fact about the platform, and that trace is the part of
   C knowledge that transfers to a language you have not met yet.
   A list of gotchas does not transfer. */

/* One worked shape, showing how the five parts interlock for the
   subject that matters most in C. This is the skeleton of chapters
   3 through 5, compressed:

   WHAT IT IS          a pointer is an address.
   THE MECHANISM       an address is a number. Dereferencing one is
                       a load at that address. Nothing checks that
                       the address is readable.
   CONSEQUENCES         therefore a pointer is valid only for the
                       region it points into; therefore a pointer
                       outliving that region is a defect the
                       language cannot detect; therefore ownership
                       has to be in the interface.
   WHAT GOES WRONG      reading through a freed pointer (use after
                       free), reading through a NULL (a crash you can
                       debug), and reading past the end of an array
                       (a read that succeeds and returns rubbish -
                       the worst of the three, because nothing
                       reports it).
   PRACTICES           one rule of ownership per pointer; a length
                       alongside every pointer that points to more
                       than one thing; and a single place that frees,
                       so there is one place to audit. */
/* Character sets: a byte is not a character, and "the eight-bit
   accident" is why. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <locale.h>

int main(void)
{
    /* 1. char is signed on x86 and unsigned on ARM. The SAME binary
       gives different results on both. */
    char c = 0xFF;
    printf("%d\n", (int)c);        /* -1 on x86, 255 on ARM */

    /* The portable way to test for a byte value, and the one the
       character functions all use internally. */
    printf("%d\n", c == (char)0xFF);     /* WRONG: c promoted to
                                               int before compare */
    printf("%d\n", (unsigned char)c == 0xFFu);  /* RIGHT */

    /* 2. char is ONE BYTE. How many bytes that is, is not
       guaranteed - it is 8 on every platform anyone deploys to,
       and the standard permits 16. */
    printf("%zu %d\n", sizeof(char), CHAR_BIT);

    /* 3. strcspn and friends are not byte functions. They are
       character functions, and in the "C" locale a byte IS a
       character. Switch to UTF-8 and a multibyte sequence is one
       character, which is exactly what breaks a length assumption. */
    const char *s = "h\xc3\xa9llo";     /* h, U+00E9, l, l, o */
    printf("%zu\n", strlen(s));          /* 6 BYTES             */
    setlocale(LC_ALL, "C.UTF-8");
    printf("%zu\n", mbstowcs(NULL, s, 0) + 1); /* 6: 5 chars + NUL */
    /* ...which is why a UTF-8 string sliced by BYTE index can split
       a character and produce a replacement glyph, and why
       strlen is the wrong tool for any non-ASCII text. */
    return 0;
}
/* For fixed-width text handling, work in bytes and be explicit that
   you are. For anything that renders to a user, convert at the
   boundary and work in wchar_t or a UTF-8-aware library above it. */

1 · Types, Declarations and the Machine

1.1 The object model in one page1.2 Integers, and the rules everyone gets wrong1.3 Floating point, and why the answer is sometimes wrong1.4 Declarations, storage classes and linkage1.5 Character sets, encodings and the eight-bit accident

1.1 The object model in one page

C's entire object model is four ideas: an object is a region of storage, an object has a type, a type has an alignment, and an expression reads or writes an object. Everything else in the language is notation over those four.

Four ideas, and everything else is a consequence

An object in C is a region of storage whose contents a program can interpret as a value. It is not a JavaScript object or a Python object — it is an address range, and the language's entire job is to decide how many bytes it occupies and what operations are legal on them. A type is a set of values plus the representation of those values plus a permitted set of operations, and the standard library is written as a collection of functions that accept and return types, which is why passing the wrong type is not a compile error in any interesting way. Alignment is a requirement, not an optimisation: a type may only be stored at an address that is a multiple of its alignment, and the compiler inserts whatever padding makes that true. And an expression names a value, which is either an lvalue with storage or an rvalue without. The lvalue/rvalue distinction is the mechanism behind the errors beginners meet first — assigning to a function call is not possible because a call is an rvalue, and modifying a function argument that was passed by value is not possible because the parameter is not an lvalue of the caller's object. Then the three things that follow, and each is a requirement rather than an observation. A structure's size is the sum of its members plus padding to the structure's own alignment, and the padding is not optional: it exists so that the structure as a whole satisfies the strictest alignment requirement among its members, which is what makes an array of structures walkable. An object's lifetime is one of four, — static, thread, automatic, or allocated — and each has a different rule about when it starts and stops being valid. And the same bits can be read through different types, which is legal and is called aliasing, and which has a specific and surprising set of rules that chapter 3 returns to. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Assuming the layout you expect, which works until the compiler inserts a different amount of padding for a different target — and which is why a structure written to a file and read back on a machine with a different alignment is a corrupt file rather than a crash. Passing a structure by value and modifying the copy, which is legal and which a reader will mistake for a bug, and which is expensive for any structure large enough to matter. And using an object after its lifetime ended, which is the entire class of use-after-free bugs and which the language cannot detect. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Print the sizes you depend on once, as static assertions,, so a layout change becomes a compile error on the platform where it matters rather than a mystery on somebody else's. And never assume a pointer is not null just because you assigned it, because a function's return value is not a special case and an unchecked return is the most common null dereference in C.

#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdio.h>

/* An object: a region of storage whose contents a program can read
   as a value. Not a language-level "object" with behaviour - just
   bytes, a type, and an alignment. */

struct rgb { uint8_t r, g, b; };
struct rgba { uint8_t r, g, b, a; };
struct two_words { uint16_t lo, hi; };

/* Padding is not optional. Each of these is larger than the sum of
   its members, and the difference is the cost of the alignment rule
   rather than of anything the programmer asked for. */
static_assert(sizeof(struct rgb)  == 3, "3 bytes, alignment 1");
static_assert(sizeof(struct rgba) == 4, "4 bytes, alignment 1");
static_assert(sizeof(struct two_words) == 4, "2 x uint16, alignment 2");

/* The four lifetimes an object can have:
     static    - from before main to after exit. Zero-initialised.
     automatic - a block. From entry to exit. Uninitialised on entry,
                 which is the single most common source of garbage
                 values in C.
     allocated - from malloc to free. Explicit, and therefore
                 auditable, which is its whole appeal.
     thread    - one per thread, from thread start to thread end. */

/* An lvalue has storage; an rvalue does not. That is the entire
   reason assignment has the shape it has. */
void lvalue_demo(void)
{
    int  a = 1;
    int *p = &a;   /* &a is an lvalue; p now names an object */

    a  = 2;       /* fine: a is an lvalue            */
    *p = 3;        /* fine: *p is an lvalue           */
    a + 1 = 4;     /* error: a + 1 is an rvalue       */

    /* A function call is an rvalue even if it returns a pointer,
       which is why this needs a temporary:
         malloc(n)->next = NULL;      does not compile
         struct node *t = malloc(n); t->next = NULL;   does  */
}

/* sizeof on an expression does not evaluate it, which is what makes
   array-size idioms legal: */
static int last_index_of(int a[static 1]) { return (int)(sizeof a / sizeof a[0]) - 1; }

/* The exception that proves the rule: sizeof an ARRAY uses the array
   type and gives the element count. Once the array decays to a
   pointer - passed to a function, assigned to a pointer - that
   information is gone, and sizeof a gives you the pointer size.
   This asymmetry is the whole reason every C interface threads a
   length next to a buffer. */
void decay_demo(int a[16])
{
    extern int elsewhere(int a[16]);   /* declared the same way */
    (void)a;
    /* sizeof(a) here is sizeof(int*), which is 8 on LP64 and 4 on
       a 32-bit target. It is NOT 16 ints. */
}  /* sizeof a / sizeof a[0] would be 2 on LP64. */

int main(void)
{
    printf("%zu %zu %zu
",
           sizeof(struct rgb), sizeof(struct rgba), sizeof(struct two_words));
    return 0;
}
/* Typical output: 3 4 4 */
/* The object model, as the compiler lays it out. The gap between
   sizeof and _Alignof is where structure padding comes from. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdalign.h>

struct one_int   { int  i; };                 /* 4  bytes, align 4  */
struct two_int   { int  a, b; };             /* 8  bytes, align 4  */
struct int_char  { int  i; char c; };        /* 8  bytes: 3 pad    */
struct char_int  { char c; int  i; };        /* 8  bytes: 3 pad    */
struct reordered { char c; char pad; int i; };/* 5 -> padded to 8   */

struct outer {
    char  tag;      /* offset 0, 1 byte  */
    /* 3 bytes of padding follow, so that: */
    double value;   /* offset 8, 8 bytes */
};

_Static_assert(sizeof(struct one_int)  ==  4, "one int");
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct int_char) ==  8, "int then char pads");
_Static_assert(offsetof(struct outer, value) == 8, "the hole is real");

int main(void)
{
    printf("%zu\n", sizeof(struct int_char));   /* 8  */
    printf("%zu\n", alignof(struct int_char));  /* 4  */
    printf("%zu\n", offsetof(struct outer, value)); /* 8 */
    return 0;
}
/* Every one of those static assertions is a layout contract. Change
   the member order and the compiler stops the build instead of
   writing a file that a different tool reads at the wrong offsets —
   which is the specific mechanism behind "it works on my machine,
   the importer says the record is corrupt". */

1.2 Integers, and the rules everyone gets wrong

The standard guarantees minimum widths and almost nothing else, and every interesting integer bug in C comes from the gap between the guarantee and the platform.

The guarantees, and the gap

C's integer types are defined by minimum width, not by width. char is at least eight bits and is the only type whose width the language actually guarantees to the bit, because it is the unit that bytes are counted in and because CHAR_BIT is not always eight — on a DSP it can be sixteen or thirty-two, and code that assumes a byte is eight bits fails there. short is at least sixteen bits, int is at least sixteen, long is at least thirty-two, long long is at least sixty-four — and all of those minimums were set in 1989 and are now met with room to spare by every platform you will meet. What the standard does not guarantee is that they are all different from one another, which is the trap: on a 64-bit Unix platform, int, long and long long are all sixty-four bits, so a function taking a long can be called with an int with no diagnostic and no truncation. Then the three rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Signed integer overflow is undefined behaviour, not a wraparound — which means the compiler is entitled to assume it cannot happen and may optimise a loop into an infinite one, remove a bounds check it deduced was unnecessary, and produce a program that behaves correctly on your data and incorrectly on the data you were protecting against. Conversion to a narrower signed type is implementation-defined when the value does not fit, which means a cast is a statement to the compiler that you have checked. And the usual arithmetic conversions apply to both operands of a binary arithmetic operator, so an unsigned value silently wins: adding a size_t and an int produces an unsigned result, and comparing an int against an unsigned length turns a negative value into a huge one. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Comparing a signed value against an unsigned length with - one, which is the loop idiom from every other language, and which for int i = n - 1; i >= 0; --i never terminates when the value is unsigned — a hang that no debugger explains. Truncating an identifier by storing it in a narrower type, which produces collisions rather than errors: a 64-bit value stored in a 32-bit field silently maps many distinct values onto the same one. And mixing a size with a signed count, which is the same usual-conversion rule appearing as a plausible-looking comparison. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use fixed-width types from the standard header for anything whose width matters — the explicit-width family and the pointer-sized one — because they are named for the property you care about rather than for a historical accident. And never let a signed value meet an unsigned length unless you have converted on purpose, converting at the boundary where the value enters your function, so the mixed comparisons are the caller's mistake and are visible at the call.

#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdio.h>

/* The guarantees, and what they leave out.

   Minimum widths, set in 1989, met with room to spare:
     char    >= 8 bits   and the ONLY width fixed to the bit
     short   >= 16       int    >= 16
     long    >= 32       long long >= 64

   NOT guaranteed: that these differ. On 64-bit Unix,
   int == long == long long == 64 bits. A function taking a long
   accepts an int with no diagnostic and no truncation. */

static void widths(void)
{
    printf("int %zu long %zu ptr %zu
",
           sizeof(int), sizeof(long), sizeof(void *));
    /* LP64: 4 8 8     LLP64 (Windows): 4 4 8  <- differs!        */
    /* ILP32 (32-bit):  4 4 4                                     */
}

/* THE bug. The ordinary loop idiom, applied to an unsigned length. */
size_t count_back(const char *s)
{
    int i = (int)strlen(s) - 1;   /* ok while lengths fit in int  */
    for (; i >= 0; --i) { /* ... */ }
    return (size_t)(i + 1);
}

/* What actually happens: the usual arithmetic conversions. int is
   converted to size_t (unsigned), so i >= 0 becomes i >= 0 on an
   unsigned type, which is always true, and the loop runs until the
   unsigned subtraction wraps to SIZE_MAX and then 0, and the
   comparison is STILL true. The process appears to hang. */
size_t count_back_broken(const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    int i = n - 1;              /* n is size_t, so n - 1 is size_t  */
    for (; i >= 0; --i) {       /* i converted to size_t: >= 0 is   */
        /* always true. Hangs. The compiler may also delete this    */
        /* check entirely once it proves i is non-negative.         */
    }
    return 0;
}

/* Three fixes, in increasing order of preference. */

/* 1. Count down to a known-good stopping condition. */
size_t count_down(const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    for (size_t i = n; i-- > 0; ) {
        /* i is the index BEFORE decrement in the body             */
    }
    return n;
}

/* 2. Be explicit about the signed type you meant. */
int count_down_signed(const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    for (int i = (int)n; i > 0; --i) {   /* i > 0, not i >= 0    */
        /* index is (size_t)(i - 1)                                 */
    }
    return 0;
}

/* 3. Do not count down at all. This is what you should write. */
size_t count_forward(const char *s)
{
    size_t n = 0;
    while (s[n] != '') ++n;     /* no conversion, no wrap       */
    return n;
}

/* Signed overflow is undefined, not modular. This is a real example:
   the compiler is entitled to conclude i is non-negative, drop the
   check, and produce a hang or a wrong answer. */
int overflow_demo(int n)
{
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        total += 1000000;          /* UB as soon as total overflows */
    }
    return total;
}

/* Sizes, offsets and truncation. Fixed-width types for anything
   whose width is a property of your data, not of the platform. */
struct record {
    uint64_t id;      /* the identifier: 64 bits, everywhere       */
    uint32_t kind;    /* a small enum: 32 bits is plenty            */
    uint16_t flags;   /* bit flags                                   */
    int16_t  delta;   /* signed: deltas are negative               */
};

static_assert(sizeof(struct record) == 16, "8 + 4 + 2 + 2, no padding needed");
/* Storing a 64-bit id in a uint32_t does not error. It collides:
   two different ids become one row. That is the worst kind of bug -
   a successful operation with the wrong answer. */

/* For a size, the right-width type is the pointer-sized one, so it
   survives a 32-bit target without silently wrapping: */
static size_t bytes_for(size_t count, size_t width)
{
    if (width != 0 && count > SIZE_MAX / width) return 0;  /* the check
                                                              that
                                                              multiplication-overflow
                                                              habits do
                                                              not include */
    return count * width;
}  /* sizeof(void *) tells you the width at compile time:
     sizeof(size_t) == sizeof(void *) by definition on every hosted
     implementation, which is exactly the guarantee you want. */
/* Integer promotion and the signed/unsigned trap, which is the
   single most common source of a C loop that never terminates. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <limits.h>
#include <stddef.h>

int main(void)
{
    /* THE HANG. size_t is unsigned; i is signed int; both are 64-bit
       wide, so int is converted to size_t for the comparison and -1
       becomes SIZE_MAX. i decrements forever. */
    for (int i = (int)count - 1; i >= 0; i--) { /* ill-formed in spirit */
        printf("%d\n", i);
    }

    /* RIGHT: do not compare a signed index against size_t. */
    size_t n = count();
    for (size_t i = n; i-- > 0; ) {            /* the idiomatic form */
        printf("%zu\n", i);
    }

    /* OR: keep the index signed and bound it correctly. */
    for (ptrdiff_t i = (ptrdiff_t)n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%td\n", i);
    }

    /* Comparison semantics: a signed value converts to unsigned
       whenever the unsigned operand's rank is >= or wider. */
    int   s = -1;
    size_t u = 1;
    printf("%d\n", s > u);        /* 0: -1 became SIZE_MAX */
    printf("%d\n", s < u);        /* 0: and not less-than  */

    /* Limits, for reference rather than for arithmetic. */
    printf("%d %d %ld %lld\n", INT_MIN, INT_MAX, LONG_MIN, LLONG_MAX);
    return 0;
}

/* The rule behind all of it: never mix signed and unsigned in a
   comparison. If the signs must differ, convert the signed value to
   a wider SIGNED type first, or compare after both have been cast
   to a common type you chose deliberately. */

1.3 Floating point, and why the answer is sometimes wrong

Binary floating point cannot represent most decimal fractions, and every application that handles money, measurements or identifiers eventually discovers this the hard way.

Representation, and what it costs

A double holds a sign, an exponent and a mantissa — sixty-four bits arranged as one sign bit, eleven exponent bits and fifty-two explicitly-stored fraction bits, with an implied leading one. That structure means the set of representable values is sparse and non-uniform: near zero the gaps between representable values are tiny, and near the largest magnitude they are enormous. 0.1 is not representable, and neither is any decimal fraction whose denominator is not a power of two — which is every fraction whose denominator contains a factor of five. The stored value is the nearest representable number, and printing it produces the shortest string that round-trips, which is why 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3 is false in C as it is in every other language. Then the three rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Equality on a computed floating-point value is almost always the wrong test, because two mathematically equal quantities computed by different routes differ in the last bits, and the right test is a comparison against a tolerance scaled to the magnitude of the values involved. Summation accumulates error and the error depends on the order, so summing a million small values in ascending order is measurably worse than using a compensated summation, and the symptom is a total that is off by a fraction of a unit after enough terms. And money must not be floating point at all, because 0.1 + 0.2 not being 0.3 is not an edge case when the number has a legal meaning. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Using a floating-point value as a map key or a set member, which produces a lookup that fails for a value you can print identically, because the two values differ in bits below the precision you are printing. Comparing against zero after arithmetic, which is the right test for a difference that cannot cancel and the wrong one for a difference of two nearly-equal numbers, where the answer reflects the inputs' precision and not the computation's. And accumulating an index in a floating-point type, which loses exactness above two to the fifty-third and makes a loop that terminates on equality potentially never terminate. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Represent money as an integer count of the smallest unit and keep a scale, so that an amount of currency is an exact number with a documented precision, and so that the arithmetic is exact and the rounding is a deliberate, single, testable step. And compare with a relative tolerance derived from the magnitude, so a test that passes at one scale fails at another only if the failure is real — an absolute tolerance of a millionth is far too tight for values in the millions and far too loose for values near zero.

#include <math.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdio.h>

/* The structure of a double: 1 sign, 11 exponent, 52 stored
   fraction, with an implied leading 1. The consequence that matters:
   the representable values are sparse, and non-uniformly so. */

/* 0.1 has no exact representation because its denominator, ten,
   contains a factor of five, and the only denominators representable
   exactly are powers of two. */
static void why(void)
{
    printf("0.1  = %.20g
", 0.1);
    printf("0.1+ = %.20g
", 0.1 + 0.2);
    printf("eq? %d
", 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);      /* 0, and always 0   */

    /* The error is real and tiny: about 5.5e-17 relative. */
    printf("err = %.3e
", (0.1 + 0.2) - 0.3);
}

/* Comparison needs a tolerance SCALED TO THE MAGNITUDE. An absolute
   tolerance is wrong at one end of the range and at the other. */
static int nearly_equal(double a, double b)
{
    double scale = fabs(a) > fabs(b) ? fabs(a) : fabs(b);
    if (scale == 0.0) return a == b;
    return fabs(a - b) <= 1e-12 * scale;
}

/* NaN is not equal to itself, which breaks a surprising amount of
   code that uses != as "these are different". */
static int nan_demo(void)
{
    double x = 0.0, y = 0.0;
    double n = x / y;                 /* 0/0 is NaN, not an error   */
    return n == n;                    /* 0. isequal(n, n) is 1     */
}

/* Summation error grows with count and depends on order. Neumaier
   compensation costs one extra variable and removes most of it. */
static double sum_naive(const double *a, size_t n)
{
    double t = 0.0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) t += a[i];
    return t;
}

static double sum_compensated(const double *a, size_t n)
{
    double t = 0.0, c = 0.0;          /* c is the running error     */
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        double y = a[i] - c;
        double tt = t + y;
        c = (tt - t) - y;             /* what we lost this round     */
        t = tt;
    }
    return t;
}

/* MONEY IS NOT FLOATING POINT. An integer count of the smallest
   unit is exact; the scale is data, and the rounding is one
   deliberate step you can test. */
typedef struct { int64_t units; int scale; } money_t;   /* e.g. 12345, 2 */

static money_t money_add(money_t a, money_t b)
{
    /* Scale must match; promote the smaller scale by rescaling. */
    if (a.scale < b.scale) { int d = b.scale - a.scale;
                             for (int i = 0; i < d; ++i) a.units *= 10; }
    if (b.scale < a.scale) { int d = a.scale - b.scale;
                             for (int i = 0; i < d; ++i) b.units *= 10; }
    money_t r = { a.units + b.units, a.scale > b.scale ? a.scale : b.scale };
    return r;
}

/* Never accumulate an index in floating point. Above 2^53 an integer
   is no longer representable exactly, so i++ stops advancing for
   some values and a loop terminating on equality can spin forever. */
static void index_demo(void)
{
    for (double i = 0.0; i < 1e18; i += 1.0) { /* wrong */ }
    for (uint64_t i = 0; i < 1000000000000000000ULL; ++i) { /* right */ }
}  /* A float loop bound also fails the other way: i < 0.1 + 0.2
     is comparing against 0.30000000000000004, so a float accumulator
     can land one step short and the loop never ends. Use a count. */
/* The three ways floating point lies, and a comparison that is
   correct without any of them. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#include <float.h>

int main(void)
{
    /* 1. 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3. Not a bug: neither value is
       representable in binary, and the two approximations are
       different approximations. */
    printf("%.20f\n", 0.1 + 0.2);   /* 0.30000000000000004441 */

    /* 2. Accumulation. Each add rounds, and the error is bounded but
       does not cancel. 10 million additions of 0.1 loses ~0.001. */
    double sum = 0.0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 10000000; i++) sum += 0.1;
    printf("%.6f\n", sum);            /* 999999.999837 */

    /* 3. Comparisons. 0.1 + 0.2 > 0.3 is true, and it is true
       because both sides were rounded the same way in this
       particular case - which is not something you can rely on. */
    printf("%d\n", 0.1 + 0.2 > 0.3); /* 1 */

    /* CORRECT: compare within an epsilon scaled to the magnitude.
       An absolute epsilon is wrong for large values; a relative one
       is wrong for values near zero. Use both, or use a ULP. */
    int nearly_equal(double a, double b)
    {
        if (a == b) return 1;               /* handles infinities */
        double diff = fabs(a - b);
        double scale = fmax(fmax(fabs(a), fabs(b)), DBL_MIN);
        return diff <= 1e-12 * scale;
    }
    printf("%d %d\n",
        nearly_equal(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3),      /* 1: correct */
        nearly_equal(1.0, 1.0000001));      /* 0: correctly
                                                different        */

    /* For money, do not use double at all: 0.1 + 0.2 must be
       exactly 0.3, and an integer count of cents makes it so. */
    long cents_a = 10, cents_b = 20;
    printf("%ld\n", (cents_a + cents_b) / 100.0);
    return 0;
}
/* The rule: use double for measurement and for physics, use an
   integer count of the smallest unit for money, and never compare
   two floats with == unless you have proved they are the same
   value. */

1.4 Declarations, storage classes and linkage

A declaration in C states four things at once — type, name, storage duration and linkage — and two of them are invisible in most code you have read, which is why two files can disagree about a function without either one being wrong.

What a declaration actually says

When you write a declaration you are making four statements: what type the object has, what its name is, how long it exists, and whether any other part of the program can refer to that name. The last two are almost never written down explicitly and they are the source of an entire class of confusion. Storage duration is one of four: static — the whole execution, zero-initialised by default; automatic — a block, starting at entry and ending at exit, uninitialised; and allocated — from the allocation call to the matching free. Linkage is one of three: external, so the name is visible to other translation units and must be defined exactly once; internal, so the name is visible only in this file; and none, so the name is visible only in this declaration. Then the four rules that follow, and each is a requirement. A declaration without a definition is a promise, and the compiler does not check it: if two files declare the same function with different parameter types, both compile, and the failure is either a link error that names the symbol rather than the cause or — much worse — a silent call through a mismatched signature. A static function at file scope has internal linkage, which means the symbol is not visible to another file, and which is the correct default for every function you do not intend to export. A file-scope static variable has internal linkage AND static storage duration, so it is zero-initialised before main and persists across calls — a private global, and the right way to write a small cache. And a function's parameters have automatic storage, which means they are copies: a modification inside the function is invisible outside it, and a pointer parameter is a copy of the address, so the function can change what it points to but not where the caller's pointer lives. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A header declaring a function differently from its definition, which is the mismatch above, and which a compiler cannot detect because the two are in different translation units. A non-static helper with a generic name, which at link time collides with another file's function of the same name and produces a multiple-definition error naming two files and neither definition. And taking the address of a function declared with the wrong signature, which compiles and calls through a mismatched type. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Give every function internal linkage unless it is part of an interface, by marking it static, because the compiler will then warn about an unused one and the linker will never surprise you. And put every declaration in a header and every definition in exactly one .c file, so that a signature change is one edit rather than a hunt — and include your own header first in the .c file, which is what makes a missing declaration in a header a compile error rather than an implicit declaration.

/* A declaration makes FOUR statements at once:

     type            what the object is
     name            what it is called
     duration        how long it exists
     linkage         who else can see the name

   The last two are almost never written, which is why two files can
   disagree about a function without either being wrong. */

/* ---- storage duration ------------------------------------------- */

static int counter;        /* file scope + static = internal linkage,
                              static duration. Zero-initialised before
                              main, persists across calls. A private
                              global; the right way to write a cache. */

static void bump(void) { ++counter; }

static void duration_demo(void)
{
    int local;             /* AUTOMATIC: created on entry, destroyed on
                              exit, UNINITIALISED. Reading it first is
                              undefined behaviour - not "zero", not
                              "garbage", undefined. The single most
                              common source of a mystery value. */
    static int persisted;  /* STATIC duration, block scope: initialised
                              once, survives across calls. */
    local = 1;             /* fine: assigning initialises it        */
    /* printf("%d", local);  undefined: reading before writing  */
    ++persisted;
}

/* ---- linkage --------------------------------------------------- */

/* No static keyword: EXTERNAL linkage. This name is visible to every
   translation unit, so it must be defined exactly once in the whole
   program. Two files that both define it is a link-time error that
   names both files and neither definition. */
int shared_counter;

/* The static keyword here: INTERNAL linkage. Only this file names it.
   The compiler warns if it is never used, and the linker can never
   surprise you. This is the correct default for every function you
   do not intend to export. */
static int clamp(int v, int lo, int hi)
{
    return v < lo ? lo : (v > hi ? hi : v);
}

/* Parameters have AUTOMATIC storage. A modification inside the
   function is invisible outside it.

   The parameter is a COPY OF THE ADDRESS, so the callee can change
   what it points at, but cannot change where the caller's pointer
   lives. That distinction is the whole of C's by-reference story:

     f(&x)      f receives a copy of x's address. Writes through it
                are visible to the caller.
     f(x)       f receives a copy of x. Writes are not visible.
     f(p)       f receives a copy of p, which is an address. Writes
                through *p are visible. Assigning to p is not.  */

/* ---- the mismatch that no compiler catches --------------------- */

struct point { double x, y; };

/* a.c declares:  double norm(struct point *p);
   b.c defines:   double norm(const struct point *p);

   Both compile. The linker's symbol name on most platforms does not
   encode the parameter types, so it links. The call then passes a
   pointer through a mismatched declaration - undefined behaviour,
   and not always a crash, which is why it survives testing.

   The fix is not a flag. It is ONE HEADER, included by both, with
   the definition in exactly one .c file, and the .c file including
   its own header FIRST:

     #include "geom.h"      <- first line of a.c

   A mismatched definition then fails to compile, because the
   compiler sees the declaration and the definition together. */

/* The compiler flag that helps here is the one that warns about a
   call through a pointer to an undeclared function - some dialects
   make it an error, and where it is only a warning it still catches
   the commonest cause. Under the current standard an implicit
   declaration is not valid at all, so a missing header is a hard
   error rather than a silent int-returning call. */
/* Storage classes, linkage, and the two declarations that look
   identical and are not. */
#include <stdio.h>

int  g_initialised = 42;      /* storage: static. linkage: external */
static int g_internal = 7;    /* storage: static. linkage: INTERNAL
                                  - visible only in this file     */
extern int g_defined_elsewhere; /* storage: static. linkage: external.
                                  NO storage is reserved here      */

int counter(void)
{
    static int n = 0;   /* survives between calls. ONE instance,
                           not one per call. This is the whole
                           purpose of function-local static. */
    return ++n;
}

void shadowing(void)
{
    int x = 1;
    {
        int x = 2;      /* a NEW object. The outer x is untouched
                           and still alive, just not named here. */
        printf("%d\n", x);
    }
    printf("%d\n", x);  /* 1 */
}

int main(void)
{
    printf("%d %d %d\n", counter(), counter(), counter());
    /* 1 2 3 - UNSPECIFIED argument evaluation order in C, so this
       could print 3 2 1. Never rely on it.                     */
    printf("%d\n", g_initialised + g_internal);
    shadowing();
    return 0;
}

/* The linkage rules in one table:
     extern, no init     declaration only, storage elsewhere
     extern, with init   definition, external linkage
     (none), no init     tentative definition -> becomes 0
     static, no init     internal linkage, tentative
     auto                automatic duration, block scope
     register            automatic, but the ADDRESS may not be taken
     typedef             not a storage class: a new NAME for a type

   auto and register are historical. auto is now the default and
   means nothing; register is a promise about the address that the
   compiler may honour at the cost of UB if you break it, and which
   nobody needs. Do not write either. */

1.5 Character sets, encodings and the eight-bit accident

C's character model is one type, one signedness choice and no encoding at all, and that combination is why "text" is the single most dangerous category of data to pass through a C interface.

What the language actually says about characters

char is a small integer type. Its width is CHAR_BIT bits, which is eight on every platform you will meet and is not guaranteed. Its signedness is implementation-defined: on x86 it is signed, on ARM it is unsigned, and that single fact has broken the same string-handling code on both. The literals you write in the source are of type int, not char, and a byte in an array is char, so a comparison between a literal and a stored character is a comparison between two different types with two different signednesses — which is where the classic bug comes from. There is no encoding in the language. The source file's encoding, the encoding of a string read from a file, and the encoding a browser sends are three independent facts, and C compares the resulting bytes. Then the three rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Use unsigned char for anything that is bytes rather than text, so that a value above 127 is a positive number rather than a negative one and comparisons behave. Use the multi-byte and wide-character types when you mean characters rather than bytes, and be aware that a "wide character" is a fixed width in code units rather than a character, so a string length in wide characters is still not a character count for anything outside the basic multilingual plane. And when you take text from outside the program, establish and record its encoding, because the failure mode without that is mojibake that appears only for the users whose language is not the one the author tested with. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Testing c >= 0 to test for end-of-string, which works on a platform where char is unsigned and fails on a platform where it is signed, and which is the reason the comparison is written with an explicit cast. Passing a string length in characters when the API wants bytes, which truncates or overruns by the ratio of the encoding's code-unit width, and which is invisible until a non-ASCII name appears. And assuming one byte is one character, which is true for ASCII and for Latin-1 and false for everything else, and which produces a buffer that is sized correctly for the author's test data and too small for the first real user. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Keep bytes and text as different types all the way through your interface, so that a function that wants bytes cannot be handed text by accident, and so the boundary at which you decide what encoding you have is a single, greppable one. And choose one internal encoding and convert at the edges, — UTF-8 is the one to choose on any platform that has to interoperate — because a program with one internal encoding has exactly one set of bugs to look for and a program with two has the bugs of both plus the bugs of converting between them.

#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stddef.h>

/* C's character model in full:

   char        a small integer type, CHAR_BIT wide (8 everywhere
               you will meet, NOT guaranteed by the standard)
   signedness  IMPLEMENTATION-DEFINED: signed on x86, unsigned on
               ARM. The same code is correct on one and wrong on the
               other, and this is not hypothetical - it is one of the
               oldest portability bugs in the language.
   literals    'a' has type INT, not char. A stored byte is char.
               So c == 'a' compares two types with two different
               signednesses.
   encoding    NONE. The source encoding, a file's encoding and a
               browser's encoding are three independent facts, and
               the language compares the bytes they produce. */

static bool is_ascii_digit(char c) { return c >= '0' && c <= '9'; }

/* This is the version that is correct on both platforms, and the
   cast is not decoration - it is the whole fix. On a platform where
   char is signed, (unsigned char)c turns 0xFF from -1 into 255, so
   the comparison against EOF is a comparison of like with like. */
static bool is_eof_byte(int c)
{
    return c != EOF && c != (unsigned char)EOF;   /* EOF is int, -1 */
}

static size_t strlen_bytes(const char *s)
{
    size_t n = 0;
    while (s[n] != '') ++n;      /* '' is int 0, promoted; fine   */
    return n;
}

/* Byte length vs character length. Under UTF-8, strlen() returns
   BYTES. Every code point outside ASCII is two to four bytes, so a
   buffer sized with strlen() and filled by a caller that counted
   CHARACTERS is between one and four times too small.

   "naive" is 5 characters, 6 bytes. A function that asks for
   strlen(x)+1 and a caller that counts characters disagree, and the
   overflow is invisible until somebody types an accented name. */
static const char *sample = "naive";

/* UTF-8's structural property that makes counting possible at all:
   a byte with its top bit set is a continuation of the code point
   that started before it. So counting bytes that are NOT
   continuation bytes gives you code points. This is what lets a C
   program do length arithmetic without a full decoder - and it is
   also why you cannot index into a UTF-8 string by character. */
static size_t utf8_length(const char *s)
{
    size_t chars = 0;
    for (const unsigned char *p = (const unsigned char *)s; *p; ++p)
        if ((*p & 0xC0u) != 0x80u) ++chars;      /* not a continuation */
    return chars;
}

/* Why not to "just use wide characters": wchar_t is 16 bits on
   Windows and 32 on Unix, so a wchar_t string is 2 bytes per unit
   on one platform and 4 on the other, and it is STILL code units -
   a code point above the basic multilingual plane is two of them on
   a 32-bit wchar_t. So wcslen() is not a character count either, on
   either platform. */

/* The rule that avoids all of this, and it is one rule:
   keep BYTES and TEXT as different things all the way through.

     typedef struct { unsigned char *p; size_t n; } bytes_t;

   A function taking bytes_t cannot be handed a text pointer without
   a visible conversion, and the conversion is where the decision
   about what encoding you have gets made. A program with one
   internal encoding has one set of bugs; a program with two has
   the bugs of both plus the bugs of converting between them. */
/* A pointer VALUE is an address. Declaring one allocates storage for
   the ADDRESS, not for the thing it points at. That asymmetry is
   the source of most pointer confusion in C. */

static int  global = 42;      /* one object, whole program          */
static int *global_p = &global;/* a pointer initialised AT STARTUP,
                                  because static initialisers run
                                  before main. Not a local init.   */

void basics(void)
{
    int  value = 7;

    /* Three separate objects: the pointer itself, and the int. */
    int  *p    = &value;       /* points at value                    */
    int **pp   = &p;           /* points at p                        */

    /* The ONE thing to internalise: dereferencing is only about
       how many levels you name, and it costs nothing at run time
       on any compiler that can see what it is doing. */
    int x = *pp;               /* pp -> p -> value -> 7              */
    int y = **pp;              /* two levels, also 7                 */

    /* Pointer arithmetic is integer arithmetic, scaled. This is
       the ONLY reason p + 1 works at all: */
    char *c = (char *)&value;  /* legal: an object may be inspected
                                  through a character type - the
                                  aliasing exception.               */
    c += 1;                    /* one byte forward                   */
    /* (char *)&value + 1 != (int *)&value + 1
       The first advances one address; the second advances
       sizeof(int) addresses. Same syntax, four times the move.
       This is also why a char * and an int * to the same object
       are NOT interchangeable, and why subtracting two char *
       values gives a byte count while subtracting two int * does
       not. */
}

/* Arrays decay. This is the fact behind the most-reported C bug. */

static int *one_a = 0;              /* fine: an int has no address  */
static int  one[16];
static int *two_a = one;             /* fine: address of one[0]      */
static int  one[16] = { 0 };         /* fine: all elements zero      */
static int *bad  = &one;             /* ERROR: not an int *          */
static int (*pbad)[16] = &one;       /* fine, but almost never what
                                         you want to write          */

static size_t count(int a[], size_t cap)
{
    /* a is an int *, so sizeof a is 8 on LP64 - the size of a
       pointer - and 16 would be the size of the array only if the
       compiler knew, which it does not: at the call it is already
       a pointer. */
    return sizeof a / sizeof a[0];  /* 8/4 == 2 on LP64. WRONG.     */
    (void)cap;
}

/* The fix is to pass the length, and the better fix is to make it
   impossible to pass one without the other: */
typedef struct { int *p; size_t n; } int_span;

static size_t span_count(int_span s) { return s.n; }

/* One past the end is a VALID address to form and compare. It is
   not valid to dereference. One past the end of one array is not
   even the start of another. */
void one_past(void)
{
    int a[4] = { 1, 2, 3, 4 };
    int *end  = a + 4;               /* legal                      */
    /* *end = 5;   undefined behaviour                               */
    if (end != a + 4) { /* ... */ }  /* legal and well-defined     */
}

/* Null is not a small address. A null pointer has no object, so
   dereferencing it is undefined behaviour - which is why a check
   is a comparison and never arithmetic. */
int *null_like = 0;
void null_checks(int *p)
{
    if (p != NULL) { /* ... */ }
    /* if (p) is equivalent and reads better. What is NOT equivalent:
         if (p > NULL)      - comparing two unrelated pointers
         if (p - NULL > 0)  - subtracting a null pointer          */ */

/* Prefer one function that changes what a pointer points at.
   Prefer two functions when you also need the pointer itself to
   change, and document which one you have. */
static void  load_value(int *dst, int src) { *dst = src; }   /* writes through */
static void  load_pvalue(int *dst, int src) { dst = &src; } /* REBINDS only -
                                                               src is about to
                                                               die              */

2 · Pointers and Memory

2.1 A pointer is an address, and nothing else2.2 Lifetime, ownership and the rules that prevent leaks2.3 Allocation, alignment and the bugs the allocator cannot see2.4 Aliasing, strict aliasing, and the optimisation that ate your code

2.1 A pointer is an address, and nothing else

Everything C is famous for mis-handling follows from one fact: a pointer is a number that happens to be used to reach memory, and the language has no way to check that the memory it names is yours.

What a pointer is, and what it is not

A pointer value is an address. Declaring one allocates storage for the address; it does not allocate the thing pointed to. The address is just an integer of a fixed width, which is why pointer arithmetic is really integer arithmetic scaled by the size of the pointed-to type, and why casting a pointer to an integer and back is legal when the integer is wide enough. A pointer is not an object in itself, — the pointed-to thing is the object — and this distinction is the reason a pointer can be null, invalid, or dangling while the pointer itself is perfectly well defined. And a pointer to a pointer is how you express indirection without allocating anything, which is the difference between modifying what a caller's object holds and modifying where a caller's pointer points. Then the four rules that follow, and each is a requirement. An array decays to a pointer to its first element in almost every context, which is why a function taking an array is really taking a pointer and therefore why the length must be a separate parameter. Pointer arithmetic is defined only within one array, — one past the end is permitted to be formed and compared but not dereferenced, and anything beyond that is undefined behaviour even on a flat address space. A null pointer is not a pointer to zero, and dereferencing it is undefined behaviour, which is why the check must be an explicit comparison rather than an arithmetic trick. And adding an integer to a pointer scales by the size of the pointed-to type, so p + 1 on a char * advances one byte and on an int * advances four — a fact that makes pointer arithmetic readable and also makes a wrong type silently wrong by a factor. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Declaring a pointer without initialising it, which leaves it holding whatever was on the stack, and which the compiler will warn about only if you enable the warning — and which is the source of "it printed an address that looks almost right". Passing an array to a function that expects a pointer and using sizeof on it, which yields the pointer size rather than the array size, and which is the single most common C bug report there is. And comparing two pointers into different arrays, which is undefined behaviour even though it usually works, because the compiler may assume no relation between them and optimise accordingly. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Initialise every pointer at the point of declaration, even to null, because a declaration with an initialiser is also documentation and because it removes a whole class of "uninitialised" reads from the compiler's warning list. And put every buffer's length in the same declaration as the pointer, in a small structure, so that a pointer and its length travel together and there is no signature in which one can be passed without the other.

/* Pointer arithmetic, one past the end, and comparing pointers
   into different arrays. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stddef.h>

static int  global_array[5] = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};

int main(void)
{
    int a[5] = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};
    int *p = a;

    /* Arithmetic is in ELEMENTS, not bytes. */
    printf("%ld\n", (long)(p + 1 - a));   /* 1, not 4 */
    printf("%d\n", *(a + 3));             /* 40 - same as a[3] */

    /* ONE PAST THE END is a valid address to FORM and COMPARE. */
    int *end = a + 5;
    printf("%d\n", end > a);              /* 1: legal */
    printf("%ld\n", (long)(end - a));     /* 5: legal */
    /* printf("%d", *end);                 UNDEFINED: not
                                            dereferenceable. One
                                            past is not an
                                            element.            */

    /* One past ONE array is not the start of the NEXT. These are
       different objects and comparing them is undefined behaviour
       even though it prints 1 on every flat address space. */
    int b[5];
    printf("%d\n", a + 5 == b);           /* UB, usually prints 1 */

    /* The reason pointer subtraction is legal at all: the difference
       is only meaningful within one array. */
    printf("%ld\n", (long)(&global_array[3] - &global_array[1]));  /* 2 */

    /* An array is not a pointer: sizeof sees the whole thing, and
       &a is a pointer to the ARRAY, a different type from a. */
    printf("%zu %zu %zu\n", sizeof a, sizeof p, sizeof &a);  /* 20 8 8 */
    /* ...and that is why &a[0] + 1 walks elements while &a + 1
       walks 20 bytes. Both are legal and they are not the same. */
    printf("%ld\n", (long)((char *)(&a + 1) - (char *)&a));  /* 20 */
    return 0;
}
/* The rules to keep: arithmetic and subtraction are defined only
   within one array; one past the end may be formed, compared and
   decremented but never dereferenced; and a pointer to an array and
   a pointer to its first element have the same ADDRESS but not the
   same TYPE, which is why the pointer arithmetic on them differs. */
/* The portable macro that makes a pointer+length pair impossible to
   split, and the compiler diagnostics that say so. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>

typedef struct { const void *data; size_t len; } view;

/* Pass ONE argument, so the pair cannot be separated at a call site.
   This is the highest-value change you can make to an interface in C
   and it costs no runtime. */
static void log_view(const char *label, view v)
{
    printf("%s: %zu bytes\n", label, v.len);
}

/* A function that takes the pointer and the length separately can be
   called with a length that does not describe the buffer. Nothing
   checks it. GCC and Clang both warn when the literal lengths
   disagree, which is the only reason this bug is not more common. */
static void copy_bad(char *dst, size_t dst_cap, const char *src, size_t src_len)
{
    if (src_len > dst_cap) src_len = dst_cap;   /* silent truncation */
    for (size_t i = 0; i < src_len; i++) dst[i] = src[i];
}

/* The three pointer types, and what const buys in each position. */
static void takes_const_ptr(const char *s)   { /* cannot write *s   */ }
static void takes_ptr(char *s)               { /* may write *s      */ }
static void takes_const_ptr_ptr(const char **s) { /* cannot rebind    */ }
static void takes_ptr_ptr(char **s)          { /* may rebind *s     */ }

int main(void)
{
    char buf[16] = "hello";
    view v = { buf, 5 };
    log_view("greeting", v);

    const char *cs = "immutable";
    char       *ms = buf;
    takes_const_ptr(cs);      /* fine */
    takes_ptr(ms);            /* fine */

    /* NULL is not a pointer to address zero, and the difference is
       only visible through undefined behaviour. */
    char *p = NULL;
    if (p == NULL) printf("null\n");
    /* if (p > NULL)    comparison of unrelated pointers: UB */

    /* offsetof: the portable way to ask about layout. */
    struct s { char a; double b; };
    printf("%zu\n", offsetof(struct s, b));   /* 8, not 1 */
    return 0;
}
/* The rule: a pointer is an address and nothing else. It carries no
   length, no validity guarantee, and no association with anything.
   The two defensive habits that follow are to pass pointer+length as
