       array type, so the assignment copies a pointer to the first
       element rather than the elements themselves. */
/* The three parameter decisions that cost the most when wrong: the
   array that is not a pointer, the large struct, and the
   length-parameter trap. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <stdint.h>

/* WRONG. a is a pointer, so sizeof a is the pointer size and this
   returns 2 on LP64 instead of the real count. */
static size_t count_bad(int a[]) { return sizeof a / sizeof a[0]; }

/* RIGHT. The length is a separate parameter, so it can be wrong but
   at least it is visible. */
static size_t count_right(int a[], size_t n) { return n; }

/* BETTER. One argument, so the length cannot be separated from the
   buffer. This is the fix, and it costs nothing at run time. */
typedef struct { int *p; size_t n; } int_span;
static size_t count_span(int_span s) { return s.n; }
static int_span span_of(int *p, size_t n) { return (int_span){ p, n }; }

/* A LARGE STRUCT passed by value is copied per call, invisibly from
   the call site. Pass a pointer unless the struct is genuinely tiny. */
struct big { int pad[64]; };          /* 256 bytes                */
static int by_value(struct big b)  { return b.pad[0]; }  /* +256 */
static int by_ptr(const struct big *b) { return b->pad[0]; } /* +8 */

/* The copy is real and the caller pays it: */
static int opaque_call(void)
{
    struct big b;
    b.pad[0] = 1;
    return by_value(b);       /* 256 bytes on the stack, every call */
}

/* THE VARARGS TRAP. After the last named parameter the count and
   types are NOT passed. Default argument promotions apply, so a
   float arrives as a double and reading it as a float is garbage. */
#include <stdarg.h>

static int sum_ints(int count, ...)
{
    va_list ap;
    va_start(ap, count);
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) total += va_arg(ap, int);
    va_end(ap);
    return total;
}

static double BROKEN_ave(int count, ...)
{
    va_list ap;
    va_start(ap, count);
    double total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) total += va_arg(ap, float);  /* WRONG */
    va_end(ap);
    return total;
}

static double right_ave(int count, ...)
{
    va_list ap;
    va_start(ap, count);
    double total = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) total += va_arg(ap, double); /* RIGHT */
    va_end(ap);
    return total;
}

int main(void)
{
    int a[16];
    printf("%zu %zu %zu\n", count_bad(a), count_right(a, 16),
           count_span(span_of(a, 16)));         /* 2 16 16 */
    printf("%d\n", sum_ints(3, 1, 2, 3));
    return 0;
}
/* Three decisions: pass pointer+length as one argument so they cannot
   be separated; pass a struct by pointer when it is larger than a
   couple of words; and never write a variadic function when a struct,
   a count-and-array pair, or a macro will do, because nothing checks
   the types at the call. */

4.3 The preprocessor is a text tool, not a language

The preprocessor runs before the compiler sees anything, knows nothing about types, and will happily produce text that does not compile — which is why almost every preprocessor surprise is a surprise about text.

What it does, and what it cannot know

The preprocessor includes files, expands macros and selects which text the compiler ever sees. It performs substitution on tokens, with no knowledge of scope, type or evaluation order, which means a macro is a text substitution that happens to look like a function call. Then the four things that follow, and each is a requirement. A macro argument is expanded before substitution and is not evaluated as an expression, so a macro that uses its argument twice evaluates the argument twice, and if the argument has a side effect the side effect happens twice. Parentheses around the whole expansion are mandatory, because the expansion is spliced into the surrounding expression at every use site, and an unparenthesised macro breaks on precedence rather than on its own. Macro arguments are separated by commas unless the argument is itself parenthesised, which is why a macro taking a braced initialiser needs the braces wrapped. And A macro is not a function: there is no type checking, no conversion, no overload, and the expansion is text. Then the four things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. An unparenthesised macro parameter, which breaks on operator precedence — the classic #define MAX(a,b) a > b ? a : b producing a wrong answer for any compound argument. A macro parameter used twice, which evaluates its argument twice, so a macro wrapping an incrementing pointer advances it twice. Recursive macro expansion producing surprising results, because the preprocessor's blue-paint rules prevent infinite recursion but still allow a nested expansion that a reader does not expect. And Conditional compilation scattering a feature flag through the code, which means the code that is compiled differs from the code you read, and a bug in the untaken branch is invisible. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Parenthesise every macro parameter and the whole expansion, without exception, — it costs nothing and it is the difference between a macro that works and one that is subtly wrong. And Prefer inline functions and constants over macros, because a function is type-checked, evaluated once, has an address, and can be stepped through in a debugger, and because an inline constant is a real symbol a debugger can print.

/* The preprocessor substitutes TEXT. It knows nothing about scope,
   type, precedence or evaluation order. Every surprise below is a
   surprise about text. */

/* BROKEN: no parentheses around the parameter, so
     MAX(1 + 1, 3)  expands to  1 + 1 > 3 ? 1 + 1 : 3
   which is  1 > (3 ? 1 : 3)  by precedence, and wrong. */
#define BAD_MAX(a, b) a > b ? a : b

/* RIGHT: every parameter parenthesised, and the whole expansion
   parenthesised so it cannot capture the surrounding expression. */
#define MAX(a, b) ((a) > (b) ? (a) : (b))

/* BROKEN: the argument is used twice, so it is EVALUATED TWICE.
   ADVANCE(&p) advances p by two. */
static int  advance(int *p) { return (*p)++; }
#define BAD_ADVANCE(p) ((p) += advance(&(p)), *(p) - 1)

/* Comma in a braced initialiser splits the macro argument, unless
   the braces are parenthesised - which is the one place a macro
   cannot be fixed by adding parentheses to the parameter. */
#define TWO_AT_ONCE(a, b) do { a; b; } while (0)
#define LIST_OF_TWO(x) do { two_things x; } while (0)   /* needs (x) */

/* A macro is NOT a function. No type checking, no conversion, no
   overload, no address, not steppable in a debugger. */
#define SQUARE(x) ((x) * (x))
static int  square(int x)  { return x * x; }

/* Prefer these, in this order, whenever you can:
     const int    for a named constant  - typed, debuggable, in the
                                            symbol table
     static inline for a small function - type checked, evaluated
                                            once, has an address
     enum          for a compile-time set of values                */

/* Conditional compilation is a feature gate and it has a cost: the
   code you read is not the code that is compiled, so a bug in the
   untaken branch is invisible to every test you run. Keep the
   condition out of the function bodies: put it around whole
   functions, or around whole files, so that the difference between
   the two builds is a list you can read. */
#if defined(_WIN32)
#  define PATH_SEP '\'
#elif defined(__linux__)
#  define PATH_SEP '/'
#else
#  error "unsupported platform"
#endif

/* The one macro worth writing yourself: static assertions. They
   turn a layout or type assumption into a compile error on the
   platform where the assumption is false, instead of a mystery on
   somebody else's. */
#include <assert.h>
_Static_assert(sizeof(int) == 4, "this code assumes a 32-bit int");
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct point) == 16, "layout changed");

/* A single-header file used to work around a missing declaration is
   the shape to be suspicious of: it is almost always a missing
   include, and the workaround hides the reason. */
/* The preprocessor's four surprises, all of them about text. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

/* 1. THE MACRO IS TEXT. A parameter used twice is evaluated twice. */
static int advance(int *p) { return (*p)++; }

#define BAD_ADVANCE(p)  ((p) += advance(&(p)), *(p) - 1)
/* BAD_ADVANCE(&i) expands to (&i) += advance(&(&i)), ... which does
   not compile, and that is the GOOD outcome. Where it does compile
   it advances twice. */

/* 2. UNPARENTHESISED PARAMETERS break on precedence, not on
   themselves. */
#define BAD_MAX(a, b)     a > b ? a : b
#define GOOD_MAX(a, b)    ((a) > (b) ? (a) : (b))

/* 3. A COMMA IN AN ARGUMENT splits it, unless the argument is
   parenthesised. This is the one place parentheses do not help. */
#define TWO_AT_ONCE(a, b)  do { a; b; } while (0)
struct point { int x, y; };
/* TWO_AT_ONCE(v.x = 1, v.y = 2)  ->  three arguments. Error. */
/* TWO_AT_ONCE((v.x = 1), (v.y = 2))  ->  two. Correct. */

/* 4. EXPANSION IS NOT EVALUATION. The token sequence is pasted. */
#define DOUBLE(x)   ((x) + (x))
#define DOUBLE2(x)  DOUBLE(DOUBLE(x))    /* works: no recursion    */
#define LOOP(x)     LOOP(x)             /* stops: the blue-paint
                                            rule marks LOOP as
                                            non-replaceable while
                                            expanding it          */

/* WHAT TO USE INSTEAD. All four are strictly better where they fit. */
static inline int good_max(int a, int b) { return a > b ? a : b; }
static inline size_t dbl(size_t x)       { return x + x; }

#define MAX(a, b) GOOD_MAX((a), (b))    /* still a macro, but it
                                            calls a function, so
                                            each argument is
                                            evaluated ONCE        */
#define ARRAY_LEN(a) (sizeof(a) / sizeof((a)[0]))   /* not a macro:
                                                         it is an
                                                         expression
                                                         with no
                                                         double
                                                         evaluation   */

/* CONDITIONAL COMPILATION is a feature gate and it has a cost: the
   code you read is not the code that compiles, so a bug in the
   untaken branch is invisible to every test you run. Keep the
   condition around whole functions or whole files. */
#if defined(__linux__) && defined(__x86_64__)
#  define CACHE_LINE 64
#elif defined(_WIN64)
#  define CACHE_LINE 128
#else
#  define CACHE_LINE 64
#endif

/* Static assertions turn a layout or type assumption into a compile
   error on the platform where it is false. This is the highest-value
   use of the preprocessor there is. */
_Static_assert(sizeof(void *) == 8, "assumes a 64-bit pointer");
_Static_assert(CACHE_LINE >= 64, "cache line is never smaller");

int main(void)
{
    printf("%d %d\n", BAD_MAX(1, 2), GOOD_MAX(1, 2));
    printf("%zu\n", ARRAY_LEN("hello"));
    printf("%d\n", CACHE_LINE);
    return 0;
}
/* Parenthesise every macro parameter and the whole expansion, without
   exception, and prefer an inline function whenever there is a body
   to write: it is type checked, evaluated once per argument, has an
   address, and can be stepped through in a debugger. */

4.4 The function-call hazards that survive review

Some C defects are visible in the source and some are not, and the ones that are not are the ones that reach production: argument evaluation order, the missing error check, and the function whose contract nobody wrote down.

Three hazards the compiler cannot see

Argument evaluation order is unspecified. When a call has two arguments with side effects, either may be evaluated first, and a compiler is free to choose, so f(g(), h()) has an order the source does not state. A missing error check is legal, compiles cleanly and is invisible until the input that triggers it. And the return value of a function whose result matters is often discarded, because discarding it is not a warning in most configurations. Then the four things to do properly, and each is a requirement. Sequence side effects explicitly — evaluate each into a named variable in separate statements — which costs two lines and makes the order the source's rather than the compiler's. Check every return value, including the ones that "cannot fail", because the ones that cannot fail today are the ones a later change will make fail. Make failure loud — a function that aborts on a violated invariant is better than one that continues with a broken one, and the difference shows up as a diagnosable crash rather than a wrong answer three modules away. And Keep the contract in the header, so that the preconditions and the failure behaviour travel with the declaration and a caller reads them at the call site. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Two arguments with side effects in one call, which produces an order-dependent result that changes when the compiler or its version changes. A return value discarded by a cast to void, which silences a warning that was the only signal. And A function that returns early on error without saying so in its name or signature, which means a caller that forgets to check cannot be distinguished from one that deliberately ignored it. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Make every function that can fail return a value you are required to look at, by returning a structure whose code field you must compare, so the compiler's unused-result warning applies. And Turn on the warning that flags an ignored return value, for the functions in your own headers, because it is the one warning that finds a whole class of defect rather than a single line.

#include <stdlib.h>

/* ---- 1. ARGUMENT EVALUATION ORDER IS UNSPECIFIED ---------------- */
static size_t next_token(const char **p);      /* advances *p       */
static size_t peek_token(const char *p);

/* The order is the source's business, and the source does not say. */
static void  before(int *a, int *b) { *a = 1; *b = 2; }
static void  show(int a, int b) { (void)a; (void)b; }

static void order_hazard(void)
{
    int x = 0, y = 0;
    /* UNSPECIFIED which runs first. A compiler may choose either,
       and may choose differently at different optimisation levels,
       so this is a bug that appears in release and not in debug. */
    show(before(&x, 0) + before(&y, 0), 0);

    /* Sequence it explicitly. Two lines, and the order is now the
       source's. */
    int a = 0, b = 0;
    before(&x, 0); before(&y, 0);
    show(a + b, 0);
}   /* The rule to remember: if two arguments have side effects, one
     of them is going to be surprising. */

/* ---- 2. THE RETURN VALUE NOBODY CHECKED ----------------------- */
static int parse_port(const char *s, int *out);

/* Compiles cleanly. Is a warning in most configurations. Produces
   an uninitialised value downstream if it fails. */
static void bad_caller(const char *s)
{
    int port;
    parse_port(s, &port);        /* result ignored: is port set?   */
    connect_to(port);            /* ... maybe not.                 */
}

/* RIGHT: make the result a value you cannot ignore silently. */
typedef struct { int code; int value; } int_result;
static int_result parse_port_checked(const char *s);

static void right_caller(const char *s)
{
    int_result r = parse_port_checked(s);
    switch (r.code) {
    case  0: connect_to(r.value); break;
    default: report("bad port", r.code); break;
    }
}

/* ---- 3. THE UNDOCUMENTED CONTRACT ------------------------------ */
/* Most surviving C defects are not syntax. They are a precondition
   that one file relies on and another file's refactor removed.
   The defence is to write the contract where the declaration is, so
   a caller reads it at the call site. */

/* The precondition is a real one and it is checkable at run time
   cheaply, which means a violated invariant is better as a loud
   abort than as a wrong answer three modules away. */
struct span { const char *p; size_t n; };

static char span_at(struct span s, size_t i)
{
    /* Violating the precondition here would read out of bounds.
       Returning a sentinel makes it detectable; aborting makes it
       diagnosable. Choose deliberately, and write which you chose. */
    if (i >= s.n) { fprintf(stderr, "span_at: out of range
"); abort(); }
    return s.p[i];
}

/* The warning that finds a whole class rather than a line. */
#if defined(__GNUC__) || defined(__clang__)
#  define MUST_CHECK __attribute__((warn_unused_result))
#elif defined(_MSC_VER)
#  define MUST_CHECK _Check_return_
#else
#  define MUST_CHECK
#endif

MUST_CHECK static int  write_all(const void *p, size_t n);
MUST_CHECK static int  fsync_now(int fd);
MUST_CHECK static char *dup_lower(const char *s);

/* Ignored return values then warn at EVERY call site, which is the
   whole mechanism: the warning follows the function rather than
   living in a code review checklist. */
/* Two hazards the compiler cannot see: argument evaluation order and
   a discarded result. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <stdint.h>

static void show(int a, int b) { printf("%d %d\n", a, b); }

/* ARGUMENT EVALUATION ORDER IS UNSPECIFIED in C. Both may be
   evaluated in either order, or interleaved. A compiler may choose
   differently at different optimisation levels, which is why this is
   a bug that appears in release and not in debug. */
static int first(void)  { printf("first\n");  return 1; }
static int second(void) { printf("second\n"); return 2; }

static void unspecified(void)
{
    show(first(), second());
    /* Either "first second" or "second first". Both are conforming.
       The C standard added a sequence point after each argument in
       C23; before that, nothing ordered them. */
}

/* Sequence it explicitly. Two named variables, and the order is now
   the source's. */
static void sequenced(void)
{
    int a = first();
    int b = second();
    show(a, b);
}

/* THE DISCARDED RESULT. Ignoring a return value is legal, is rarely
   a warning, and is the most common way a C program fails at run
   time. */
static int parse_port(const char *s, int *out);
MUST_CHECK int parse_port_checked(const char *s, int *out);

static void bad_caller(const char *s)
{
    int port;
    parse_port(s, &port);        /* result ignored: is port set? */
    connect_to(port);            /* ... maybe not                */
}

static void right_caller(const char *s)
{
    int port;
    if (parse_port_checked(s, &port) != 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "bad port: %s\n", s);
        return;
    }
    connect_to(port);
}

/* THE WARNING THAT FINDS THE WHOLE CLASS. It follows the FUNCTION,
   so it appears at every call site rather than in a checklist. */
#if defined(__GNUC__) || defined(__clang__)
#  define MUST_CHECK __attribute__((warn_unused_result))
#elif defined(_MSC_VER)
#  define MUST_CHECK _Check_return_
#else
#  define MUST_CHECK
#endif

MUST_CHECK static int write_all(const void *p, size_t n);
MUST_CHECK static int fsync_now(int fd);
MUST_CHECK static char *dup_lower(const char *s);

int main(void)
{
    unspecified();
    sequenced();
    return 0;
}
/* Two disciplines: sequence side effects into named variables
   whenever a call has more than one argument with a side effect, and
   mark every function whose result matters with the compiler's own
   unused-result attribute so the warning follows the function rather
   than living in a review checklist. */

5 · The Standard Library You Will Actually Use

5.1 Headers, the two halves of every function, and errno5.2 The string and memory functions, and their traps5.3 Time, random numbers and the two libraries with hidden state5.4 Randomness, and why the obvious function is a security hole

5.1 Headers, the two halves of every function, and errno

A standard library declaration carries two separate contracts — the return value and a thread-local error code — and reading only the first is the reason so much C code fails silently.

Return value or errno, and how to tell which you have

Some library functions report failure in the return value: an allocation that returns null, a read that returns zero. Others report failure only in errno, which means the return value is a valid result and you must check the error variable separately. And the two are not exclusive: a file read that returns zero bytes at the end of a file and a file read that failed both look like zero until you check errno, which is the specific mechanism behind an infinite loop or a truncated read. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Check errno only when the return value indicates a problem, because a successful call is permitted to leave it set from an earlier call. Set it to zero before the call if you intend to read it after, so a stale value from earlier cannot be mistaken for this call's. Include the header that declares the function, which in a strict implementation is the only way it can be called at all. And Know that the error code is thread-local, so it is per-thread state rather than a global, which is what makes it safe in a threaded program and what makes saving it across calls pointless. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Reading errno after a successful call, which reports a previous error and sends the investigation in the wrong direction. Not clearing it before a call that can fail, which means a failure with no error code looks like a failure with a stale one. And Assuming a returned zero means end-of-file rather than an error, which truncates a file at the first transient read error and is the mechanism behind an "intermittent" truncated download. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Wrap every library call that can fail in a function that converts it to a value, so the rest of the program never reads errno and cannot get the convention wrong. And Log the error code, not the message, because the message is localised and the code is stable, and because the code is what tells you which of the dozen causes actually happened.

#include <errno.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

/* A library declaration carries TWO contracts: the return value and
   a thread-local error variable. Reading only the first is why so
   much C code fails silently. */

/* These report failure in the RETURN VALUE. */
static void *malloc(size_t);                  /* NULL on failure    */
static FILE *fopen(const char *, const char *);

/* These report failure ONLY in errno. The return value is a valid
   result and you must check the variable separately. */
static char  *strerror_r(int, char *, size_t);
static int    rename(const char *, const char *);

/* And they are NOT exclusive. fread returning zero is both
   end-of-file and a read error until you look. */
static size_t fread(void *, size_t, size_t, FILE *);

/* Read at offset, and tell the two apart. This is the shape every
   real reader needs. */
static int read_all(FILE *f, unsigned char *dst, size_t want)
{
    size_t got = 0;
    while (got < want) {
        errno = 0;                     /* BEFORE the call        */
        size_t n = fread(dst + got, 1, want - got, f);
        if (n == 0) {
            if (ferror(f)) return -1;  /* a real error           */
            if (feof(f))  return 0;    /* clean end              */
            if (errno)    return -1;    /* something else        */
            return -1;                 /* not making progress    */
        }
        got += n;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* The rules.

   1. CHECK errno ONLY when the return value indicates a problem.
      A successful call may leave it set from an earlier one, so
      reading it unconditionally reports the WRONG error.
   2. SET IT TO ZERO before the call, or a stale value from earlier
      looks like this call's error.
   3. INCLUDE THE HEADER that declares the function. Under the
      current standard a call to an undeclared function is not
      valid at all, so a missing header is a hard error.
   4. IT IS THREAD-LOCAL. Per-thread, not global. Safe in a
      threaded program, and pointless to save across calls. */

/* Wrap it once, so nothing else in the program reads errno. */
typedef enum { IO_OK, IO_EOF, IO_ERR } io_status;

static io_status io_read(FILE *f, void *dst, size_t want, size_t *got)
{
    *got = 0;
    errno = 0;
    size_t n = fread(dst, 1, want, f);
    if (n == 0) return ferror(f) ? IO_ERR : IO_EOF;
    *got = n;
    if (n < want) return IO_EOF;
    return IO_OK;
}

/* Log the CODE, not the message: the message is localised and the
   code is stable, and the code is what tells you which of the dozen
   possible causes actually happened. */
static void log_io_error(const char *what, const char *path)
{
    int code = errno;
    fprintf(stderr, "%s: %s: %s (errno %d)
", what, path, strerror(code), code);
    /* strerror may return a pointer to a static buffer that another
       thread is about to overwrite - strerror_r is the version that
       is safe to call. That is one of the few places in C where the
       thread-safety argument changes which function you want. */
}
/* errno, and the three states every library call can be in. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <errno.h>
#include <unistd.h>
#include <fcntl.h>

/* Reading errno when the call SUCCEEDED reports a previous error and
   sends the investigation in the wrong direction. Zero it before the
   call if you intend to read it after. */
static int read_exact(int fd, void *dst, size_t want)
{
    unsigned char *p = dst;
    size_t got = 0;
    while (got < want) {
        errno = 0;                       /* BEFORE the call        */
        ssize_t k = read(fd, p + got, want - got);
        if (k < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue; /* a signal, not a failure */
            return -1;
        }
        if (k == 0) return 0;             /* clean short read       */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* THE THREE STATES a fallible call distinguishes, and only three:
   success, end of data, error. fread returning zero is BOTH of the
   last two until you look. */
static int read_all(int fd, void *dst, size_t want, size_t *got_out)
{
    *got_out = 0;
    for (;;) {
        errno = 0;
        ssize_t k = read(fd, (unsigned char *)dst + *got_out, want - *got_out);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return 0;            /* end of data             */
        *got_out += (size_t)k;
        if (*got_out == want) return 0;
    }
}

/* errno is THREAD-LOCAL, which is what makes it safe in threaded
   code - and also pointless to save across calls, because another
   thread's calls cannot affect yours but your own calls can. */
static int is_retryable(int e)
{
    switch (e) {
    case EINTR:      /* a signal arrived                        */
    case EAGAIN:     /* would block: retry with a poll           */
    case EBUSY:      /* the device is in use: retry later        */
        return 1;
    case EACCES:     /* permission: permanent                   */
    case ENOENT:     /* missing: permanent                      */
    case ENODEV:     /* not present: permanent                  */
        return 0;
    default:
        return 0;
    }
}

/* Log the CODE, not the message: the message is localised and
   changes with the locale, and the code is what tells you which of
   the dozen possible causes actually happened. */
static void report(const char *what, const char *path)
{
    int code = errno;                     /* capture FIRST        */
    fprintf(stderr, "%s: %s: %s (errno %d)\n",
            what, path, strerror(code), code);
}

int main(void)
{
    int fd = open("/etc/hostname", O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) { report("open", "/etc/hostname"); return 1; }

    char buf[256];
    size_t got;
    if (read_all(fd, buf, sizeof buf - 1, &got) == 0)
        buf[got] = '\0';
    close(fd);
    printf("%s\n", buf);
    return 0;
}
/* The four rules: check the return value first and only then look at
   errno; zero errno before a call whose error you intend to read;
   treat errno as thread-local state you never save across calls; and
   log the numeric code alongside the message. */

5.2 The string and memory functions, and their traps

The string and memory functions are the most-used code in C and the most-misused code in C, because several of them do not do what their names suggest and one of them does not terminate the destination at all.

The six functions that need a second look

The traps are concentrated in a handful of functions, and they share a cause: the API predates the platform's conventions and has been frozen ever since. The copy-with-a-count function does not terminate when the source fills the destination, which is the single most surprising behaviour in the library and is why it has a dedicated warning in most compilers. The concatenation functions append without checking whether the result fits. The comparison functions stop at the terminator, so comparing two buffers with embedded zeros compares only their prefixes. The copy and set functions take a length in bytes and trust it, which is safe only if the length is correct. The search functions return a pointer into the buffer, so a result from a function that may have reallocated is a dangling pointer. And the conversion functions have no failure mode you can see, so a number that does not convert becomes zero with no diagnostic. Then the four things to get right, and each is a requirement. Prefer the length-carrying interfaces for everything that is not a C string, because a buffer with a length is a buffer you can index safely. Treat every length as bytes, explicitly,, and never assume it is elements — the difference is the multiplication. Never use a result from a search on a buffer that a following call may reallocate, which is the mechanism behind a crash in a parser that looks correct. And Check the result of every numeric conversion, because there is no error and zero is a legitimate result. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Copying with a count into a destination that is exactly as long as the source, which leaves the destination unterminated and every later string function on it reads past the end. Truncating a path with a fixed-size buffer, which produces a path that names a different file rather than failing — the mechanism behind an operation that quietly writes to the wrong place. And Comparing a password with the byte-count comparison, which is not constant-time and therefore measurable, and which is the difference between a comparison and a check. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Write one bounded copy helper and route every copy through it, so the termination rule exists once and the truncating cases are handled explicitly rather than per call site. And Keep a length beside every buffer, in a structure, — the same advice as chapter 3's span, repeated here because it is the single highest-value convention in systems C.

#include <string.h>

/* THE TRAP. The count-copy does NOT terminate the destination when
   the source fills it. Every later string function on that buffer
   then reads past the end. Most compilers warn about exactly this. */
static char  dst[8];
static const char *src = "12345678";   /* 8 chars, no room for NUL */

static void the_trap(void)
{
    strncpy(dst, src, sizeof dst);     /* dst has NO terminator    */
    /* strlen(dst) reads past the end. strcmp, every print, every
       concatenation: all of them read whatever follows.            */
}

/* The version that terminates, and that TELLS you about truncation.
   This is the shape to write once and use everywhere. */
static int copy_bounded(char *out, size_t cap, const char *in)
{
    size_t n = strlen(in);
    if (n >= cap) {                    /* refuse, do not truncate */
        if (cap) out[0] = '';
        return 0;
    }
    memcpy(out, in, n + 1);
    return 1;
}

/* THE SECOND TRAP. Concatenation does not check whether it fits. */
static void concat_trap(void)
{
    char buf[16] = "prefix";
    strcat(buf, "-and-a-long-suffix");  /* writes past 16 bytes    */
}

/* THE THIRD TRAP. A fixed-size path buffer truncates SILENTLY, and
   the result names a different file rather than failing. */
#include <limits.h>
static void path_trap(void)
{
    char path[PATH_MAX];
    snprintf(path, sizeof path, "/var/lib/%s/%s", name, other);
    /* If the result is longer than PATH_MAX it is TRUNCATED, and the
       truncation produces a path to some other file. Check the
       return value of snprintf, not just that it is non-negative. */
}

/* THE FOURTH TRAP. Comparison stops at the terminator, so two
   buffers with embedded zeros compare only on their prefix. And a
   byte comparison of a secret is not constant-time, which makes it
   measurable. */
static int constant_time_eq(const void *a, const void *b, size_t n)
{
    const volatile unsigned char *x = a, *y = b;
    unsigned char d = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) d |= (unsigned char)(x[i] ^ y[i]);
    return d == 0;                     /* no early exit           */
}

/* THE FIFTH TRAP. A search result is a pointer INTO the buffer, so a
   following call that reallocates the buffer leaves it dangling.
   This is the mechanism behind a parser that looks correct and
   crashes on the input that triggered a growth. */
static int parse(const char *s)
{
    char *buf = malloc(strlen(s) + 1);
    strcpy(buf, s);
    const char *hit = strchr(buf, '=');   /* points INTO buf      */
    buf = realloc(buf, 4096);             /* buf moved            */
    (void)hit;                            /* DANGLING              */
    free(buf);
    return 0;
}

/* THE SIXTH TRAP. Numeric conversion has no visible failure mode, so
   an input that does not convert becomes zero with no diagnostic. */
#include <stdlib.h>
static void conversion_trap(void)
{
    const char *s = "not-a-number";
    long v = strtol(s, NULL, 10);         /* 0, no error          */
    /* strtol reports failure through the end pointer and errno.
       Asking for both is the only way to distinguish "the user typed
       zero" from "the user typed nonsense". */
    char *end;
    long w = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s || *end != '') { /* reject                  */ }
    (void)v; (void)w;
}  /* The two practices, once more because they are the whole
     chapter: route every copy through one bounded helper so the
     termination rule exists once, and keep a length beside every
     buffer in a structure so the two cannot be separated. */
/* One bounded helper, and the calls that stop going through it. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <ctype.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

/* THE HELPER. Copy or refuse. Never truncate silently. */
static int copy_checked(char *out, size_t cap, const char *in)
{
    size_t n = strlen(in);
    if (n >= cap) { if (cap) out[0] = '\0'; return 0; }
    memcpy(out, in, n + 1);
    return 1;
}

/* snprintf returns the WANTED length, so truncation is detectable
   without a second pass. Checking only that it is non-negative is
   the common miss. */
static int join_path(char *out, size_t cap,
                    const char *dir, const char *name)
{
    int want = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/%s", dir, name);
    if (want < 0) return -1;
    if ((size_t)want >= cap) { out[0] = '\0'; return -1; }
    return 0;
}

/* TRUNCATION IS WORSE THAN FAILURE. A path that names a different
   file is not an error - it is a successful write to the wrong
   place, which is the mechanism behind an incident nobody can
   reproduce. */
static void truncation_hazard(void)
{
    char path[16];
    /* snprintf truncates to 15 chars and NUL-terminates. No error,
       no warning, and the result is a valid path to something else. */
    snprintf(path, sizeof path, "/var/lib/verylong/%s", "app.db");
    printf("%s\n", path);       /* /var/lib/verylon - wrong file */
}

/* A FIXED-SIZE buffer plus an explicit length is the safe shape. */
typedef struct { char data[64]; size_t len; } small_str;

static int small_append(small_str *s, const char *text)
{
    size_t n = strlen(text);
    if (s->len + n + 1 > sizeof s->data) return 0;   /* refuse */
    memcpy(s->data + s->len, text, n + 1);
    s->len += n;
    return 1;
}

/* strtol reports failure through the end pointer AND errno. Asking
   for both is the only way to distinguish "the user typed zero" from
   "the user typed nonsense". */
static int parse_port(const char *s, unsigned *out)
{
    char *end;
    errno = 0;
    long v = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s)            return -1;   /* no digits at all     */
    if (*end != '\0')        return -1;   /* trailing garbage    */
    if (errno == ERANGE)      return -1;   /* overflow            */
    if (v < 1 || v > 65535)   return -1;   /* out of range        */
    *out = (unsigned)v;
    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    char dst[8];
    printf("%d\n", copy_checked(dst, sizeof dst, "1234567"));  /* 1 */
    printf("%d\n", copy_checked(dst, sizeof dst, "12345678")); /* 0 */

    unsigned p;
    printf("%d %d\n", parse_port("8080", &p), parse_port("80x", &p)); /* 0 -1 */

    small_str s = { {0}, 0 };
    small_append(&s, "hello ");
    small_append(&s, "world");
    printf("%s\n", s.data);
    truncation_hazard();
    return 0;
}
/* The two rules: write one bounded copy or join helper and route
   every call through it, so the "refuse rather than truncate" policy
   exists in exactly one place; and check snprintf's return value
   against the buffer size, because a non-negative return only means
   it did not error, not that it fit. */

5.3 Time, random numbers and the two libraries with hidden state

Time and randomness are the two areas where the obvious C function is the wrong one, and both have a replacement that is strictly better and that beginners are never told about.

Time: two clocks and a calendar that is not one

There are two clocks and they answer different questions. The monotonic clock cannot go backwards — it is unaffected by a clock adjustment, which is why it is the only correct source for measuring an interval. The real-time clock can go backwards, because the user or a synchronisation service can set it, and that is why it is the only source for saying what time it is. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Measure intervals with the monotonic clock, always, because a real-time clock that steps backwards mid-measurement produces a negative duration and a loop that never terminates. Store absolute times in the real-time clock's units and attach a timezone, because a timestamp without a timezone is ambiguous and because a system that stores local time cannot survive a user changing their timezone. Format times through the calendar functions rather than by hand, because month names, leap years, daylight saving transitions and week numbering are all things that are wrong if you compute them. And Never assume the calendar is proleptic Gregorian unless you have checked, because the conversion functions may map a date outside the representable range. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Measuring with the real-time clock, which produces a negative interval when the clock is adjusted and a measurement that is simply wrong when it is not. Storing a local time, which means the same stored value means two different instants depending on who reads it. And Using a random function for anything that matters, which is the next section and the single most common security mistake in otherwise careful C. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Wrap the clock in two functions, — one for "now" and one for "how long since" — so the choice of clock is made once and the distinction cannot be forgotten at a call site. And Store timestamps as a fixed-width integer in a stated epoch, and convert for display only, so the stored value is unambiguous and the conversion is testable.

#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <time.h>

/* Two clocks, two questions.

   MONOTONIC: never goes backwards. Unaffected by a clock
   adjustment. The ONLY correct source for measuring an interval.
   REAL TIME: can go backwards - the user or a sync service can set
   it. The only source for saying what time it is. */

typedef struct { uint64_t ns; } instant;      /* monotonic        */
typedef struct { int64_t  unix_s; } stamp;    /* real time         */

static instant now(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    /* CLOCK_MONOTONIC. On Linux you may prefer the RAW variant so
       that an NTP slew - which adjusts the RATE rather than the
       value - cannot shorten your measurement. */
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return (instant){ (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000ull
                    + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec };
}

static uint64_t elapsed_ns(instant a, instant b)
{
    return b.ns - a.ns;      /* unsigned: correct across the wrap  */
}  /* Signed subtraction here produces a NEGATIVE duration when the
     clock steps, which turns a timed loop into an infinite one. */

/* Wall-clock time, stored unambiguously and converted for display. */
static stamp now_stamp(void)
{
    return (stamp){ (int64_t)time(NULL) };
}

/* NEVER store a local time. The same stored value means two
   different instants depending on who reads it, and a user who
   changes their timezone sees their own history reorder itself. */

static void print_stamp_local(stamp s)
{
    time_t t = (time_t)s.unix_s;
    struct tm tmv;
    /* The _r variants take a buffer and are thread-safe. The
       non-_r ones return a pointer to a static buffer that another
       thread may already have overwritten. */
    if (!localtime_r(&t, &tmv)) return;
    char buf[64];
    if (strftime(buf, sizeof buf, "%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S %z", &tmv) == 0) return;
    puts(buf);
}

/* Calendar arithmetic is not something to write yourself: month
   lengths, leap years, week numbering and daylight saving
   transitions are all cases where an off-by-one is easy and a
   wrong answer is invisible until a customer sees it. */

static void monotonic_demo(void)
{
    instant a = now();
    /* work */
    instant b = now();
    printf("%llu ns
", (unsigned long long)elapsed_ns(a, b));
}

/* Two wrapper functions, and the reason for them: the choice of
   clock is made ONCE, so no call site can accidentally use the
   wall clock for a duration. That is the whole benefit - it is not
   tidiness, it is removing a decision from thirty call sites. */

/* And the warning that belongs next to any use of a general random
   function: see the next section. A rand()-based token is not a
   token; it is a number anybody can predict by observing four
   outputs. */
/* Two clocks, and the trap that comes from choosing wrong. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <time.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <errno.h>

typedef struct { uint64_t ns; } instant;
typedef struct { int64_t  unix_s; } stamp;

/* MEASURE WITH THE MONOTONIC CLOCK. It cannot go backwards, so a
   duration computed from it is correct even if the wall clock was
   adjusted mid-measurement. */
static instant now(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return (instant){ (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000ull
                    + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec };
}

static uint64_t elapsed_ns(instant a, instant b)
{
    return b.ns - a.ns;    /* unsigned: correct across the wrap */
}

/* THE TRAP. Parsing a timestamp to measure elapsed time is two wall
   clock reads, and an adjustment between them gives a negative or
   wildly wrong duration. It looks correct on every machine whose
   clock never moves. */
static double WRONG_measure(void)
{
    const char *s1 = "2026-01-01T00:00:00Z";
    const char *s2 = "2026-01-01T00:00:01Z";
    struct tm t1, t2;
    memset(&t1, 0, sizeof t1);
    memset(&t2, 0, sizeof t2);
    strptime(s1, "%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ", &t1);
    strptime(s2, "%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ", &t2);
    return difftime(mktime(&t2), mktime(&t1));
}

/* SLEEP FOR A DURATION, NEVER A DEADLINE. An interrupted sleep must
   not shorten the wait, so the remaining time has to be recomputed
   from the request structure the kernel writes back. */
static int sleep_ms(unsigned ms)
{
    struct timespec ts = { (time_t)(ms / 1000),
                           (long)(ms % 1000) * 1000000L };
    while (nanosleep(&ts, &ts) != 0) {
        if (errno != EINTR) return -1;
        /* ts now holds the REMAINING time. Return it to the kernel;
           restarting with the original request oversleeps. */
    }
    return 0;
}

/* A sleep is not a synchronisation primitive: two processes sleeping
   the same interval wake within scheduling granularity, not in
   order. Express a wait as a CONDITION. */
static int wait_for_fd(int fd, int timeout_ms);

int main(void)
{
    instant a = now();
    /* work */
    instant b = now();
    printf("%llu ns\n", (unsigned long long)elapsed_ns(a, b));
    sleep_ms(100);
    return 0;
}
/* Two clocks, two questions: the monotonic clock cannot jump and is
   the only correct source for an interval; the real-time clock can
   jump and is the only source for saying what time it is. Format a
   real-time value for display and attach a timezone to everything
   you store — a stored local time means two different instants
   depending on who reads it. */

5.4 Randomness, and why the obvious function is a security hole

The general random number generator in the C library is a linear congruential generator with no cryptographic property whatsoever, and using it for anything a user should not be able to predict is one of the most widespread security defects in C.

What the standard function gives you, and what it is

The library provides a linear congruential generator: a state that is advanced by multiplying and adding, and taking the remainder. Its output is entirely determined by its seed, — a handful of consecutive outputs is enough to recover the seed, and therefore to predict every subsequent value including the ones you have not produced yet. The seed function takes an integer, so the state space is the range of one integer, which on a typical platform is a few billion possibilities, and an attacker who triggers a few outputs has narrowed that to one. And it is not even a good statistical generator: the low bits of a linear congruential generator cycle with short periods, so taking the output modulo a small number produces visible patterns. Then the four things to get right, and each is a requirement. Use the operating system's cryptographic source for anything security-relevant — identifiers, tokens, nonces, keys, salts — because it is seeded from the kernel's own entropy pool, is not reconstructible from its outputs, and on the platforms that matter is a system call rather than a library. Never use the seeded function for anything an attacker interacts with, because a seed derived from the clock or the process identifier is known to an attacker who knows roughly when the process started. Use the unseeded function only for things where predictability is harmless, — jitter in a simulation, a load-shedding coin flip, an identifier used to break a tie locally. And Do not roll your own generator, because the failure mode of a broken generator is invisible and the properties that matter are subtle. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A token generated from the clock, which an attacker narrows to a few thousand values by guessing roughly when the action was triggered. A password reset link containing a predictable identifier, which is the same mechanism and is the shape of most reset-link advisories. And Using the general generator for a shuffle, which produces a permutation whose order is mathematically constrained even when the values look unrelated — the mechanism behind a "random" ordering that a user can predict after a few observations. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Centralise randomness behind one module, so that every use is a deliberate choice between the secure source and the general one, and so that a future change of algorithm touches one file. And Never compare a generated token with the ordinary byte comparison, but with the constant-time one, because a comparison that returns early leaks the length of the matching prefix — which turns a 128-bit token into a token whose first byte you can guess a million times a second.

/* The library generator is a LINEAR CONGRUENTIAL generator:

     state = state * 1103515245 + 12345
     out   = (state / 65536) % 32768

   Its output is ENTIRELY DETERMINED BY ITS SEED. A handful of
   consecutive outputs is enough to recover the seed, and therefore
   to predict every value you have not produced yet. It is also not
   a good STATISTICAL generator: the low bits cycle with short
   periods, so out % 10 is visibly patterned. */

/* The seed function takes an INTEGER, so the whole state space is
   the range of one integer - a few billion on a typical platform -
   and an attacker who sees a few outputs narrows it to one. */

/* ---- the correct answer for anything security-relevant -------- */

/* On Linux and the BSDs: getrandom(2), which reads the kernel
   entropy pool and blocks until enough is available. On Windows,
   the equivalent is BCryptGenRandom or the RtlGenRandom system
   interface. Both are a SYSTEM CALL, not a library, and both are
   seeded from the kernel rather than from a number you chose. */

#include 
#include <errno.h>

enum { TOKEN_BYTES = 32 };   /* 256 bits: not guessable           */

static int secure_token(unsigned char out[TOKEN_BYTES])
{
    size_t got = 0;
    while (got < TOKEN_BYTES) {
        ssize_t n = getrandom(out + got, TOKEN_BYTES - got, 0);
        if (n < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;    /* a signal, not an error */
            return -1;
        }
        got += (size_t)n;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* Where the interface has no system call, read from the device
   that provides one and never fall back silently. */
#include <fcntl.h>
static int device_token(unsigned char *out, size_t n)
{
    int fd = open("/dev/urandom", O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) return -1;
    int rc = 0;
    while (n) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, out, n);
        if (k <= 0) { if (k < 0 && errno == EINTR) continue; rc = -1; break; }
        out += k; n -= (size_t)k;
    }
    close(fd);
    return rc;
}

/* ---- where the weak generator IS acceptable -------------------- */

/* Jitter in a simulation, a coin flip for load shedding, a local
   tie-break identifier. All harmless if predicted. */
static unsigned jitter(unsigned bound)
{
    unsigned r;
    /* The unseeded function uses a fixed seed, so its output is the
       SAME on every run on every machine. Deterministic, which is
       exactly what a repeatable test wants and exactly wrong for
       anything else. */
    r = (unsigned)rand();
    return bound ? r % bound : r;
}

/* ---- three failures, three mechanisms ------------------------- */

/* 1. A TOKEN FROM THE CLOCK. An attacker who knows roughly when the
      action was triggered narrows the seed to a few thousand
      values and tries them all. */
static long bad_token_from_time(void)
{
    return (long)time(NULL);       /* guessable in seconds        */
}

/* 2. A PASSWORD RESET LINK containing a predictable identifier.
      Same mechanism, and the shape of most reset advisories:
      the link's secret is a counter, a timestamp or an id. */

/* 3. A SHUFFLE from the weak generator. The permutation's order is
      mathematically constrained even when the values look
      unrelated, so a user can predict it after a few observations.
      A Fisher-Yates shuffle needs an UNBIASED RANDOM INDEX in
      [0, i] for every step, which is a stronger requirement than a
      uniform value in the whole range - and the weak generator's
      short-period low bits fail it. */

/* The second practice, which is the one people forget: a token
   comparison must be CONSTANT TIME. An early-exit comparison leaks
   the length of the matching prefix, which turns a 256-bit token
   into one whose first byte can be guessed a million times a
   second. See constant_time_eq in section 5.2. */
/* Why rand() is not a random source, and what to use instead. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <errno.h>
#include <unistd.h>
#include <fcntl.h>

/* THE LIBRARY GENERATOR IS A LINEAR CONGRUENTIAL one:
     state = state * 1103515245 + 12345
   Its output is ENTIRELY DETERMINED BY ITS SEED. A handful of
   consecutive outputs is enough to recover the seed, and therefore
   to predict every value you have not produced yet - including the
   tokens. */

/* The seed function takes an INTEGER, so the whole state space is
   the range of one integer: a few billion on a typical platform,
   narrowed to one by an attacker who triggers a few outputs. */
static unsigned short weak_state;
static void     weak_seed(unsigned s)  { weak_state = (unsigned short)s; }
static unsigned weak_next(void)
{
    weak_state = (unsigned short)(weak_state * 1103515245u + 12345u);
    return (unsigned)((weak_state / 65536u) % 32768u);
}

/* It is also not a good STATISTICAL generator: the low bits cycle
   with a short period, so weak_next() % 10 is visibly patterned. */

/* THE CORRECT SOURCE for anything security-relevant. On Linux and
   the BSDs this reads the kernel entropy pool; on Windows it is
   BCryptGenRandom. Both are system calls, not libraries. */
#include 

enum { TOKEN_BYTES = 32 };        /* 256 bits: not guessable */

static int secure_token(unsigned char out[TOKEN_BYTES])
{
    size_t got = 0;
    while (got < TOKEN_BYTES) {
        ssize_t n = getrandom(out + got, TOKEN_BYTES - got, 0);
        if (n < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;   /* a signal, not an error */
            return -1;
        }
        got += (size_t)n;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* Where the system call is unavailable, read the device directly and
   never fall back silently to the weak generator. */
static int device_token(unsigned char *out, size_t n)
{
    int fd = open("/dev/urandom", O_RDONLY);
    if (fd < 0) return -1;
    int rc = 0;
    while (n) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, out, n);
        if (k <= 0) {
            if (k < 0 && errno == EINTR) continue;
            rc = -1;
            break;
        }
        out += k;
        n -= (size_t)k;
    }
    close(fd);
    return rc;
}

/* WHERE THE WEAK GENERATOR IS FINE: jitter in a simulation, a
   load-shedding coin flip, a local tie-break identifier. Its
   determinism is what a repeatable test wants. */
static unsigned jitter(unsigned bound)
{
    unsigned r = (unsigned)rand();
    return bound ? r % bound : r;
}

/* A Fisher-Yates shuffle needs an UNBIASED index in [0, i] at each
   step, which is a stronger requirement than a uniform value in the
   whole range - and the weak generator's short-period low bits fail
   it. This is why "random" orderings are sometimes predictable. */
static void shuffle(int *a, size_t n)
{
    for (size_t i = n; i-- > 1; ) {
        size_t j = secure_random_u32() % (i + 1);
        int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[j]; a[j] = t;
    }
}

int main(void)
{
    weak_seed((unsigned)time(NULL));
    printf("%u %u\n", weak_next(), weak_next());   /* predictable */

    unsigned char tok[TOKEN_BYTES];
    if (secure_token(tok) == 0) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) printf("%02x", tok[i]);
        printf("\n");
    }
    return 0;
}
/* Two rules: take every token, nonce, key and identifier from the
   operating system's cryptographic source and never from the seeded
   function, whose seed is derived from the clock or the process id
   and is therefore known to an attacker; and compare a generated
   token with the constant-time comparison from section 5.2, because
   an early-exit comparison turns a 256-bit token into one whose
   first byte can be guessed a million times a second. */

6 · Files, Errors and the System Calls Underneath

6.1 Streams, buffering and why a write is not a write6.2 Error handling: three strategies and when each is right6.3 Opening files safely, and the paths that are not what you think6.4 The system-call boundary: what changes and what does not

6.1 Streams, buffering and why a write is not a write

A standard stream is a layer between your code and a file descriptor, and its buffering is the reason a program can lose data on exit and the reason a program that reads a file it is writing sees nothing.

Three layers, and where the surprises live

There are three ways to move bytes and each has a different set of surprises. An unbuffered system call goes straight to the kernel and is what you want when you need the operation to have happened before the next one. A buffered stream adds a userspace buffer that is flushed on demand, which is fast for bulk transfer and is the reason a program that crashes loses its buffer. A direct memory access transfer bypasses the CPU entirely, which is the only way to move a large amount quickly and which has its own alignment requirements. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Close every stream, and check the close, because the buffer is flushed at close and the flush can fail — a disk full during close is reported by close, not by the writes that preceded it. Flush before you need the data to be visible elsewhere, which is the mechanism behind "the other process sees an empty file". Do not mix buffered and unbuffered access to the same file without flushing, because the two views can disagree and the file position is shared. And Know that closing a stream that was never opened on some implementations flushes an unrelated stream, which is the historical reason the library closes all streams at exit and the reason a leak in one part of a program can corrupt another part's output. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. An unflushed buffer at exit, which loses the last write in a program that exits by returning from main after an error path that closed something else first. A read on a file being written, which reads the file the writer has written so far, and which is the mechanism behind a program that appears to loop forever waiting for data it has already written. And A partial read or write treated as a whole, which is normal on both — a system call can transfer fewer bytes than asked and the only correct response is to loop. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Write one function per direction that loops until done or fails, so the partial-transfer case is handled once and every caller inherits it. And Make the flush explicit at every point where another process may be looking, which is cheap and which removes an entire class of "it did not see my data" report.

#include <errno.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <unistd.h>

/* Three ways to move bytes, three sets of surprises:

   UNBUFFERED system call - straight to the kernel. Use when the
     operation must have happened before the next one.
   BUFFERED stream - a userspace buffer, flushed on demand. Fast for
     bulk transfer, and the reason a crash loses the buffer.
   DIRECT MEMORY ACCESS transfer - bypasses the CPU. The only way to
     move a large amount quickly; has its own alignment rules. */

/* A system call can transfer FEWER bytes than asked. That is normal,
   not an error, and the only correct response is to loop. */
static int write_all(int fd, const void *p, size_t n)
{
    const unsigned char *q = p;
    while (n) {
        ssize_t k = write(fd, q, n);
        if (k < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;   /* a signal, not a fail  */
            return -1;
        }
        if (k == 0) return -1;              /* no progress          */
        q += (size_t)k;
        n -= (size_t)k;
    }
    return 0;
}

static int read_all(int fd, void *p, size_t n)
{
    unsigned char *q = p;
    while (n) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, q, n);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) break;                  /* END OF FILE          */
        q += (size_t)k;
        n -= (size_t)k;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* CLOSE CHECKS ITS OWN FAILURE. The buffer is flushed at close, and
   the flush can fail - a disk full is reported by CLOSE, not by any
   of the writes that preceded it. This is the single most-missed
   error check in systems programming. */
static int write_file(const char *path, const void *data, size_t n)
{
    int fd = open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_TRUNC, 0600);
    if (fd < 0) return -1;

    if (write_all(fd, data, n) != 0) { int e = errno; close(fd); errno = e; return -1; }

    if (close(fd) != 0) return -1;          /* the flush happens here */
    return 0;
}

/* DO NOT MIX buffered and unbuffered access to the same file
   without flushing: the file position is shared, so the two views
   can disagree about where they are. */
static void mixed_access(void)
{
    FILE *f = fopen("f", "w+");
    fputs("hello", f);
    fflush(f);                              /* or the read below
                                               sees nothing        */
    rewind(f);
    /* ... */
    fclose(f);
}

/* The other-process case: a program that reads a file it is writing
   sees whatever has been flushed, so it must poll. */
static int wait_for_nonempty(const char *path, int max_ms)
{
    for (int ms = 0; ms < max_ms; ++ms) {
        FILE *f = fopen(path, "r");
        if (f) {
            int c = fgetc(f);
            fclose(f);
            if (c != EOF) return 0;
        }
        struct timespec ts = { 0, 1000000L };   /* 1 ms             */
        nanosleep(&ts, NULL);
    }
    return -1;
}

/* At exit the library flushes and closes every stream. A stream left
   open therefore has its buffer flushed by that path - but only if
   the program exits normally. An abort, a fatal error or a signal
   skips it, and the last write is lost. That is the argument for
   flushing at the point where you have decided the data is
   complete, rather than at the point where you happen to exit. */

/* Close checks its own failure: the buffer is flushed at close, and
   the flush can fail. A full disk is reported by fclose. */
static int write_file(const char *path, const void *data, size_t n)
{
    FILE *f = fopen(path, "wb");
    if (!f) return -1;
    if (fwrite(data, 1, n, f) != n) {
        int e = errno;
        fclose(f);
        errno = e;
        return -1;
    }
    if (fclose(f) != 0) return -1;   /* the flush happens HERE */
    return 0;
}

/* Do not mix buffered and unbuffered access to the same file: the
   file position is shared, so the two views disagree. */
static void mixing_is_a_bug(void)
{
    FILE *f = fopen("data.txt", "w+");
    fputs("hello", f);
    rewind(f);                /* the STREAM's notion of position */
    char buf[16];
    fgets(buf, sizeof buf, f); /* ... but the write may be buffered */
    fclose(f);
}

/* The explicit flush that fixes "the other process sees an empty
   file": commit at the point the data is complete, not at the point
   the program happens to exit. */
static void flush_at_completion(void)
{
    FILE *f = fopen("progress.txt", "w");
    for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++) {
        fprintf(f, "%d\n", i);
        fflush(f);
    }
    fclose(f);
}

/* A system call can transfer FEWER bytes than asked - a signal, a
   partial pipe, a slow disk. The only correct response is to loop. */
static int write_all(int fd, const void *p, size_t n)
{
    const unsigned char *q = p;
    while (n) {
        ssize_t k = write(fd, q, n);
        if (k < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;
            return -1;
        }
        if (k == 0) return -1;   /* not an error, but not progress */
        q += (size_t)k;
        n -= (size_t)k;
    }
    return 0;
}

6.2 Error handling: three strategies and when each is right

C gives you no exception mechanism, so every program picks one of three strategies — return a value, return through a pointer, or terminate — and mixing them in one module is the most common structural defect in C code.

The three strategies and their costs

Return a status. Cheap, explicit, and forces every caller to deal with it — but it pollutes every signature and it is invisible to a caller who ignores the return value, which the compiler will not stop unless you ask. Return through an out-parameter. Same explicitness, and it lets the real result and the status have different types so they cannot be confused. Or return a sentinel. One value means failure — null, minus one, a status enumeration — and it is compact, but the return type must not have a legitimate value that means the same thing, which is why a status enumeration is better than a sentinel wherever a status enumeration is possible. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Pick one strategy per module and write it down, because a module where half the functions return a status and half print to standard error cannot be called from a library context and cannot have its failures tested. Never mix a status return with a diagnostic written to a stream, because the caller cannot suppress or redirect one and therefore cannot decide whether the message is wanted. Propagate a failure you cannot handle immediately, rather than logging it and continuing, because continuing after a failed allocation is the mechanism behind most crashes in long-running services. And Make the terminating strategy an explicit opt-in, so that a library never aborts a caller's program and a program can choose it for its own invariants. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A function that returns a status and also prints, which produces a message on a caller's stream that the caller cannot intercept. An error code that is also a legitimate result, which makes the failure undetectable by a caller who compares against a value rather than against the code. And Continuing after a failed allocation, which is a null dereference a few frames away from the cause and therefore attributed to the wrong function. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Put every error type in one header and give each a function that renders it as text, so the whole program's error vocabulary is one file and one function rather than a set of local conventions. And Assert your invariants, not your inputs, — an internal contradiction should abort with a message naming the assertion, while a bad argument should return an error, because those are two different events with two different audiences.

#include <stdarg.h>
#include <stdio.h>

/* Three strategies. Pick ONE per module and write it down. */

/* 1. RETURN A STATUS. Cheap, explicit, forces every caller to deal
      with it - but invisible to a caller who ignores the return,
      which the compiler will not stop unless you ask it to. */
static int store_record(const record *r);

/* 2. RETURN THROUGH A POINTER. Same explicitness, and it lets the
      real result and the status have DIFFERENT TYPES, so they cannot
      be confused. */
typedef struct { int code; const char *msg; } err_t;
static err_t parse_input(const char *s, record *out);

/* 3. RETURN A SENTINEL. Compact - but the type must not have a
      legitimate value that means the same thing, which is why a
      status enumeration beats a bare -1 wherever it is possible. */
static record *find_record(id_t id);

/* NEVER MIX a status return with a diagnostic written to a stream:
   the caller cannot suppress or redirect one, so it cannot decide
   whether the message is wanted. That is what makes a library
   unprintable in a test and unusable in a service. */

/* One error type for the whole module, in one header, with one
   function that renders it. The vocabulary is then a file rather
   than a set of local conventions. */
typedef enum {
    E_OK = 0, E_NOMEM, E_RANGE, E_NOTFOUND, E_IO, E_CONFLICT, E_STATE
} code_t;

const char *err_text(code_t c);
static err_t  fail(code_t c) { return (err_t){ c, err_text(c) }; }

/* Mark the ones a caller must look at. The warning follows the
   FUNCTION, so it appears at every call site rather than in a
   review checklist. */
#if defined(__GNUC__) || defined(__clang__)
#  define CHECKED __attribute__((warn_unused_result))
#else
#  define CHECKED
#endif

CHECKED static err_t save(record *r);
CHECKED static err_t load(id_t id, record *out);

/* PROPAGATE what you cannot handle. Continuing past a failed
   allocation is the mechanism behind most crashes in long-running
   services: the null is dereferenced three frames later, in a
   function that looks innocent, and the stack trace points at the
   wrong place entirely. */
CHECKED static err_t save_all(record *rs, size_t n)
{
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        err_t e = save(&rs[i]);
        if (e.code != E_OK) return e;      /* stop here            */
    }
    return fail(E_OK);
}

/* ASSERT invariants, RETURN on bad inputs. These are two different
   events with two different audiences:
     - a violated invariant is a bug in THIS program. Abort with a
       message naming the assertion, because a caller cannot fix it
       and continuing produces a worse failure.
     - a bad argument is the CALLER's problem. Return an error, so
       the caller can report it and continue. */
static err_t set_limit(config *c, long limit)
{
    if (limit < 0 || limit > HARD_MAX)
        return fail(E_RANGE);              /* caller's problem     */

    assert(c->magic == CONFIG_MAGIC);      /* OUR problem          */
    c->limit = limit;
    return fail(E_OK);
}  /* Aborting inside a library is a choice, and it is a legitimate
     one - but it must be the PROGRAM's choice, never a library
     silently taking away its caller's process. */

typedef enum { E_OK = 0, E_RANGE, E_NOMEM, E_IO } code_t;
typedef struct { code_t code; const char *msg; } err_t;

static err_t fail(code_t c) { return (err_t){ c, "see code" }; }

/* An out-parameter: the result and the status have DIFFERENT TYPES,
   so they cannot be confused. */
static err_t parse_port(const char *s, unsigned *out)
{
    char *end;
    errno = 0;
    long v = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s || *end != '\0') return fail(E_RANGE);
    if (errno == ERANGE)            return fail(E_RANGE);
    if (v < 1 || v > 65535)         return fail(E_RANGE);
    *out = (unsigned)v;
    return fail(E_OK);
}

/* Propagate what you cannot handle. Continuing past a failed
   allocation is a null dereference three frames away, attributed to
   the wrong function. */
static err_t save_all(const char **keys, const char **vals, size_t n)
{
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (store_write(keys[i], vals[i]) != 0)
            return fail(E_IO);   /* stop HERE, not later */
    return fail(E_OK);
}

/* Assert invariants, return on bad input: two different events with
   two different audiences. */
static err_t set_limit(int *config, long limit)
{
    if (limit < 0 || limit > HARD_MAX) return fail(E_RANGE); /* the
                                                               caller's */
    assert(config != NULL);      /* OUR bug: abort, because a caller
                                    cannot fix it                */
    *config = (int)limit;
    return fail(E_OK);
}

/* Never mix a status return with a diagnostic on a stream: the caller
   cannot suppress one, which is what makes a library unprintable in
   a test and unusable in a service with its own logging. */
static err_t WRONG(void)
{
    if (acquire_lock() < 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "error\n");   /* lands in the caller's log */
        return fail(E_IO);
    }
    return fail(E_OK);
}

6.3 Opening files safely, and the paths that are not what you think

A file path is a string the program did not create, in a directory structure the program does not control, and every step between the two is a place a hostile value can get in.

What opening a file actually involves

Opening a file by name resolves a string through the current directory, a chain of symbolic links, and a permission check — in that order, and every step is attacker-influenced if the name came from outside. The safe pattern is to resolve to a descriptor first and use the descriptor afterwards, because a descriptor is an integer the kernel handed you and cannot be redirected afterwards; a path is a string that the filesystem state can change under you. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Never construct a path by concatenating untrusted input, because a component containing a separator turns one name into two. Use the functions that take a descriptor and a name separately, rather than the ones that take one path, so that the name cannot escape its directory. Open with the narrowest mode that works,, because a mode that permits more than needed is a mode another process can exploit through the file you created. And Verify what you opened, — a symbolic link to a file you did not intend is the mechanism behind a class of attacks that no amount of care inside your own process prevents. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Checking a path string for separators, which is defeated by encoding, by a platform-specific separator, and by a component that is not a path at all. Treating a temporary file as private, which is defeated because the name is predictable, so another process can open it between your creation and your first write. And Following a symbolic link in a directory you assumed you controlled, which is the mechanism behind a symlink attack in a shared temporary directory. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Create temporary files with the library's exclusive-create call, which fails if the name exists and therefore cannot be pre-empted, and set the permissions explicitly because the default from the umask is not a choice you made. And Open a file, then work with the descriptor, — never re-open by name inside the operation — so that a rename or a symlink swap during the operation cannot redirect what you are reading.

#include <fcntl.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include 
#include <unistd.h>

/* A path is a STRING the program did not create, resolved through
   the current directory, a chain of symlinks and a permission check
   - in that order, and every step is attacker-influenced if the name
   came from outside.

   The safe pattern: resolve to a DESCRIPTOR first, then use the
   descriptor. An integer the kernel handed you cannot be redirected
   afterwards; a string can. */

/* NEVER concatenate untrusted input into a path: a component with a
   separator turns one name into two. Checking for separators is
   defeated by encoding, by another platform's separator, and by a
   component that is not a path at all. */

/* The descriptor-based family, where the directory is fixed by
   construction and the name is a single component: */
static int open_in_dir(int dirfd, const char *name, int flags)
{
    return openat(dirfd, name, flags | O_NOFOLLOW);
}

/* Exclusive create: fails if the name exists, so it cannot be
   pre-empted by a process that guessed the name. */
static int make_temp(char *out, size_t cap)
{
    const char *dir = getenv("TMPDIR");
    if (!dir || !*dir) dir = "/tmp";
    int fd;
    for (int tries = 0; tries < 100; ++tries) {
        unsigned r = secure_random_u32();
        int n = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/tmp.XXXXXXXX", dir);
        if (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= cap) return -1;
        char *x = strstr(out, "XXXXXXXX");
        /* fill the template with unpredictable characters */
        for (int i = 0; i < 8; ++i) x[i] = "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789"[r % 36], r /= 36;
        fd = open(out, O_CREAT | O_EXCL | O_RDWR, 0600);
        if (fd >= 0) return fd;
        if (errno != EEXIST) return -1;      /* not a name collision */
    }
    return -1;                               /* gave up             */
}

/* Permissions are EXPLICIT because the default from the umask is a
   value you did not choose and cannot see. 0600 is a file only the
   owner may read: the right default for anything a user supplied. */

/* VERIFY what you opened. A symlink to a file you did not intend is
   the mechanism behind a whole class of attacks that no care inside
   your process prevents. O_NOFOLLOW refuses the obvious one. */
static int open_regular(const char *path)
{
    struct stat st;
    int fd = open(path, O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW);
    if (fd < 0) return -1;
    if (fstat(fd, &st) != 0) { close(fd); return -1; }
    if (!S_ISREG(st.st_mode)) { close(fd); errno = EINVAL; return -1; }
    /* Open the DIRECTORY first and confirm it is not a symlink before
       trusting the name inside it. */
    return fd;
}   /* The rule that makes all of this robust: OPEN ONCE, THEN WORK
     WITH THE DESCRIPTOR. Never re-open by name inside the
     operation, so a rename or a symlink swap during the operation
     cannot redirect what you are reading. That single discipline
     removes a category of attack that no amount of string checking
     would have covered. */

/* The descriptor-based family: the directory is fixed by
   construction, the name is a single component, and the kernel
   refuses a symlink in the final position. */
static int open_in_dir(int dirfd, const char *name, int flags)
{
    if (!name || !*name || strchr(name, '/')) { errno = EINVAL; return -1; }
    if (!strcmp(name, ".") || !strcmp(name, "..")) { errno = EINVAL; return -1; }
    return openat(dirfd, name, flags | O_NOFOLLOW | O_CLOEXEC);
}

/* Exclusive create: fails if the name exists, so it cannot be
   pre-empted by a process that guessed it. This is the symlink
   attack in a shared /tmp, closed with one flag. */
static int make_temp(char *out, size_t cap)
{
    const char *dir = getenv("TMPDIR");
    if (!dir || !*dir) dir = "/tmp";
    for (int tries = 0; tries < 100; tries++) {
        unsigned r = secure_random_u32();
        int n = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/tmp.XXXXXXXX", dir);
        if (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= cap) { errno = ENAMETOOLONG; return -1; }
        char *x = out + n - 8;
        for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) { x[i] = "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789"[r % 36]; r /= 36; }
        int fd = open(out, O_CREAT | O_EXCL | O_RDWR | O_CLOEXEC, 0600);
        if (fd >= 0) return fd;
        if (errno != EEXIST) return -1;   /* not a name collision */
    }
    errno = EEXIST;
    return -1;
}

/* Permissions are EXPLICIT because the default from the umask is a
   value you did not choose. 0600 is a file only the owner reads,
   which is the right default for anything user-supplied. */

/* Open once, then work with the descriptor. Never re-open by name
   inside the operation, so a rename or a symlink swap during it
   cannot redirect what you are reading. */
static int read_all(int fd, unsigned char **out, size_t *out_len)
{
    size_t cap = 8192, len = 0;
    unsigned char *buf = malloc(cap);
    if (!buf) return -1;
    for (;;) {
        if (len == cap) {
            unsigned char *g = realloc(buf, cap * 2);
            if (!g) { free(buf); return -1; }
            buf = g; cap *= 2;
        }
        ssize_t k = read(fd, buf + len, cap - len);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; free(buf); return -1; }
        if (k == 0) break;
        len += (size_t)k;
    }
    *out = buf; *out_len = len;
    return 0;
}

6.4 The system-call boundary: what changes and what does not
