A system call is a controlled transfer into the kernel and back, and almost every surprise at that boundary comes from one fact: it is an expensive, interruptible, blocking operation on a resource you do not own.

What the boundary costs and what it guarantees

Crossing into the kernel costs a fixed overhead on the order of a microsecond or two, plus whatever the operation itself does. The kernel may interrupt any system call, with a signal, so every one must handle an interruption and restart rather than treating it as a failure. A file descriptor is an index into a per-process table, so it is meaningful only in the process that has it and must be passed to a child process explicitly. And the kernel copies data across the boundary for a read or a write, which is why the cost of a large transfer is dominated by copying rather than by the operation. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Restart on interruption, because a signal is not a failure and a partial read interrupted by a signal is normal. Do not assume a descriptor survives a child process, because it does not unless it was inherited or deliberately shared. Do not assume a memory mapping is durable, because a mapping is a cache of a file and the kernel may write it back at a time you do not choose. And Prefer the vectored form when the data is in several places, because it transfers in one crossing rather than several. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Treating an interruption as an error, which truncates a read and produces a file that is mysteriously short. Using a descriptor in a child that did not inherit it, which is a closed-descriptor error at the child's first use. And Assuming a memory mapping means the data is on disk, which is false until it is explicitly written back, and which is the mechanism behind a crash that loses recent writes. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Wrap each system call in a retry-on-interruption helper, so the restart rule is implemented once and every call inherits it rather than half the codebase inheriting it. And Decide deliberately whether durability matters at each write, and pay for the flush explicitly where it does, because the default behaviour is fast and loses the most recent writes on a crash.

#include <errno.h>
#include <fcntl.h>
#include 
#include <unistd.h>

/* Crossing into the kernel costs a fixed overhead, plus whatever the
   operation does. Three facts about the boundary cause nearly every
   surprise:
     - THE KERNEL MAY INTERRUPT ANY CALL with a signal, so every one
       must handle an interruption and restart.
     - A FILE DESCRIPTOR is an index into a per-process table. It is
       meaningful only in the process that has it.
     - THE KERNEL COPIES DATA across the boundary, so the cost of a
       large transfer is dominated by copying.                      */

/* RESTART ON INTERRUPTION. A signal is not a failure, and a read
   interrupted by one has simply transferred fewer bytes. */
static ssize_t read_retry(int fd, void *p, size_t n)
{
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, p, n);
        if (k < 0 && errno == EINTR) continue;   /* retry            */
        return k;
    }
}

/* The same rule for anything that can block: a terminal, a socket, a
   pipe. Without the retry, a program with a signal handler silently
   loses data. */
static int open_retry(const char *path, int flags, mode_t m)
{
    for (;;) {
        int fd = open(path, flags, m);
        if (fd < 0 && errno == EINTR) continue;
        return fd;
    }
}

/* VECTORED TRANSFER: one crossing for several buffers. Use it
   whenever the data is already in more than one place, which is
   common when writing a header followed by a body. */
static int writev_all(int fd, struct iovec *iov, int n)
{
    size_t done = 0;
    int first = 0;
    while (first < n) {
        ssize_t k = writev(fd, iov + first, n - first);
        if (k < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;
            return -1;
        }
        /* writev may consume some iovec entries and part of one, so
           walk forward rather than assuming it stopped at an entry. */
        size_t off = (size_t)k;
        while (first < n && off >= iov[first].iov_len) {
            off -= iov[first].iov_len;
            ++first;
        }
        if (first < n && off) {
            iov[first].iov_base = (char *)iov[first].iov_base + off;
            iov[first].iov_len  -= off;
        }
        done += (size_t)k;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* A DESCRIPTOR DOES NOT SURVIVE A CHILD unless inherited or shared.
   The three flags that matter at exec:
     O_CLOEXEC  on the descriptor: closed automatically at exec
     explicit inheritance in the child's descriptor list
     a deliberate share through the runtime's descriptor API      */
static void spawn_with(const char *prog, int fd)
{
    /* If the child is not supposed to have fd, it should have been
       opened with O_CLOEXEC. Retro-fitting that after the fact is
       the bug: the descriptor is already inheritable and the child
       will keep it open for its whole life. */
    (void)prog; (void)fd;
}

/* A MEMORY MAPPING IS A CACHE OF A FILE, not a durable write. The
   kernel writes it back when it chooses - which may be never, if the
   process dies first. That is the mechanism behind a crash that
   loses the most recent writes, and it is why an explicit flush is
   the durability decision. */
static void durable_append(const char *path, const void *data, size_t n)
{
    int fd = open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND, 0600);
    if (fd < 0) return;
    /* O_APPEND plus a single write is the atomic form: the offset is
       set by the kernel at write time, so two processes appending do
       not interleave. A separate seek-then-write is NOT atomic. */
    (void)write_retry(fd, data, n);
    (void)fsync(fd);          /* the durability decision, explicit */
    (void)close(fd);
}   /* The rule: wrap each system call in a retry helper so the
     restart rule is implemented once, and decide deliberately - per
     write - whether durability matters, because the default is fast
     and loses the most recent writes on a crash. */

/* The kernel may interrupt ANY call with a signal. A signal is not a
   failure: the operation did not complete and the correct response
   is to try again. True of every blocking call. */
static ssize_t read_retry(int fd, void *p, size_t n)
{
    for (;;) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, p, n);
        if (k < 0 && errno == EINTR) continue;
        return k;
    }
}

/* writev crosses the kernel ONCE. Use it whenever the data is
   already in more than one place. */
static int writev_all(int fd, struct iovec *iov, int n)
{
    int first = 0;
    while (first < n) {
        ssize_t k = writev(fd, iov + first, n - first);
        if (k < 0) {
            if (errno == EINTR) continue;
            return -1;
        }
        /* writev may consume some entries AND part of one, so walk
           forward rather than assuming it stopped at an entry. */
        size_t off = (size_t)k;
        while (first < n && off >= iov[first].iov_len) {
            off -= iov[first].iov_len;
            ++first;
        }
        if (first < n && off) {
            iov[first].iov_base = (char *)iov[first].iov_base + off;
            iov[first].iov_len  -= off;
        }
    }
    return 0;
}

/* A descriptor does not survive exec unless inherited or shared.
   O_CLOEXEC is the default you want on everything else: an inherited
   descriptor is a channel you did not intend to open and it keeps
   the other end alive. Retro-fitting the flag afterwards is
   impossible - by then it is already inheritable. */
static int open_log(const char *path)
{
    return open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND | O_CLOEXEC, 0600);
}

/* The pipe that hangs forever: the child keeps its copy of the write
   end, so the parent's read never sees end-of-file. Close it in the
   parent immediately after spawning. That one line is the
   difference between a prompt return and a hung service. */
static void the_hang(int fds[2])
{
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid == 0) { close(fds[0]); run_child(); _exit(0); }
    close(fds[1]);        /* THIS LINE */
    read_to_eof(fds[0]);
    close(fds[0]);
}

/* A memory mapping is a CACHE of a file. The kernel writes it back
   when it chooses, which may be never if the process dies first.
   That is the mechanism behind a crash that loses recent writes. */
static void durable_append(const char *path, const void *data, size_t n)
{
    int fd = open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND | O_CLOEXEC, 0600);
    if (fd < 0) return;
    /* O_APPEND plus a SINGLE write is the atomic form: the offset is
       set by the kernel at write time, so two processes appending do
       not interleave. A separate seek-then-write is not atomic. */
    (void)write(fd, data, n);
    (void)fsync(fd);       /* the durability decision, explicit */
    (void)close(fd);
}

7 · Processes, Execution and Inter-Process Communication

7.1 Processes, forking and the three states7.2 Running another program, and replacing yourself7.3 Talking between processes: pipes, sockets, shared memory7.4 Limits, resources and the environment a program runs in

7.1 Processes, forking and the three states

A process is an address space plus a set of kernel resources, and creating one is a copy followed by a special return value — which is why almost every rule about forking is a rule about what the two copies share.

What a fork actually does

Creating a process duplicates the address space so that the two are indistinguishable to the program at first, and then returns a different value in each: zero in the child and the child's identifier in the parent. Everything the two copies share is a kernel object, not memory, — the same executable, the same open file descriptions with a shared file offset, the same signal disposition, and the same process group. Everything the two copies have separately is everything else, which is the copy-on-write memory and which means the expensive-looking operation is cheap until one of them writes. Then the four rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Do not assume which copy runs first, because neither does, so any logic that depends on the order is a bug that appears on a machine with a different scheduler. Do not use anything that was created before the fork in the child if the parent will also use it, because a lock held at the time of the fork stays held in the child and the child can deadlock on a lock nobody will release. Close descriptors you do not want the child to have, because an inherited descriptor is a channel the parent did not intend to open and which keeps the other end alive. And Be careful with buffered streams, because the buffer is duplicated and both copies will flush it, which is the mechanism behind every duplicated-output bug in a forking server. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A lock created before the fork, which the child inherits held and which the child will wait on forever. A buffered stream not flushed before the fork, which produces the same bytes twice because both copies flush. A shared file offset between parent and child, which means two processes writing to one inherited file description interleave in a way neither asked for. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Do the fork as early as possible and as little as possible in the child, — close descriptors, reset signal handlers, then do the work and exit — because every step between the fork and the work is a step where the child is a near-copy of something it does not want. And Flush before you fork, explicitly, because the rule is easy to forget and the symptom appears far from the cause.

#include <unistd.h>
#include 

/* Creating a process DUPLICATES the address space so the two are
   indistinguishable at first, then returns a DIFFERENT VALUE in
   each: zero in the child, the child's pid in the parent.

   SHARED between the copies: the executable, the open file
     descriptions (INCLUDING THE FILE OFFSET), signal disposition,
     process group.
   SEPARATE: everything else - and the memory is copy-on-write, which
     is why the expensive-looking operation is cheap until one side
     writes. */

/* THE RULES.

   1. DO NOT ASSUME WHICH COPY RUNS FIRST. Neither does. Any logic
      depending on the order is a bug that appears on a machine with
      a different scheduler.
   2. DO NOT USE anything created before the fork in the child if the
      parent will also use it. A lock held at the fork stays held in
      the child, and the child deadlocks on a lock nobody will
      release.
   3. CLOSE descriptors the child should not have. An inherited
      descriptor is a channel you did not intend to open, and it
      keeps the other end alive.
   4. BE CAREFUL WITH BUFFERED STREAMS. The buffer is duplicated and
      BOTH copies flush it. This is every duplicated-output bug in a
      forking server. */

/* The shape to copy: fork, then in the child do as little as
   possible before doing the work and exiting. */
static pid_t spawn_worker(int (*fn)(void *), void *arg)
{
    fflush(NULL);                 /* rule 4, done explicitly       */
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid < 0) return -1;

    if (pid == 0) {
        /* Child. From here on: close, reset, work, exit. Never
           return from here into the parent's code paths. */
        signal(SIGINT,  SIG_DFL);        /* rule 2: undo handlers  */
        signal(SIGTERM, SIG_DFL);
        _exit(fn(arg) == 0 ? 0 : 1);      /* _exit, not exit: no
                                             stdio flush, so no
                                             duplicate buffer      */
    }
    return pid;
}

/* Rule 2 in detail: a mutex held at the time of the fork is held in
   BOTH processes, and the only thread that could release it is not
   in the child. The classic case is a library initialised before
   the fork whose first call takes a lock. */
#include <pthread.h>
static pthread_mutex_t registry_lock = PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
static int *registry;                  /* created before the fork  */

/* A correct library initialises inside a one-time guard that is
   fork-aware, or that simply uses a lock-free path in the child.
   POSIX has pthread_atfork for exactly this: register handlers that
   run in the child, in the parent, or both, around the fork. */

/* Reaping. A child that exits becomes a ZOMBIE until waited for, and
   an unreaped child is a table entry that never goes away. */
static int reap(pid_t pid)
{
    int status;
    while (waitpid(pid, &status, 0) < 0) {
        if (errno != EINTR) return -1;   /* a signal, not a failure */
    }
    if (WIFEXITED(status))   return WEXITSTATUS(status);
    if (WIFSIGNALED(status)) return 128 + WTERMSIG(status);
    return -1;
}

/* Two traps worth naming.
   - waitpid with a pid that is not your child, or a pid you have
     already reaped: both are errors, and the second is common in a
     server that handles the same request twice.
   - A SIGCHLD handler that reaps indiscriminately will steal the
     exit status of a child another thread is waiting for. Collect in
     one place. */

/* The shape to copy. In the child: close, reset, work, exit. */
static pid_t spawn_worker(int (*fn)(void *), void *arg)
{
    fflush(NULL);        /* the buffer is DUPLICATED and BOTH copies
                            flush it, so the same bytes appear
                            twice. This is every duplicated-output
                            bug in a forking server.               */
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid < 0) return -1;

    if (pid == 0) {
        /* Reset anything inherited that the parent will also use: a
           signal handler, a lock, a stdio buffer. */
        signal(SIGINT,  SIG_DFL);
        signal(SIGTERM, SIG_DFL);
        _exit(fn(arg) == 0 ? 0 : 1);
        /* _exit, NOT exit: exit flushes stdio again.            */
    }
    return pid;
}

/* THE LOCK TRAP. A mutex held at the time of the fork is held in
   BOTH processes, and the only thread that could release it is not
   in the child. POSIX has pthread_atfork for exactly this. */
static pthread_mutex_t registry_lock = PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
static int *registry;              /* created before the fork     */

/* Reaping: an exited child stays a ZOMBIE until waited for, and an
   unreaped child is a table entry that never goes away. */
static int reap(pid_t pid)
{
    int status;
    while (waitpid(pid, &status, 0) < 0) {
        if (errno != EINTR) return -1;   /* a signal, not a failure */
    }
    if (WIFEXITED(status))   return WEXITSTATUS(status);
    if (WIFSIGNALED(status)) return 128 + WTERMSIG(status);
    return -1;
}

/* Two traps worth naming:
   - waitpid with a pid you have already reaped is an error, and it
     is common in a server that handles the same request twice.
   - A SIGCHLD handler that reaps indiscriminately STEALS the exit
     status of a child another thread is waiting for. Collect in one
     place: a self-pipe, or a dedicated reaper thread. */

/* Do not assume which copy runs first. Neither does, so logic that
   depends on the order is a bug that appears on a machine with a
   different scheduler and nowhere else. */
static int nondeterministic(void)
{
    int order = 0;
    if (fork() == 0) { order = 1; _exit(0); }
    wait(NULL);
    return order;   /* never read this as the child's value */
}

7.2 Running another program, and replacing yourself

Replacing the current program image is one call, and everything interesting about it is what survives the replacement and what does not.

What survives an exec

The replacement keeps the process identifier, the parent, the process group, the session, and the signal dispositions that were set to be ignored. It loses everything else: the memory, the descriptors marked close-on-exec, the signal handlers, the working directory unless you set it, the resource limits unless you restore them, and the environment unless you pass it. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Mark every descriptor close-on-exec by default, because a descriptor that leaks into an unrelated child program is a security problem in that program and a functional surprise in yours. Construct the argument list as an array, never as a parsed string, because the argument vector is what the program receives and a string you then split has already lost the information about what was quoted. Search the path explicitly, or do not search it at all, because the implicit search consults the current directory on some platforms and is a mechanism by which a file you did not write becomes a program you run. And Handle the difference between "could not execute" and "executed and failed", which are two different outcomes with two different exit conventions. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A descriptor without close-on-exec, which the child inherits and keeps open, so a pipe the child was supposed to use for a status code is instead held open by a long-lived process and the parent waits forever. An argument list built by splitting a string, which turns a filename containing a space into two arguments. And A relative program name with an implicit path search, which means the program you run depends on the current directory — and a file placed there by somebody else decides what runs. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Open every descriptor with close-on-exec and pass the few you need explicitly, which makes the set of things a child can see explicit and reviewable rather than a consequence of the order of your code. And Use an absolute path or an explicit validated path, so the program's identity is a fact you decided rather than a fact about the current directory.

#include <fcntl.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <unistd.h>

/* What SURVIVES the replacement: the pid, the parent, the process
   group, the session, and signal dispositions set to be IGNORED.
   What is LOST: memory, close-on-exec descriptors, signal handlers,
   the working directory, resource limits, and the environment -
   unless you set or pass them again. */

/* Rule 1: CLOSE-ON-EXEC BY DEFAULT. A descriptor that leaks into an
   unrelated child is a security problem in THAT program and a
   functional surprise in yours. */
static void run(const char *prog, char *const argv[], int out_fd)
{
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid == 0) {
        /* Rule 2: the argument list is an ARRAY. Building it by
           splitting a string has already lost the information about
           what was quoted - a filename with a space becomes two
           arguments. */
        dup2(out_fd, STDOUT_FILENO);
        execv(prog, argv);              /* absolute, explicit     */
        _exit(127);                     /* only if it failed      */
    }
}

/* Rule 3: SEARCH THE PATH EXPLICITLY, OR DO NOT SEARCH IT AT ALL.
   The implicit search consults the CURRENT DIRECTORY on some
   platforms, which is a mechanism by which a file you did not write
   becomes a program you run. This function does the search itself,
   refuses relative results, and is therefore auditable. */
#include 
static int resolve_program(char *out, size_t cap, const char *name)
{
    const char *path = getenv("PATH");
    if (!path || !*path) return -1;
    const char *dirs[] = { "/usr/local/bin", "/usr/bin", "/bin", NULL };

    for (const char **d = dirs; *d; ++d) {
        int n = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/%s", *d, name);
        if (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= cap) continue;
        if (access(out, X_OK) == 0) return 0;
    }
    (void)path;
    return -1;
}

/* Rule 4: "COULD NOT EXECUTE" AND "EXECUTED AND FAILED" ARE TWO
   DIFFERENT OUTCOMES. The first is reported by the exec call
   returning; the second by the exit status of a process that ran. */
enum { EXIT_OK = 0, EXIT_FAILED = 1, EXIT_CANNOT_EXEC = 126, EXIT_NOT_FOUND = 127 };

static int exec_checked(const char *prog, char *const argv[])
{
    char path[4096];
    if (resolve_program(path, sizeof path, prog) != 0) return EXIT_NOT_FOUND;
    execv(path, argv);
    return EXIT_CANNOT_EXEC;      /* reached only if exec failed  */
}   /* The two disciplines, restated because they are the whole
     chapter: open every descriptor close-on-exec and pass the few
     you need explicitly, so the set of things a child can see is a
     list somebody reviewed rather than a consequence of the order
     your code happens to be written in. And resolve the program to
     an absolute path yourself, so what runs is a fact you decided
     rather than a fact about the current directory. */

/* The pipe that hangs forever, which is the classic symptom of a
   missing close-on-exec:

       parent: pipe(p); spawn(child, /* p[1] */);
       child : ... ; exit(0);
       parent: read(p[0])            <- blocks

   The child's copy of p[1] is open. The parent's read therefore has
   a WRITER still alive as long as the child does, and a pipe with a
   writer never reports end-of-file. Close the write end in the
   parent after spawning. This one line is the difference between a
   prompt return and a hung service. */

/* Search the path EXPLICITLY, or do not search it at all. The
   implicit search consults the current directory on some systems,
   which is a mechanism by which a file you did not write becomes a
   program you run. */
static int resolve_program(char *out, size_t cap, const char *name)
{
    static const char *const dirs[] = {
        "/usr/local/bin", "/usr/bin", "/bin", NULL
    };
    for (const char *const *d = dirs; *d; ++d) {
        int n = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/%s", *d, name);
        if (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= cap) continue;
        if (access(out, X_OK) == 0) return 0;
    }
    return -1;
}

/* The argument list is an ARRAY. Building it by splitting a string
   has already lost the information about what was quoted: a filename
   containing a space becomes two arguments. */
static int run(const char *prog, char *const argv[], int out_fd)
{
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid < 0) return -1;
    if (pid == 0) {
        if (dup2(out_fd, STDOUT_FILENO) < 0) _exit(126);
        /* close the original too, or the pipe's reader never sees
           EOF and the parent waits forever */
        if (out_fd > STDERR_FILENO) close(out_fd);
        char path[4096];
        if (resolve_program(path, sizeof path, argv[0]) != 0) _exit(127);
        execv(path, argv);
        _exit(126);              /* only reached if exec failed */
    }
    return pid;
}

/* "Could not execute" and "executed and failed" are TWO DIFFERENT
   OUTCOMES. The first is reported by execv returning; the second by
   the exit status of a process that ran. Conflating them is why a
   missing binary and a failing program produce the same message. */
enum {
    EXIT_OK          = 0,
    EXIT_FAILED      = 1,
    EXIT_CANNOT_EXEC = 126,   /* found but not executable */
    EXIT_NOT_FOUND   = 127    /* not found                 */
};

/* Mark every descriptor close-on-exec BY DEFAULT, and pass the few a
   child needs explicitly, so the set of things a child can see is a
   list somebody reviewed rather than a consequence of code order. */
static int open_log(const char *path)
{
    return open(path, O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_APPEND | O_CLOEXEC, 0600);
}

7.3 Talking between processes: pipes, sockets, shared memory

Inter-process communication is a choice between three mechanisms with genuinely different performance and different failure modes, and the choice is mostly about who owns the data and how much of it there is.

Three mechanisms, three cost models

A pipe is a kernel buffer with two ends. Writing more than the buffer holds blocks until a reader consumes it, which makes it a natural flow-control mechanism and an accidental deadlock if nobody reads. A socket pair is the same idea with the full socket machinery, which adds message boundaries, the ability to connect between unrelated processes, and multiplexing. Shared memory is a mapping, so a read or a write between processes costs about what it costs between functions, and every synchronisation becomes your problem. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Close the ends you do not use in every process, because the mechanism's end-of-file detection depends on all copies being closed and a stray copy is a hang. Design the message framing, — length prefix, terminator, or fixed size — because a byte stream has no boundaries and a reader that assumes one will desynchronise on the first partial read. For shared memory, put the synchronisation in the shared region, because a lock in the private memory of one process protects nothing. And Be explicit about who owns each piece of data, because a shared region with two writers and no ownership rule is a data race with extra steps. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A pipe whose read end is never drained, which blocks the writer and therefore blocks whatever else that writer was doing. A message protocol with no length prefix, which desynchronises permanently on a partial read and produces garbage for every subsequent message. And Shared memory with a lock that is not in the shared region, which protects nothing at all and gives the false confidence of a mutex. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Use a length-prefixed message and a single read loop that assembles whole messages, so partial reads are a normal case handled once rather than an assumption embedded in every caller. And Prefer a pipe or a socket until you have measured that the copy is the bottleneck, because shared memory moves the problem rather than solving it: the copy is gone and the synchronisation is yours, and most programs do not need to pay that.

#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>
#include 
#include <unistd.h>

/* PIPE        a kernel buffer with two ends. Writing more than it
                holds BLOCKS until a reader consumes it - a natural
                flow control, and an accidental deadlock if nobody
                reads.
   SOCKET PAIR the same idea with the full socket machinery: message
                boundaries, connection between unrelated processes,
                multiplexing.
   SHARED MEM   a mapping. A read or write between processes costs
                about what it costs between functions - and every
                SYNCHRONISATION becomes your problem.              */

/* Rule: CLOSE THE ENDS YOU DO NOT USE, IN EVERY PROCESS. Every
   mechanism's end-of-file detection depends on all copies being
   closed, and one stray copy is a hang rather than a bug. */

/* FRAMING. A byte stream has no boundaries. Choose a length prefix,
   a terminator, or a fixed size - and assume the reader will see
   partial messages, because it will. */
typedef struct { uint32_t len; } frame_t;

static int read_frame(int fd, void *buf, size_t cap, size_t *out)
{
    uint8_t hdr[4];
    for (size_t got = 0; got < 4; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, hdr + got, 4 - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return got ? -1 : 0;      /* clean or short end */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    uint32_t len;
    memcpy(&len, hdr, 4);                     /* fix your endianness  */
    if (len > cap) return -1;                 /* never trust it       */

    for (size_t got = 0; got < len; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, (uint8_t *)buf + got, len - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return -1;                /* short frame         */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    *out = len;
    return 1;
}

/* SHARED MEMORY. The copy disappears and the synchronisation is
   yours. Note where the lock lives: IN THE SHARED REGION. A mutex
   in one process's private memory protects nothing and gives the
   false confidence of having synchronised. */
struct region {
    /* a real inter-process lock lives here; a pthread_mutex_t in
       private memory would be a mutex over nothing */
    volatile int sequence;
    volatile int slot;
    char          data[4096];
};

static struct region *region_open(void)
{
    int fd = shm_open("/acme/region", O_CREAT | O_RDWR, 0600);
    if (fd < 0) return NULL;
    if (ftruncate(fd, sizeof(struct region)) != 0) { close(fd); return NULL; }
    void *p = mmap(NULL, sizeof(struct region), PROT_READ | PROT_WRITE,
                   MAP_SHARED, fd, 0);
    close(fd);                       /* the mapping keeps it alive  */
    return p == MAP_FAILED ? NULL : p;
}

/* Who OWNS each field is a design decision, not a detail: two
   writers with no ownership rule is a data race with extra steps. */
static void region_publish(struct region *r, const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s) + 1;
    if (n > sizeof r->data) n = sizeof r->data;
    memcpy((void *)r->data, s, n);    /* copy into the shared slot  */
    __atomic_store_n(&r->sequence, r->sequence + 1, __ATOMIC_RELEASE);
}

static int region_wait(struct region *r, int seen, int spins)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < spins; ++i) {
        if (__atomic_load_n(&r->sequence, __ATOMIC_ACQUIRE) != seen) return 0;
        struct timespec ts = { 0, 1000000L };
        nanosleep(&ts, NULL);
    }
    return -1;
}   /* The two disciplines: a length-prefixed message plus ONE read
     loop that assembles whole messages, so partial reads are a
     normal case handled in a single place; and prefer a pipe or a
     socket until you have MEASURED that the copy is the bottleneck,
     because shared memory moves the problem rather than solving it
     - most programs do not need to pay for it. */

enum { MAX_FRAME = 4096 };

typedef struct { uint32_t len, type; } frame_hdr;

/* A length prefix. Never trust the length from the wire. */
static int read_frame(int fd, frame_hdr *hdr, unsigned char *buf, size_t cap)
{
    uint8_t h[8];
    for (size_t got = 0; got < sizeof h; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, h + got, sizeof h - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return got ? -1 : 0;   /* clean or short end */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    memcpy(&hdr->len,  h,     4);
    memcpy(&hdr->type, h + 4, 4);
    if (hdr->len > cap) return -1;

    for (size_t got = 0; got < hdr->len; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, buf + got, hdr->len - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return -1;             /* short frame */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    return 1;
}

/* The assembling loop, which is the shape to copy: read whatever
   arrived, append it, and consume whole messages while one is
   buffered. Partial reads become a normal case handled in ONE place
   instead of an assumption in every caller. */
struct inbox { unsigned char buf[8192]; size_t len; };

static int feed(struct inbox *ib, const unsigned char *data, size_t n)
{
    if (ib->len + n > sizeof ib->buf) return -1;
    memcpy(ib->buf + ib->len, data, n);
    ib->len += n;

    for (;;) {
        if (ib->len < 4) return 0;                 /* need more  */
        uint32_t len;
        memcpy(&len, ib->buf, 4);
        if (len > MAX_FRAME) return -1;            /* malformed */
        if (ib->len < 4 + len) return 0;           /* need more  */
        handle_message(ib->buf + 4, len);
        memmove(ib->buf, ib->buf + 4 + len, ib->len - (4 + len));
        ib->len -= 4 + len;
    }
}

/* Shared memory MOVES THE PROBLEM: the copy disappears and the
   synchronisation becomes yours. The lock must live IN the shared
   region - a mutex in private memory protects nothing and gives the
   false confidence of having synchronised. */
struct region { volatile int sequence; char data[MAX_FRAME]; };

static void region_publish(struct region *r, const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s) + 1;
    if (n > sizeof r->data) n = sizeof r->data;
    memcpy((void *)r->data, s, n);
    __atomic_store_n(&r->sequence, r->sequence + 1, __ATOMIC_RELEASE);
}

7.4 Limits, resources and the environment a program runs in

A program inherits a set of limits, a working directory, an environment and a set of open files from whoever started it, and most surprises in this area are a program assuming values it never checked.

The inherited state

A program starts with a working directory, an environment block, an open file limit, a memory limit, a user and group identity, a process group and a session, and an inherited set of descriptors. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Never trust the working directory, because a program started from a shell has whatever directory that shell had, and a relative path resolves against it. Treat the environment as input, — every value in it is a string chosen by whoever started you, so parse it, validate it and reject what you cannot use rather than trusting a well-formed value. Handle a limit being lower than you assumed, because the limit is set by the environment and a program that opens a thousand descriptors and assumes it can will fail on the machine where the limit is low. And Be explicit about what you inherit,, because the default for every one of these is whatever was already true, and the default is not a decision. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A relative path resolved against an unexpected directory, which is the mechanism behind a program that works from a shell and not from a service manager. An environment value read without validation, which becomes a format string or a path that was never checked. And A file limit assumed rather than raised, which produces a failure at a point in the program where the cause is a few hundred lines earlier. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Resolve every path in your program to an absolute one at startup, from a configuration value that is itself validated, so that nothing downstream depends on where the program was started from. And Read the inherited state at startup, validate it, and fail with one clear message — a program that reports "the environment variable X must be an integer between 1 and 100" is debuggable and one that misbehaves later is not.

#include <limits.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <unistd.h>

/* What a program inherits from whoever started it:
     working directory, environment block, open-file limit,
     memory limit, user and group identity, process group, session,
     and whatever descriptors were open.
   Every DEFAULT here is whatever was already true, and a default is
   not a decision. */

/* NEVER TRUST THE WORKING DIRECTORY. A program started from a shell
   has whatever directory that shell had; a service manager may use
   the root directory. The same program then fails on one machine and
   works on another. */
static int resolve_absolute(const char *in, char *out, size_t cap)
{
    if (in[0] == '/') {
        if (strlen(in) >= cap) return -1;
        strcpy(out, in);
        return 0;
    }
    char cwd[PATH_MAX];
    if (!getcwd(cwd, sizeof cwd)) return -1;
    int n = snprintf(out, cap, "%s/%s", cwd, in);
    return (n < 0 || (size_t)n >= cap) ? -1 : 0;
}

/* TREAT THE ENVIRONMENT AS INPUT. Every value is a STRING chosen by
   whoever started you. Parse, validate, and reject what you cannot
   use. The default of trusting a well-formed value is how a format
   string and a path nobody checked get into a program. */
static int env_port(const char *name, unsigned *out)
{
    const char *s = getenv(name);
    if (!s || !*s) return -1;
    char *end;
    long v = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s || *end != '') return -1;      /* not a number    */
    if (v < 1 || v > 65535) return -1;            /* out of range    */
    *out = (unsigned)v;
    return 0;
}

/* HANDLE A LIMIT BEING LOWER THAN YOU ASSUMED. The limit is set by
   the environment, so a program that opens a thousand descriptors and
   assumes it can will fail on the machine where the limit is low -
   and the failure appears at a point hundreds of lines from the
   cause. */
static int fd_budget(void)
{
    long lim = sysconf(_SC_OPEN_MAX);
    if (lim < 0) lim = 256;              /* unknown: assume small  */
    /* Raise the soft limit toward the hard limit; do not fail if it
       is refused - carry on with what you have. */
    struct rlimit rl;
    if (getrlimit(RLIMIT_NOFILE, &rl) == 0 && rl.rlim_cur < rl.rlim_max)
        rl.rlim_cur = rl.rlim_max;
    (void)setrlimit(RLIMIT_NOFILE, &rl);
    return lim > 65536 ? 65536 : (int)lim;
}

/* Report the problem ONCE, in a message a person can act on. A
   program that says "the environment variable X must be an integer
   between 1 and 100" is debuggable; one that misbehaves three
   minutes later is not. */
int main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    unsigned port;
    if (env_port("ACME_PORT", &port) != 0) {
        fprintf(stderr, "ACME_PORT must be an integer between 1 and 65535
");
        return 2;
    }
    char root[PATH_MAX];
    if (resolve_absolute(argc > 1 ? argv[1] : ".", root, sizeof root) != 0) {
        perror("resolve");
        return 2;
    }
    (void)fd_budget();
    return 0;
}   /* Two disciplines: resolve every path to an absolute one at
     startup, from a validated configuration value, so nothing
     downstream depends on where the program was started from; and
     read the inherited state once, validate it, and fail with a
     single clear message. */

8 · Signals, Timers and Concurrency

8.1 Signals are a mechanism, not an event queue8.2 Timers, sleeping and the clock you meant8.3 Threads: what the library guarantees and what you must add8.4 Atomics: the one place you need them

8.1 Signals are a mechanism, not an event queue

A signal is a software interrupt delivered to a process, and the single most important fact about them is that the handler runs in the middle of whatever the process was doing — which makes almost everything you might want to do in a handler unsafe.

What a signal actually is

A signal is delivered by the kernel at a point it chooses, and the handler runs on some thread of the process, interrupting the code that was there. The set of things you may safely do in a handler is very short: set a flag of a type the compiler treats as special, read or write an atomic object, or call a function that is explicitly safe. Everything else — allocation, anything that takes a lock you might already hold, anything that can block — is a deadlock waiting for a coincidence. A signal has no argument, no return value and no queue, so several instances of the same signal collapse into one delivery, which means "count the interrupts" does not work and any state you want to record must be recorded atomically. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. The handler sets a flag; the main loop acts on it, which is the pattern that makes signals usable at all. Make every flag that a handler touches volatile, so the compiler does not cache it in a register across the point where the handler ran, and so the compiler emits the access in the order you need. Block the signals you intend to handle around any non-reentrant region, so a handler cannot arrive in the middle of it. And Decide what happens for every signal you do not handle, because the default for several is to terminate and for several is to ignore, and the difference is a behaviour your program has without having chosen it. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Calling a non-reentrant function in a handler, which corrupts the library's internal state if the signal arrived inside a call to that same function. A handler that does work, which means the work runs at an arbitrary point in the program's logic with a fraction of the program's state consistent. And A signal mask left in place around a blocking operation, which turns a signal that should interrupt a wait into a wait that cannot be interrupted. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Make every handler three lines long and give it no arguments beyond the signal number, so that the only thing that can go wrong inside it is a store to a flag, and so that reading the handler tells you exactly what the signal does. And Use the self-pipe trick for the cases where a flag is not enough — write one byte to a pipe from the handler and read it in the main loop — because it works with the ordinary blocking primitives rather than requiring you to restructure the loop around them.

#include <signal.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <unistd.h>

/* A signal is delivered by the KERNEL at a point IT CHOOSES, and the
   handler runs on some thread, interrupting whatever was there.

   WHAT YOU MAY DO IN A HANDLER:
     - set a flag of the special type the compiler understands
     - read or write an atomic object
     - call a function explicitly documented as safe
   Everything else - allocation, anything taking a lock you might
   already hold, anything that can block - is a deadlock waiting for
   a coincidence. */

/* A SIGNAL HAS NO ARGUMENT, no return value and NO QUEUE. Several
   instances of the same signal collapse into one delivery, so
   "count the interrupts" does not work and any state you want to
   record must be recorded atomically. */

static volatile sig_atomic_t got_term = 0;   /* the right type      */
static volatile sig_atomic_t got_int  = 0;

static void on_term(int sig) { (void)sig; got_term = 1; }
static void on_int(int sig)  { (void)sig; got_int  = 1; }

/* THE HANDLER SETS A FLAG; THE MAIN LOOP ACTS ON IT. That is the
   pattern that makes signals usable at all. */
static void install(void)
{
    struct sigaction sa;
    memset(&sa, 0, sizeof sa);
    sa.sa_handler = on_term;
    sigemptyset(&sa.sa_mask);
    /* NO SA_RESTART: we WANT an interruptible call to return an
       error, so the main loop gets a chance to notice. */
    sigaction(SIGTERM, &sa, NULL);
    sigaction(SIGINT,  &sa, NULL);
    /* What you do NOT install has a default, and for several signals
       the default is to terminate and for several it is to ignore.
       That difference is behaviour your program has without having
       chosen it - so decide for each one. */
    signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN);   /* else a closed pipe kills you    */
}

static int loop(void)
{
    for (;;) {
        if (got_term) { shutdown_cleanly(); return 0; }
        if (got_int)  { shutdown_cleanly(); return 130; }
        do_one_unit_of_work();
    }
}   /* volatile matters: without it the compiler may cache the flag
     in a register across the point where the handler ran, and the
     loop never notices. */

/* BLOCK THE SIGNAL around any non-reentrant region, so a handler
   cannot arrive in the middle of it. */
#include <setjmp.h>
static sigjmp_buf escape;
static void on_fatal(int sig)
{
    siglongjmp(escape, sig);
}

/* THE SELF-PIPE TRICK, for when a flag is not enough: write one
   byte from the handler, read it in the main loop. This works with
   the ordinary blocking primitives rather than requiring the loop
   to be restructured around non-blocking calls. */
static int wake_pipe[2] = { -1, -1 };

static void on_wakeup(int sig)
{
    (void)sig;
    unsigned char b = 1;
    ssize_t n = write(wake_pipe[1], &b, 1);   /* async-signal-safe */
    (void)n;                                   /* save the errno    */
}

static int install_wakeup(void)
{
    if (pipe(wake_pipe) != 0) return -1;
    struct sigaction sa;
    memset(&sa, 0, sizeof sa);
    sa.sa_handler = on_wakeup;
    sigemptyset(&sa.sa_mask);
    sigaction(SIGCHLD, &sa, NULL);
    return 0;
}

/* The three failures, and the discipline that prevents all three:
   a handler that CALLS A NON-REENTRANT FUNCTION corrupts the
   library's state if the signal arrived inside a call to that same
   function; a handler that DOES WORK runs it at an arbitrary point
   with a fraction of the program consistent; and a signal mask left
   in place around a blocking operation turns a signal that should
   interrupt a wait into a wait that cannot be interrupted.

   So: three lines, one argument, one store - and read, poll and act
   in the loop. */

/* volatile sig_atomic_t is NOT "volatile int". sig_atomic_t is the
   only type the standard guarantees can be accessed from a handler,
   and it is what stops the compiler caching the flag in a register
   across the point where the handler ran. */
static volatile sig_atomic_t got_term = 0;

static void on_term(int sig) { (void)sig; got_term = 1; }

static void install(void)
{
    struct sigaction sa;
    memset(&sa, 0, sizeof sa);
    sa.sa_handler = on_term;
    sigemptyset(&sa.sa_mask);
    /* NO SA_RESTART: we WANT an interruptible call to return an
       error so the loop can notice the flag. With SA_RESTART the
       read is retried internally and the flag is never examined. */
    sigaction(SIGTERM, &sa, NULL);

    /* What you do NOT install has a default, and for several signals
       that default is to terminate and for several it is to ignore.
       That difference is behaviour your program has without having
       chosen it - so decide for each one. */
    signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN);   /* else a closed pipe kills you */
}

static int loop(void)
{
    for (;;) {
        if (got_term) { shutdown_cleanly(); return 0; }
        do_one_unit_of_work();
    }
}

/* The self-pipe trick, for when a flag is not enough: write one byte
   from the handler and read it in the main loop. It works with the
   ordinary blocking primitives instead of requiring the loop to be
   restructured around non-blocking calls. */
static int wake_pipe[2] = { -1, -1 };

static void on_wakeup(int sig)
{
    (void)sig;
    unsigned char b = 1;
    ssize_t n = write(wake_pipe[1], &b, 1);   /* async-signal-safe */
    (void)n;
}

/* A signal has NO QUEUE. Several instances collapse into one
   delivery, so "count the interrupts" does not work and any state
   you record must be recorded atomically. */

/* Block the signal around any region a handler must not interrupt. */
static int run_critical(void)
{
    sigset_t block, prev;
    sigemptyset(&block);
    sigaddset(&block, SIGTERM);
    sigprocmask(SIG_BLOCK, &block, &prev);
    update_invariant_held_together();
    sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, &prev, NULL);
    return 0;
}

8.2 Timers, sleeping and the clock you meant

Sleeping looks trivial and is not, because a sleep is a promise about a future instant made with a clock that may not still be the right clock by the time it fires.

Three clock choices and what each is for

A timer can be measured against three clocks and they answer different questions. The real-time clock says what time it is and can jump, so it is right for a deadline expressed in wall-clock terms. The monotonic clock cannot jump and is right for a duration. The process and thread CPU-time clocks measure consumed processor time, so a program that blocks for ten seconds and consumes one second of processor time sees one second — which is exactly what a rate limiter wants. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Sleep for a duration, never for a deadline, because a duration computed from the monotonic clock is correct regardless of what the wall clock does. Handle interruption by recomputing the remaining time, because a signal that returns you early must not shorten the sleep — the mechanism behind a service that spins when it is under load and the interrupts keep arriving. Never busy-wait a wall-clock deadline, because the wall clock can move backwards and the wait then never ends. And Remember that a sleep is not a synchronisation primitive, because two processes sleeping for the same interval wake within scheduling granularity of each other, not in order. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A sleep implemented as a loop on a busy read, which burns the processor you were trying not to use. An interrupted sleep treated as a completed one, which shortens every wait by the time a signal took. And A monotonic deadline stored in a wall-clock variable, which is a bug on any machine whose clock is adjusted, and which looks correct on every machine whose clock never moves. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Have one sleep function for the whole program, which computes the remaining time from a monotonic deadline and returns whether it slept the whole interval, so the interruption rule exists once. And Express every wait as a condition rather than a duration, so that "wait until the child exits" is a wait that ends for the right reason rather than a sleep that happens to be long enough.

#include <errno.h>
#include <time.h>

/* Three clocks, three questions.
   REAL TIME    what time it is. CAN JUMP. Right for a deadline in
                wall-clock terms.
   MONOTONIC    never jumps. Right for a DURATION.
   CPU TIME     processor time consumed - a program that blocks for
                ten seconds and burns one second sees one second.
                Exactly what a rate limiter wants. */

/* SLEEP FOR A DURATION, NEVER FOR A DEADLINE. A duration computed
   from the monotonic clock is correct whatever the wall clock does. */
static uint64_t mono_ns(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000ull + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec;
}

static uint64_t mono_ms(void) { return mono_ns() / 1000000ull; }

/* HANDLE INTERRUPTION BY RECOMPUTING THE REMAINING TIME. A signal
   that returns you early must not SHORTEN the sleep - this is the
   mechanism behind a service that spins when it is under load and
   interrupts keep arriving. */
static void sleep_ms(unsigned ms)
{
    uint64_t deadline = mono_ms() + ms;
    struct timespec ts = { (time_t)(ms / 1000), (long)(ms % 1000) * 1000000L };
    while (nanosleep(&ts, &ts) != 0 && errno == EINTR) {
        /* ts now holds the REMAINING time. Return to the kernel with
           it; do not restart with the original request. */
    }
    (void)deadline;
}

/* The rule this implements: never busy-wait a wall-clock deadline.
   The wall clock can move backwards and the wait then never ends. */
static int sleep_until_wall(time_t when)
{
    for (;;) {
        time_t now = time(NULL);
        if (now >= when) return 0;
        time_t left = when - now;
        sleep_ms(left > 1000 ? 1000u : (unsigned)left);   /* re-read  */
    }
}

/* A SLEEP IS NOT A SYNCHRONISATION PRIMITIVE. Two processes
   sleeping for the same interval wake within scheduling granularity
   of each other - not in order, and not together. Anything that
   needs an order needs a lock or a pipe. */

static int until_ready(int fd, int timeout_ms)
{
    /* Express a wait as a CONDITION, not a duration: wait for the
       child to become ready, however long that takes, up to a bound.
       A sleep long enough to usually work is a bug that usually
       works. */
    uint64_t deadline = mono_ms() + (uint64_t)timeout_ms;
    for (;;) {
        char c;
        ssize_t n = read(fd, &c, 1);
        if (n == 1) return 0;
        if (n < 0 && errno != EINTR) return -1;
        if (mono_ms() > deadline) return -1;
    }
}

/* Two disciplines, restated because they are the whole chapter: have
   ONE sleep function for the program, computing the remaining time
   from a monotonic deadline and reporting whether it slept the whole
   interval, so the interruption rule exists once; and express every
   wait as a CONDITION rather than a duration, so a wait ends for the
   right reason rather than being long enough to usually be right. */

/* MONOTONIC: never jumps. The only correct source for an interval. */
static uint64_t mono_ns(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    /* CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW where available: not slewed by NTP, so an
       adjustment cannot shorten a measurement at all. */
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW, &ts);
    return (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000ull + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec;
}

/* CPU TIME: processor time consumed. A program that blocks for ten
   seconds and burns one second of processor time sees one second -
   which is exactly what a rate limiter wants. */
static uint64_t cpu_ns(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID, &ts);
    return (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000ull + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec;
}

/* Handle interruption by RECOMPUTING the remaining time. A signal
   that returns you early must not shorten the sleep. */
static void sleep_ms(unsigned ms)
{
    struct timespec ts = { (time_t)(ms / 1000),
                           (long)(ms % 1000) * 1000000L };
    while (nanosleep(&ts, &ts) != 0) {
        if (errno != EINTR) return;
        /* ts now holds the REMAINING time. Return it to the kernel.
           Restarting with the original oversleeps; exiting early
           undersleeps, which is the bug a loaded machine turns into
           a busy loop. */
    }
}

/* Never busy-wait a wall-clock deadline: the wall clock can move
   backwards and the wait then never ends. */
static int sleep_until_wall(time_t when)
{
    for (;;) {
        time_t now = time(NULL);
        if (now >= when) return 0;
        time_t left = when - now;
        sleep_ms(left > 1000 ? 1000u : (unsigned)left);   /* re-read */
    }
}

/* A sleep is not a synchronisation primitive. Two processes sleeping
   the same interval wake within scheduling granularity - not in
   order, and not together. Express a wait as a CONDITION. */
static int wait_for_fd(int fd, int timeout_ms)
{
    uint64_t deadline = mono_ns() / 1000000ull + (uint64_t)timeout_ms;
    for (;;) {
        struct pollfd p = { fd, POLLIN, 0 };
        int left = (int)(deadline - mono_ns() / 1000000ull);
        if (left <= 0) return -1;
        int rc = poll(&p, 1, left);
        if (rc < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (rc > 0) return 0;
    }
}

8.3 Threads: what the library guarantees and what you must add

The threading interface in C is a small set of primitives over an operating system's scheduler, and the gap between what it guarantees and what a program needs is exactly where concurrency bugs live.

Three primitives and a lifetime problem

A mutex serialises access to a shared object; a condition variable lets a thread wait for a condition without a mutex of its own; and a read-write lock lets several readers share while a writer is exclusive. Then the single hardest problem in the whole interface, which is lifetime: a thread that has been asked to stop may be blocked in any of the three, so asking it to stop and then waiting for it is a deadlock unless there is a path out of every wait. The solution is a shutdown protocol — a flag plus a condition, so the waiter can wake up, see the flag and leave. Then the four rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Never destroy a mutex or a condition while a thread could be waiting on it, because the wait is on memory that no longer exists and the crash may be in another thread. Always have a timeout or a condition on every wait, because a wait with no exit is a program that cannot be stopped. Prefer signalling a condition over holding a mutex while doing work, because holding a mutex across anything slow is the mechanism behind a convoy. And Make the lock order global, — a stated hierarchy, taken in one order everywhere — because two locks taken in different orders in different functions is a deadlock that appears only under contention. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Joining a thread that is blocked forever, which is the shutdown deadlock. Unlocking a mutex from a different thread than the one that locked it, which is undefined behaviour in the interface and a corruption in practice. And A condition wait without re-checking the condition in a loop, which is wrong because a signal can arrive between the check and the wait — the classic spurious and lost-wakeup mechanism. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Write the shutdown protocol before the worker loop, because a worker that cannot be stopped cannot be tested and cannot be upgraded. And Keep the lock scope as narrow as the invariant requires, — copy what you need under the lock, do the work outside it, and copy the result back — because the difference between that and holding the lock is the difference between one thread's latency and all of them.

#include <pthread.h>
#include <stdbool.h>

/* THE HARDEST PROBLEM IS LIFETIME. A thread asked to stop may be
   blocked in a mutex, a condition or a join. Asking it to stop and
   then waiting for it is a DEADLOCK unless there is a path out of
   every wait - so the solution is a shutdown protocol: a flag plus
   a condition, so the waiter wakes, sees the flag and leaves. */

typedef struct {
    pthread_mutex_t  m;
    pthread_cond_t   cv;
    bool             stopping;
    /* shared state protected by m */
    unsigned long    completed;
} pool;

/* Rule 1: NEVER DESTROY a mutex or condition while a thread could
   be waiting on it. The wait is on memory that no longer exists and
   the crash appears in another thread. */

/* Rule 2: EVERY WAIT HAS AN EXIT. A wait with no exit is a program
   that cannot be stopped, upgraded or debugged. */

static void worker(pthread_t self, pool *p)
{
    (void)self;
    for (;;) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);

        /* Rule 3: WAIT IN A LOOP, RE-CHECKING THE CONDITION. A
           signal can arrive between the check and the wait, and the
           condition can already be false again by the time you wake.
           One check is a lost wakeup. */
        while (!p->stopping && !have_work(p))
            pthread_cond_wait(&p->cv, &p->m);   /* unlocks, waits,
                                                   relocks on exit */

        if (p->stopping) { pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m); return; }
        /* ... do the work ... */
        ++p->completed;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);
    }
}

/* Rule 4: A GLOBAL LOCK ORDER. Two locks taken in different orders
   in different functions is a deadlock that appears only under
   contention, which means it appears in production and never in
   the test that was written to reproduce the other bug. */
enum { LOCK_STATE = 0, LOCK_LOG = 1, LOCK_COUNT = 2 };
static pthread_mutex_t locks[LOCK_COUNT];

static void take(int a, int b)
{
    /* ALWAYS in increasing order. If a > b, the pair was taken in
       the wrong order by the caller: fail loudly in debug, or swap
       and document - but never let it through silently. */
    if (a > b) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }
    pthread_mutex_lock(&locks[a]);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&locks[b]);
}
static void drop(int a, int b) { if (a > b) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&locks[b]); pthread_mutex_unlock(&locks[a]); }

/* KEEP THE LOCK SCOPE AS NARROW AS THE INVARIANT REQUIRES. Copy
   what you need under the lock, do the work outside it, copy the
   result back. The difference between that and holding the lock is
   the difference between one thread's latency and all of them. */
static void enqueue(pool *p, item it)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);
    queue_push(&p->q, it);        /* short: a few assignments      */
    pthread_cond_signal(&p->cv);  /* while still holding, so the     */
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);  /* waiter cannot miss it          */
}

/* Unlocking a mutex from a DIFFERENT THREAD than the one that locked
   it is undefined behaviour in the interface and a corruption in
   practice - it is also the fastest way to turn a working program
   into one that fails only on a machine with more than one core. */

/* The discipline that makes all of this testable: WRITE THE SHUTDOWN
   PROTOCOL BEFORE THE WORKER LOOP. A worker that cannot be stopped
   cannot be tested, cannot be upgraded and cannot be debugged, and
   every one of those is discovered at the worst possible time. */

typedef struct {
    pthread_mutex_t  m;
    pthread_cond_t   cv;
    bool             stopping;
    uint64_t         completed;   /* protected by m */
} pool;

/* The hardest problem is LIFETIME. A thread asked to stop may be
   blocked in a mutex, a condition or a join, so asking it to stop
   and then waiting for it is a DEADLOCK unless every wait has a
   path out. This protocol is that path. */
static void worker(pool *p)
{
    for (;;) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);

        /* Wait in a LOOP, re-checking the condition. A signal can
           arrive between the check and the wait, and the condition
           can be false again by the time you wake. One check is a
           lost wakeup. */
        while (!p->stopping && !have_work(p))
            pthread_cond_wait(&p->cv, &p->m);  /* unlock, wait,
                                                    relock         */

        if (p->stopping) { pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m); return; }
        ++p->completed;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);
    }
}

static void pool_stop(pool *p)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);
    p->stopping = true;
    pthread_cond_broadcast(&p->cv);   /* ALL waiters, not one: every
                                          worker must leave, not
                                          just the next to wake   */
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);
}

/* A global lock ORDER. Two locks taken in different orders in
   different functions is a deadlock that appears only under
   contention - which means in production and never in the test
   written to reproduce the other bug. */
enum { LOCK_STATE = 0, LOCK_LOG = 1 };
static pthread_mutex_t locks[2];

static void take(int a, int b)
{
    if (a > b) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }   /* ALWAYS increasing */
    pthread_mutex_lock(&locks[a]);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&locks[b]);
}

/* Keep the critical section to the INVARIANT: copy under the lock,
   work outside it, write back. Holding a lock across a slow call
   serialises every reader behind it, and a convoy is how one slow
   dependency becomes a whole service being slow. */
static void refresh(pool *p, const char *key)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);
    entry *e = p->index[key];                  /* short              */
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);
    entry *loaded = slow_network_fetch(key);   /* NO LOCK HELD      */
    pthread_mutex_lock(&p->m);
    p->index[key] = loaded;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&p->m);
}

8.4 Atomics: the one place you need them

An atomic operation is one that no other thread can observe halfway through, and in C the compiler-level primitives cover a surprisingly small set of cases — but the set that matters is exactly the set where the alternative is a data race.

What an atomic buys, and what it does not

There are two separate hazards in a concurrent program and they need separate solutions. A data race is two threads accessing the same memory with at least one writing it and no synchronisation between them, which is undefined behaviour — not "sometimes wrong", undefined, which is why a race can produce a program that works for years and then produces a value that cannot exist. A visibility problem is a write that has happened and a read that does not see it because nothing ordered them. Then the four rules that follow, and each is a requirement. Use an atomic for the flag, the counter and the pointer publication, — the three places where the answer is "a value another thread will read". Use a mutex when the invariant involves more than one variable, because a mutex is the only thing that makes a set of reads and writes atomic as a group, and an atomic per field does not achieve that. Give every atomic a memory ordering, and choose the weakest one that is correct, because the stronger orderings are more expensive and the difference is measurable in a hot loop. And Publish data with the atomic, not before it, because the ordering guarantee is created by the atomic operation and a preceding write has no guarantee attached to it at all. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A relaxed atomic where a stronger one is needed, which permits the compiler and the processor to move the write before the flag that says it happened. An atomic used as a mutex, which serialises nothing unless the increment itself is the critical section. And A flag set before the data it describes is written, which is the classic publication bug and which the ordering on the atomic is there to prevent. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Reach for a mutex first and drop to an atomic only when you can name the invariant the atomic preserves, because a mutex is correct for every case and an atomic is correct for a list you have to enumerate. And Run the concurrency tests under a thread checker, because the checker finds races that no amount of review will and that a single-threaded test cannot see by construction.

#include <stdatomic.h>
#include <stdbool.h>

/* TWO HAZARDS, TWO SOLUTIONS.
   DATA RACE  two threads access the same memory, at least one
               writes, no synchronisation. UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR -
               not "sometimes wrong"; the program may do anything,
               which is why a race works for years and then produces
               a value that cannot exist.
   VISIBILITY  a write has happened and a read does not see it
               because nothing ordered them.                   */

/* Rule 1: ATOMICS FOR THE FLAG, THE COUNTER AND THE PUBLICATION.
   Those are the places where the answer is "a value another thread
   will read". */

/* Rule 3: EVERY ATOMIC HAS AN ORDERING, and choose the WEAKEST that
   is correct - the stronger ones cost measurably in a hot loop.

   relaxed     no ordering at all. Only for a counter whose value
               nobody else interprets.
   acquire/release  one-way ordering. This is the publication
               pattern, and it is the one you will use.
   seq_cst     total order. A safe default; the easiest to reason
               about; the most expensive.                            */

struct mailbox {
    atomic_bool     ready;      /* release on store, acquire on load */
    atomic_long     seq;
    void           *payload;    /* written BEFORE ready is stored    */
};

static void publish(mailbox *m, void *p)
{
    m->payload = p;                          /* ordinary write        */
    /* The RELEASE ordering is what makes the write above visible to
       whoever sees ready == true. Without it the flag can be
       observed before the payload is - which is the classic
       publication bug, and it is why "set the flag first" fails. */
    atomic_store_explicit(&m->ready, true, memory_order_release);
}

static void *consume(mailbox *m)
{
    if (!atomic_load_explicit(&m->ready, memory_order_acquire))
        return NULL;
    /* The ACQUIRE ordering pairs with the release above: everything
       the writer did before publishing is visible here. Reading the
       flag with RELAXED and then the payload is the bug. */
    atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&m->seq, 1, memory_order_relaxed);
    return m->payload;
}

/* Rule 2: A MUTEX WHEN THE INVARIANT INVOLVES MORE THAN ONE
   VARIABLE. An atomic per field does not make a set of fields
   atomic as a group - it makes each field atomic, which is a
   different and weaker property. */
struct account { atomic_long balance; };     /* fine alone           */
struct pair { atomic_long x, y; };           /* NOT atomic as a pair */

/* Rule 4: PUBLISH WITH THE ATOMIC, NOT BEFORE IT. The ordering
   guarantee is created by the atomic operation; a preceding
   ordinary write has no guarantee attached to it at all. */

/* The three failures.
   1. A RELAXED ATOMIC WHERE A STRONGER ONE IS NEEDED: permits the
      write to be moved before the flag that says it happened.
   2. AN ATOMIC USED AS A MUTEX: an increment is atomic, so two
      threads can both read, both compute and both write - the
      increment "loses" one of them. Atomics do not serialise a
      read-modify-write sequence unless you use a compare-and-swap
      loop.
   3. A FLAG SET BEFORE THE DATA: the publication bug above.        */

static void bump_safely(atomic_long *counter)
{
    for (;;) {
        long cur = atomic_load_explicit(counter, memory_order_relaxed);
        if (atomic_compare_exchange_weak_explicit(counter, &cur, cur + 1,
                memory_order_relaxed, memory_order_relaxed))
            return;
        /* cur now holds the value that was actually there; retry. */
    }
}   /* Two disciplines: REACH FOR A MUTEX FIRST and drop to an atomic
     only when you can NAME the invariant the atomic preserves,
     because a mutex is correct in every case and an atomic is correct
     only for a list you have to enumerate. And RUN THE CONCURRENCY
     TESTS UNDER A THREAD CHECKER - it finds races no review will find
     and that a single-threaded test cannot see by construction. */

/* Two hazards, two solutions.
   DATA RACE   two threads access the same memory, at least one
               writes, no synchronisation. UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR, not
               "sometimes wrong" - the program may do anything,
               which is why a race works for years and then produces
               a value that cannot exist.
   VISIBILITY  a write has happened and a read does not see it.   */

struct mailbox {
    atomic_bool            ready;
    atomic_uint_least64_t  seq;
    void                  *payload;   /* written BEFORE ready    */
};

static void publish(mailbox *m, void *p)
{
    m->payload = p;                       /* ordinary write         */
    /* The RELEASE ordering is what makes the write above visible to
       whoever sees ready == true. Without it the flag can be
       observed BEFORE the payload - the classic publication bug,
       and the reason "set the flag first" fails. */
    atomic_store_explicit(&m->ready, true, memory_order_release);
}

static void *consume(mailbox *m)
{
    if (!atomic_load_explicit(&m->ready, memory_order_acquire))
        return NULL;
    /* ACQUIRE pairs with the release above: everything the writer
       did before publishing is visible here. Reading the flag with
       RELAXED and then the payload is the bug. */
    atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&m->seq, 1, memory_order_relaxed);
    return m->payload;
}

/* A counter built from LOAD and STORE loses updates, invisibly in a
   single-threaded test: another thread's fetch_add lands between the
   two and is overwritten. */
static atomic_long hits;
static void wrong(void) {
    long n = atomic_load(&hits);
    atomic_store(&hits, n + 1);      /* WRONG */
}
static void right(void) {
    atomic_fetch_add(&hits, 1);      /* ONE operation            */
}

/* An atomic used as a mutex SERIALISES NOTHING. Both threads can
   read, both compute, both write, and one update is lost. */
static atomic_long balance;
static void WRONG_transfer(void) {
    long mine   = atomic_load(&balance);
    long theirs = atomic_load(&balance);
    atomic_store(&balance, mine - 10);
    atomic_store(&balance, theirs + 10);   /* one update is lost  */
}

static void RIGHT_transfer(void) {
    atomic_fetch_sub(&balance, 10);
    atomic_fetch_add(&balance, 10);
}

/* THE RULE THAT MATTERS MOST: a mutex when the invariant involves
   more than one variable. Two atomics do not make a pair of fields
   atomic as a group - they make each field atomic, which is a
   different and weaker property. This is a race in the LOGIC, not in
   the memory model, and no detector finds it, because every access
   is individually atomic and correctly ordered. */

9 · Debugging, Testing and Measurement

9.1 Reading a core dump and a debugger, and when to use each9.2 Testing C without a framework9.3 Measuring before optimising, and the four profiles that lie9.4 Portability: what differs, and how to be portable cheaply

9.1 Reading a core dump and a debugger, and when to use each

A C bug report is usually a number — a signal, an address, a wrong value — and the discipline is in turning that number into a location without guessing.

Three tools, three questions

A debugger answers "what is the state at this line, now". A sanitizer answers "what is wrong, and where, without a debugger". A core dump answers "what was the state when it died, afterwards". Then the three things to keep separate, and each is a requirement. Optimisation destroys the mapping between source and machine, so a crash in a release build needs debug symbols for the exact build, and the mapping files are the artifact nobody keeps. A stack trace is a list of return addresses, not of lines, and the difference is why the top frame is frequently not where the bug is. Undefined behaviour has no trace at all, because the compiler has already been told it cannot happen. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Keep a symbol file per released version, in whatever crash service you use, keyed by build identifier — a report that cannot be symbolicated is a report you cannot fix. And Build a release-configuration binary with assertions left on for the first canary release, because assertions are the only check that fires on an impossible state, and they cost nothing when they do not fire.

<p>The order to reach for these in is worth stating, because the instinct is to reach for the wrong one first. A debugger tells you where you are. A core dump tells you where you were when the process died, which is a different and often more useful question, because the interesting state is frequently the state that is already gone. A sanitizer tells you that something is wrong even when nothing looks wrong yet. And an assertion tells you that an invariant you believed in is false, which is the cheapest of the four and the one most often left out of a codebase.

<p>So: build with the sanitizers and run the tests first, because that pass finds the classes of defect where they are still cheap to fix. When a failure reproduces only under load or only in production, capture a core dump rather than trying to reproduce it under a debugger, because a reproduction you cannot trigger on demand is a reproduction you will not reproduce. And when you finally do attach a debugger, treat it as a post-mortem tool rather than a stepping tool - run to the point of interest and read the state, because single-stepping a C program through a function call shows you the prologue and tells you nothing about the invariant that broke.

<p>Two things about core dumps are routinely misunderstood and cost hours. First, they are usually disabled by default, so the first crash on a machine produces no file at all and the investigation stalls on a permission question rather than a programming one. Second, the dump contains the whole address space, so a process that had a large allocation when it died writes a large file, and filling a disk with core files is how a crash loop takes down the host that was supposed to be diagnosing it.

<p>What a debugger is genuinely better at than a dump is the answer to "what does this variable mean, and where did it come from". A core dump gives you the bytes; a debugger gives you the type, the scope and the history of the assignment. That distinction matters for the case that comes up most in C - a structure whose padding was never written, so the bytes are whatever the last allocation to that address left behind, and the value looks like corruption rather than like an uninitialised field.

/* THE THREE TOOLS ANSWER THREE DIFFERENT QUESTIONS.
   debugGER  : what is the state at this line, right now
   SANITIZER : what is wrong, and where, with no debugger at all
   CORE DUMP : what was the state when it died, afterwards        */

/* Three things to keep separate, because they are the reason a C
   investigation stalls.

   1. OPTIMISATION DESTROYS THE source-to-machine mapping. A crash
      in a release build needs debug symbols for THAT build.
   2. A STACK TRACE IS A LIST OF RETURN ADDRESSES, not lines. The
      top frame is frequently not where the bug is - it is where
      control returned to after the damage was done.
   3. UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR HAS NO TRACE. The compiler was told it
      cannot happen, so it removed the check that would have found
      it. That is why some crashes have no explanation at all.     */

/* AddressSanitizer finds an out-of-bounds access AT THE POINT IT
   HAPPENS, with both stacks. It is worth a dedicated build: it is
   too slow for production and it changes the code's layout. */
int main(void)
{
    int a[8];
    for (int i = 0; i <= 8; ++i) a[i] = i;   /* i == 8 writes past */
    return a[3];
}
/* Build with:
     cc -fsanitize=address,undefined -fno-omit-frame-pointer -g         -O1 main.c -o main
   ASan reports the WRITE of size 4 at offset 32 into a 32-byte
   region, with the allocation stack AND the faulting stack, and
   names the bug as a heap-buffer-overflow. No guesswork.        */

/* UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer catches the cheap rules: signed
   overflow, misaligned access, invalid shifts, a load of an
   uninitialised value. It is the cheapest defect-finding tool in C
   and it has no run-time cost worth mentioning at -O1. */

/* Assertions. Keep them ON in the release configuration for the
   first canary release: they are the only check that fires on an
   impossible state, and they cost nothing when they do not fire. */
#include <assert.h>

/* The form to use: assert a CONDITION, with a message that says
   what was expected, not what the expression was. */
static size_t slice_copy(char *dst, const char *src, size_t n)
{
    assert(dst && src);
    assert(n > 0 && n < 4096);        /* the invariant, stated    */
    size_t len = strlen(src);
    if (len >= n) len = n - 1;
    memcpy(dst, src, len);
    dst[len] = '';
    return len;
}
/* Compare with the version that asserts nothing, which is the one
