   that ships in most codebases and which reports "invalid write"
   four frames away from the cause:

     size_t len = strlen(src);
     if (len >= n) len = n - 1;      <- n is 0 here
     memcpy(dst, src, len);          <- writes to a zero-length dst
     dst[len] = '';                <- writes past the end        */

/* Two disciplines.
   KEEP A SYMBOL FILE PER RELEASED VERSION, keyed by build
   identifier, uploaded to your crash service. A report that cannot
   be symbolicated is a report you cannot fix, and the symbols are
   the one artifact nobody thinks to keep.
   BUILD WITH ASSERTIONS ON for the canary release. They are the
   only check that fires on a state that should have been
   impossible, and they cost nothing when they do not fire. */

/* AddressSanitizer finds an out-of-bounds access AT THE POINT IT
   HAPPENS, with both stacks. It is worth a dedicated build: it is
   too slow for production and it changes the code's layout. */
int main(void)
{
    int a[8];
    for (int i = 0; i <= 8; i++) a[i] = i;   /* i == 8 writes PAST */
    return a[3];
}
/* $ cc -fsanitize=address -fno-omit-frame-pointer -g -O1 main.c
   ==1234==ERROR: AddressSanitizer: heap-buffer-overflow on address
   WRITE of size 4 at 0x... thread T0
       #0 0x... in main main.c:4
   allocated by thread T0 here:
       #0 0x... in main main.c:3
   SUMMARY: AddressSanitizer: heap-buffer-overflow main.c:4 in main
   No guesswork, and it names the line. */

/* UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer catches the cheap rules: signed
   overflow, misaligned access, invalid shifts, a load of an
   uninitialised value. No run-time cost worth mentioning at -O1. */

/* THE ASSERTION, in the form worth using: assert a CONDITION with a
   message that says what was expected, not what the expression was. */
static size_t slice_copy(char *dst, const char *src, size_t n)
{
    assert(dst && src);
    assert(n > 0 && n < 4096);     /* the invariant, stated       */
    size_t len = strlen(src);
    if (len >= n) len = n - 1;
    memcpy(dst, src, len);
    dst[len] = '\0';
    return len;
}
/* Without the asserts, n == 0 makes the len clamp produce n - 1, and
   dst[len] writes past the end - reported four frames away from the
   cause. */

/* THE CORE DUMP: a serialised snapshot at the moment of the signal.
   The register state, the stack and the heap map are all in it. */
void core_dump_setup(void)
{
    /* $ ulimit -c unlimited
       $ ./app            -> segfault, writes ./core
       $ gdb ./app core
       (gdb) bt full      -> the stack at the fault
       (gdb) info reg     -> the register state
       (gdb) x/16x $rsp   -> the stack words                     */
}

9.2 Testing C without a framework

C has no built-in test runner and needs almost none: a test is a function with a signature, a runner is a loop over an array of pointers, and the discipline is in the assertion, not the harness.

The smallest thing that is actually a test suite

A test returns an integer: zero for pass, non-zero for fail. A runner holds an array of function pointers and prints the name of the one that failed. The value is that the harness is thirty lines and cannot be the reason a test does not run, which is the property every larger framework gives up. Then the three things that decide whether a suite is worth having, and each is a requirement. One assertion per test, naming the value, so a failure identifies itself without a debugger. A test that can fail for exactly one reason, which means no shared setup that one test's state can change for the next. And Every boundary value tested explicitly, because the boundary is where the bug is and because the boundaries are enumerable from the specification rather than discovered by a fuzzer. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Test the boundary conditions as a table, not as code, so adding a case is adding a row and the whole boundary is visible in one place. And Run the suite under both sanitizers and at more than one optimisation level, because a bug that only appears when a check the compiler removed comes back is precisely the bug that escaped.

<p>A test framework is worth having and it is worth adding after the first twenty tests, not before. Until then the thing that matters is not the runner, the assertion library or the reporting format - it is the decision to make the test a plain function that returns a status, so it can be read, stepped through and run from a debugger like any other code. A test you cannot debug is a test that costs an afternoon every time it fails.

<p>The property that makes a C test suite work in practice is isolation of the failure. When one test fails, the next run should fail the same way, and the reason should be visible without re-reading the test. That rules out global state left behind between tests, and it is the reason the harness resets global fixtures per test rather than per run. It also rules out depending on allocation order, so a test that allocates a large buffer and frees it is not the test running first.

<p>Writing tests for C has one characteristic no other language in this library shares, and it changes what is worth testing: the boundaries are where the bugs are, and the boundaries are arithmetic. A buffer of eight bytes, a length of eight, a write of nine bytes - the test that catches it is a table of sizes around the boundary, run once per row. Writing that table once and running it against every bounded function in the codebase finds more real defects than any number of example-based tests.

/* A test is a function that returns 0 for pass and non-zero for
   fail. A runner is an array of pointers and a loop. Thirty lines,
   and it cannot be the reason a test does not run - which is the
   property every larger framework eventually gives up. */

typedef int (*test_fn)(void);

static int t_strlen_empty(void)      { return strlen("") == 0 ? 0 : 1; }
static int t_strlen_ascii(void)      { return strlen("abc") == 3 ? 0 : 1; }
static int t_strlen_embedded(void)
{
    /* THE TEST THAT MATTERS. An embedded NUL means strlen sees a
       SHORTER string than the buffer, and every function that takes
       a C string inherits the surprise. */
    const char s[] = "abcd";
    return strlen(s) == 2 ? 0 : 1;
}
static int t_copy_truncates(void)
{
    char out[4];
    if (!copy_bounded(out, sizeof out, "abcdef")) return 0;
    return strcmp(out, "abc") == 0 ? 0 : 1;
}

struct test { const char *name; test_fn fn; };

static const struct test ALL[] = {
    { "strlen/empty",     t_strlen_empty     },
    { "strlen/ascii",     t_strlen_ascii     },
    { "strlen/embedded",  t_strlen_embedded  },
    { "copy/truncates",   t_copy_truncates   },
};

static int run_all(const char *filter)
{
    int failed = 0, ran = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof ALL / sizeof ALL[0]; ++i) {
        if (filter && !strstr(ALL[i].name, filter)) continue;
        ++ran;
        int rc = ALL[i].fn();
        if (rc) { printf("FAIL %s
", ALL[i].name); ++failed; }
    }
    printf("%d run, %d failed
", ran, failed);
    return failed;
}

int main(int argc, char **argv)
{ return run_all(argc > 1 ? argv[1] : NULL) != 0; }

/* ---- boundaries as a TABLE ------------------------------------- */
/* Adding a case is adding a row, and the whole boundary becomes
   visible in one place rather than scattered through the code. */

struct boundary { int n; int want_index; int want_value; };

static const struct boundary BOUNDARY[] = {
    {  0, -1,  0 },    /* empty                        */
    {  1,  0,  0 },    /* single element               */
    {  7,  6,  6 },    /* full                         */
    {  8, -1, -1 },    /* one past: the overflow case  */
    { 16, -1, -1 },
};

static int check_boundaries(void)
{
    for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof BOUNDARY / sizeof BOUNDARY[0]; ++i) {
        int n = BOUNDARY[i].n;
        int idx = n ? n - 1 : -1;
        if (idx != BOUNDARY[i].want_index)
            return 1;
    }
    return 0;
}

/* ---- property tests -------------------------------------------- */
/* A generated case catches what an enumerated one cannot. */
#include <stdlib.h>
static int check_any_input(int (*fn)(const char *, size_t))
{
    char buf[64];
    for (int trial = 0; trial < 2000; ++trial) {
        size_t n = (size_t)(secure_random_u32() % (sizeof buf + 1));
        for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i)
            buf[i] = (char)(secure_random_u32() & 0xFF);   /* any byte */
        fn(buf, n);           /* must not crash, must not leak   */
    }
    return 0;
}

/* Two disciplines. TEST THE BOUNDARY CONDITIONS AS A TABLE, so the
   whole boundary is one readable list. And RUN THE SUITE UNDER BOTH
   SANITIZERS AND AT MORE THAN ONE OPTIMISATION LEVEL - a bug that
   appears only when the compiler removes a check is exactly the bug
   that got away, and an -O0-only suite cannot see it. */

#include <assert.h>
#include <string.h>

/* A test is a function that returns 0 for pass and non-zero for
   fail. t.Fatal STOPS the test; t.Error CONTINUES and records. Use
   Error for independent assertions and Fatal for the precondition
   everything after depends on. */
static int t_strlen_empty(void)  { return strlen("") == 0 ? 0 : 1; }
static int t_strlen_embedded(void)
{
    /* The test that matters: an embedded NUL means strlen sees a
       SHORTER string than the buffer, and every function taking a C
       string inherits the surprise. */
    const char s[] = "ab\0cd";
    return strlen(s) == 2 ? 0 : 1;
}
static int t_copy_truncates(void)
{
    char out[4];
    if (!copy_bounded(out, sizeof out, "abcdef")) return 0;  /* refused */
    return strcmp(out, "abc") == 0 ? 0 : 1;
}

struct test { const char *name; int (*fn)(void); };

static const struct test ALL[] = {
    { "strlen/empty",    t_strlen_empty    },
    { "strlen/embedded", t_strlen_embedded },
    { "copy/truncates",  t_copy_truncates  },
};

static int run_all(const char *filter)
{
    int failed = 0, ran = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof ALL / sizeof ALL[0]; ++i) {
        if (filter && !strstr(ALL[i].name, filter)) continue;
        ++ran;
        if (ALL[i].fn()) { printf("FAIL %s\n", ALL[i].name); ++failed; }
    }
    printf("%d run, %d failed\n", ran, failed);
    return failed;
}

/* BOUNDARIES AS A TABLE. Adding a case is adding a row, and the whole
   boundary becomes visible in one place rather than scattered. */
struct boundary { int n; int want_len; const char *why; };

static const struct boundary BOUNDARY[] = {
    {  0, 0, "empty"          },
    {  1, 1, "single element" },
    {  7, 7, "full"           },
    {  8, 7, "one past: truncate" },
    { 99, 7, "far past: truncate" },
};

static int check_boundaries(void)
{
    for (size_t i = 0; i < sizeof BOUNDARY / sizeof BOUNDARY[0]; ++i) {
        char buf[8];
        int n = BOUNDARY[i].n;
        if (n < 0) continue;
        if (copy_bounded(buf, sizeof buf, "1234567")) { /* ... */ }
        (void)BOUNDARY[i].want_len;
    }
    return 0;
}

9.3 Measuring before optimising, and the four profiles that lie

Performance work in C goes wrong in a predictable order: guess, change, measure, see no difference, guess again. Each wrong turn has a name and a fix.

The order that avoids wasted work

First establish that there is a problem at all, in the configuration that ships. Then find which resource is the constraint — instruction count, memory bandwidth, cache misses, branch mispredictions, or lock contention — because the fix is different for each. Then find where the time is spent, at the granularity of a function. Only then change something. Then the three things that follow, and each is a requirement. Measure in the configuration that ships, because an unoptimised build has different behaviour — inlining changes what fits in a cache line, and an assertion the compiler removed changes the cost. Measure the whole thing, not the hot loop, because a loop that is ninety per cent of the time and costs a microsecond is not the problem. And Change one thing at a time, because two changes that each improve the number by five per cent together improve it by five per cent, and you will conclude both are necessary. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Optimising at the wrong granularity, which is the compiler doing the work and doing it well. Fighting the compiler, which is hand-unrolling or manual inlining that the optimiser then has to undo, and which usually makes the code slower and longer. And Benchmarking a warm cache in a debug build, which measures nothing. Then the two practices, and both are disciplines. Keep the benchmark honest by making it part of the suite, so a regression is caught rather than noticed. And Change one thing at a time, and keep the measurement, because otherwise the second attempt is compared against a remembered number.

/* THE ORDER THAT AVOIDS WASTED WORK.

   1. Establish there IS a problem, in the configuration that ships.
   2. Find which resource is the constraint: instruction count,
      memory bandwidth, cache misses, branch mispredictions, lock
      contention. The fix is different for EACH.
   3. Find where the time goes, at function granularity.
   4. Only then change something.
   5. Measure again, the same way.                              */

/* THE FOUR PROFILES THAT LIE.

   a) THE UNOPTIMISED BUILD. Inlining changes what fits in a cache
      line, and an assertion the compiler removed changes the cost.
      An -O0 profile is a profile of a different program.
   b) THE MICROBENCHMARK. A loop that is 90% of the time and costs
      a microsecond is not the problem. Measure the WHOLE THING.
   c) THE WARM CACHE. Everything in L1. Real code is not.
   d) THE SYNTHETIC INPUT. One size, one shape, one access pattern.
      Real input distribution is where the cache misses live.  */

/* Measure the whole thing. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <time.h>
static double now_ms(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return ts.tv_sec * 1000.0 + ts.tv_nsec / 1e6;
}

static void bench(const char *name, void (*fn)(size_t), size_t n)
{
    /* warm once so the first-call costs are not in the number */
    fn(n);
    double best = 1e18;
    for (int trial = 0; trial < 7; ++trial) {
        double t0 = now_ms();
        fn(n);
        double dt = now_ms() - t0;
        if (dt < best) best = dt;        /* the MINIMUM, not the mean */
    }
    printf("%-16s %zu  %.3f ms
", name, n, best);
}   /* Take the minimum over several trials. The minimum is the run
     least disturbed by the machine's other work; the mean is
     dominated by whatever else the box was doing, and the maximum
     is dominated by one context switch.                          */

/* CHANGE ONE THING AT A TIME. Two changes that each improve the
   number by 5% together improve it by 5%, and you will conclude
   both are necessary - which is how a codebase accumulates five
   unmeasured micro-optimisations and is no faster than before. */

static void sum_i32(const size_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    int64_t t = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) t += (int64_t)a[i];
    *out = t;
}
static void sum_u32(const size_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    uint64_t t = 0;                    /* UNSIGNED is measurably
                                           faster in the wide case */
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) t += (uint64_t)a[i];
    *out = (int64_t)t;
}
static void sum_u32_unrolled(const size_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    uint64_t t = 0; size_t i = 0;
    for (; i + 4 <= n; i += 4)        /* THE OPTIMISER USUALLY
                                           ALREADY DID THIS */
        t += (uint64_t)a[i] + a[i+1] + a[i+2] + a[i+3];
    for (; i < n; ++i) t += (uint64_t)a[i];
    *out = (int64_t)t;
}  /* Note the sign. Signed overflow is UNDEFINED, so the compiler
     must add the overflow checks; unsigned wraps and needs none.
     That is why the type choice above is both a correctness and a
     speed decision - and it is the clearest example in C of the
     language's design making the fast path and the safe path the
     same path. */

/* THE ORDER THAT AVOIDS WASTED WORK:
     1. Establish there IS a problem, in the configuration that ships.
     2. Find which resource is the constraint: instruction count,
        memory bandwidth, cache misses, branch mispredictions, lock
        contention. The fix is different for EACH.
     3. Find where the time goes, at function granularity.
     4. Only then change something.                               */

/* THE FOUR PROFILES THAT LIE:
   a) the UNOPTIMISED build - inlining changes what fits in a cache
      line, so an -O0 profile is a profile of a different program;
   b) the MICROBENCHMARK - a loop that is 90% of the time and costs
      a microsecond is not the problem;
   c) the WARM CACHE - everything in L1, and your program is not;
   d) the SYNTHETIC INPUT - one size, one shape, one access pattern. */

#include <time.h>
static double now_ms(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return ts.tv_sec * 1000.0 + ts.tv_nsec / 1e6;
}

typedef void (*work_fn)(size_t);

static void bench(const char *name, work_fn fn, size_t n)
{
    fn(n);                                   /* warm once         */
    double best = 1e18;
    for (int trial = 0; trial < 7; ++trial) {
        double t0 = now_ms();
        fn(n);
        double dt = now_ms() - t0;
        if (dt < best) best = dt;            /* the MINIMUM, not the
                                                mean: the minimum is
                                                the run least
                                                disturbed by the
                                                machine's other
                                                work               */
    }
    printf("%-16s %zu  %.3f ms\n", name, n, best);
}

/* THE SIGN IS A CORRECTNESS AND A SPEED DECISION AT ONCE. Signed
   overflow is UNDEFINED, so the compiler must add the overflow
   checks; unsigned wraps and needs none. That is why the unsigned
   version below is measurably faster in the wide case - and why the
   faster path is also the defined one. */
static void sum_i32(const int32_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    int64_t t = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) t += a[i];   /* needs overflow
                                                   checks          */
    *out = t;
}

static void sum_u32(const uint32_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    uint64_t t = 0;                             /* wraps, needs none */
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) t += a[i];
    *out = (int64_t)t;
}

/* Hand-unrolling is usually what the optimiser already did, and the
   hand-written version is longer and usually slower. Measure before
   you assume otherwise. */
static void sum_u32_unrolled(const uint32_t *a, size_t n, int64_t *out)
{
    uint64_t t = 0;
    size_t i = 0;
    for (; i + 4 <= n; i += 4)
        t += (uint64_t)a[i] + a[i+1] + a[i+2] + a[i+3];
    for (; i < n; ++i) t += a[i];
    *out = (int64_t)t;
}

9.4 Portability: what differs, and how to be portable cheaply

Portability is not one problem but a list of them, and the list is short enough to memorise — which means a portability defect is always a decision somebody made without knowing the list.

The places the platforms differ

Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Never rely on the size of a type; name it, with the fixed-width family or the pointer-sized one, when the width is a property of your data. Never rely on structure layout across a boundary; encode it explicitly, as in chapter 3's wire format. Never assume the character type is signed, because it is not guaranteed and the two common platforms disagree. And Never assume the line ending, the path separator or the case sensitivity, because all three differ and all three produce failures that appear only on the platform you did not test on. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Keep the platform-dependent code in one small layer with a narrow interface, so that porting is a bounded task rather than an audit. And Build on the awkward target regularly, because portability that is only tested on the development machine is a claim.

<p>Portable C is a smaller subject than it looks, and that is the good news. The list of things that actually differ between the platforms anyone deploys to is short: the width of the fundamental integer types, whether plain char is signed, how many bits are in a byte, the byte order of a multi-byte value, the padding between structure members, and the conventions for paths, line endings and case sensitivity. Everything else in the language behaves the same. A program that avoids assuming those six things will run unchanged on a machine it was never compiled on, and a program that assumes any one of them will fail in a way that looks like a logic bug.

<p>The failures are asymmetric in a way that matters for how you plan the work. A type-width assumption fails on exactly one platform, so it survives review, survives CI if CI has one architecture, and is discovered by a customer. A path-separator assumption fails on exactly one platform in the other direction. Both are invisible on the machine the code was written on, and both are the kind of defect that arrives as "it does not work on the new server" with no error message to work backwards from.

<p>This is why the strategy that pays is not auditing every file. It is putting the platform-dependent operations behind one small layer with a narrow interface - a handful of functions for path joining and splitting, for reading an environment variable into a typed value, for spawning a child, for the current time in both forms, for listing a directory, and for the secure random source - and writing everything else against that layer. Porting then becomes a bounded task with a known size instead of an audit of the whole codebase, and a portability defect becomes a bug in a file whose entire job is to be platform-dependent.

<p>The other half of the strategy is procedural: build for the awkward target regularly, on every commit, whether or not anyone has asked for it. Portability tested only on the development machine is a claim rather than a fact, and a claim of this kind is discovered by somebody else. A continuous integration matrix with two architectures, two byte orders and one platform with a different line-ending convention costs nothing to maintain and converts an unbounded class of customer-reported defects into a bounded number of build failures you wrote the test for.

#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>

/* WHERE PLATFORMS DIFFER. This list is short, which means a
   portability defect is always a decision somebody made without
   knowing it.

   TYPE WIDTHS   int is 16, 32 or 64 bits. long is 32 or 64.
                 Both are 64 on LP64 and long is 32 on LLP64.
   CHAR SIGNEDNESS  signed on x86, unsigned on ARM. Same code, one
                 correct and one wrong.
   CHAR WIDTH    CHAR_BIT is not guaranteed to be 8. On a DSP it
                 can be 16.
   ENDIANNESS    byte order differs; so does the order of bits in a
                 byte.
   ALIGNMENT     the padding inserted between members differs, so
                 a structure's size differs.
   PATH SEPARATOR  backslash, slash, or both.
   LINE ENDINGS  newline, carriage-return-plus-newline.
   CASE          a filesystem may be case-preserving or
                 case-insensitive; a language identifier is always
                 case-sensitive.
   TEXT SIZE      the platform's own - not portable at all. */

/* Rule 1: NEVER RELY ON THE SIZE OF A TYPE; NAME IT. */
static void widths(void)
{
    /* int      4 bytes on LP64, LLP64, ILP32
       long     8 on LP64,     4 on LLP64
       long long 8 everywhere                       */
    printf("%zu %zu %zu
", sizeof(int), sizeof(long), sizeof(long long));
}   /* The pair (int, long) is 12 bytes on Unix and 8 on Windows -
     which is why a struct containing both has a different layout and
     a different size on the two, silently. */

/* Rule 2: NEVER RELY ON STRUCTURE LAYOUT ACROSS A BOUNDARY. The
   padding differs, so encode the bytes you send. */
static void put_u32le(unsigned char *p, uint32_t v)
{
    p[0] = (unsigned char)(v);
    p[1] = (unsigned char)(v >>  8);
    p[2] = (unsigned char)(v >> 16);
    p[3] = (unsigned char)(v >> 24);
}

/* Rule 3: NEVER ASSUME THE CHARACTER TYPE IS SIGNED. */
static int is_eof(int c) { return c != EOF && c != (unsigned char)EOF; }

/* Rule 4: NEVER ASSUME the line ending, the path separator or the
   case sensitivity. A program that writes '
' and reads it back as
   text works on Unix and produces one long line on Windows; one
   that compares filenames with strcmp works on Linux and fails on
   macOS's default filesystem. */

/* THE CHEAP PORTABILITY STRATEGY.

   Keep the platform-dependent code in ONE SMALL LAYER with a
   narrow interface, so porting is a bounded task rather than an
   audit of the whole codebase. In practice that layer contains:
     - path joining and splitting
     - reading an environment variable into a typed value
     - spawning a child process
     - the current time in both forms
     - listing a directory
     - the secure random source
   Everything else is written against that layer and never against
   the platform, which is what makes the second platform a week of
   work rather than a quarter. */

/* AND BUILD ON THE AWKWARD TARGET REGULARLY. Portability that is
   only ever compiled on the development machine is a claim, and the
   claim is discovered by a customer. A continuous-integration
   matrix with two architectures and two byte orders is the cheapest
   possible insurance. */

/* WHERE PLATFORMS DIFFER. The list is short, which means a
   portability defect is always a decision somebody made without
   knowing it.

   TYPE WIDTHS    int is 16, 32 or 64 bits. long is 32 or 64. Both
                  are 64 on LP64 and long is 32 on LLP64 - so a
                  struct containing both has a DIFFERENT SIZE on
                  the two, silently.
   CHAR SIGNED    signed on x86, unsigned on ARM: same source, one
                  correct and one wrong.
   CHAR WIDTH     CHAR_BIT is not guaranteed to be 8.
   ENDIANNESS     byte order differs; so does bit order in a byte.
   ALIGNMENT      the padding between members differs, so a
                  structure's SIZE differs.
   PATHS          separator, case sensitivity, line endings.        */

#include <stdint.h>
#include <stddef.h>
#include <limits.h>

static void widths(void)
{
    /* int 4, long 8 on LP64; int 4, long 4 on LLP64. The pair is 12
       bytes on Unix and 8 on Windows. */
    printf("%zu %zu %zu\n", sizeof(int), sizeof(long), sizeof(long long));
}

/* RULE 1: NEVER RELY ON THE SIZE OF A TYPE; NAME IT. */
static void named_widths(void)
{
    size_t  off  = 0xFF;   /* a count of bytes: platform-wide */
    int64_t ts   = 0;      /* a timestamp: platform-wide        */
    uint8_t b    = 0xAB;   /* a byte of a wire format           */
    uint32_t seq = 0;      /* a wire field: always 4 bytes       */
    (void)off; (void)ts; (void)b; (void)seq;
}

/* RULE 2: NEVER RELY ON STRUCTURE LAYOUT ACROSS A BOUNDARY. Encode
   the bytes explicitly - the padding differs, so the layout does. */
static void put_u32le(unsigned char *p, uint32_t v)
{
    p[0] = (unsigned char)(v);
    p[1] = (unsigned char)(v >>  8);
    p[2] = (unsigned char)(v >> 16);
    p[3] = (unsigned char)(v >> 24);
}

/* RULE 3: NEVER ASSUME THE CHARACTER TYPE IS SIGNED. */
static int is_byte(int c)
{
    /* The character functions all compare against (unsigned char). */
    return c != EOF && c != (int)(unsigned char)EOF;
}

/* RULE 4: NEVER ASSUME the line ending, the path separator or the
   case sensitivity. A program that writes a newline and reads it
   back as text works on Unix and produces one long line on Windows;
   one that compares filenames with strcmp works on Linux and fails
   on a case-insensitive filesystem. */

/* THE CHEAP PORTABILITY STRATEGY. Keep the platform-dependent code in
   ONE SMALL LAYER with a narrow interface, so porting is a bounded
   task rather than an audit of the whole codebase. In practice that
   layer contains: path joining and splitting; reading an environment
   variable into a typed value; spawning a child; the current time in
   both forms; listing a directory; and the secure random source.
   Everything else is written against that layer and never against
   the platform. */

/* AND BUILD ON THE AWKWARD TARGET REGULARLY. Portability tested only
   on the development machine is a claim, and the claim is
   discovered by a customer. A CI matrix with two architectures and
   two byte orders is the cheapest insurance there is. */

10 · Secure C and Defensive Practice

10.1 The buffer rules, and why the safe alternatives are rare10.2 Input, injection and the trust boundary10.3 Reducing the attack surface you did not write10.4 A review checklist that finds C defects

10.1 The buffer rules, and why the safe alternatives are rare

Most C security defects are one of four shapes, and three of them are a missing length next to a pointer — which is why the single highest-value convention in systems C is a length-carrying buffer type.

Four shapes, one cause

A stack overflow writes past the end of an automatic object and can overwrite a return address. A heap overflow writes past an allocated block and corrupts whatever is next. An off-by-one writes one element too far, which is the same bug with a luckier outcome. A format-string defect lets data be read as instructions. All four share a cause: a pointer to a location with no record of how much room is there. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Every buffer carries its capacity, in a structure, so that the compiler can see both. Every write is bounded by an explicit check in the same expression, so that reordering cannot remove it. Every formatted output uses the bounded form and the length is checked, because truncation is better than overflow and the return value says which happened. And Never let a format string be data, which is the fourth shape and the cheapest to eliminate. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A length that lives somewhere else, in a comment or a variable three functions away, which means the check is against the wrong value. A check that is not adjacent to the write, which a later edit can reorder away without anyone noticing. And A string operation on a buffer that is not terminated, which reads past the end looking for a zero — the same read as the last chapter's trap, but now on a security boundary. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Adopt one length-carrying buffer type and make it the only kind you write, so that the missing length is not expressible. And Turn on the compiler's own buffer diagnostics and treat them as errors, because the compiler sees a constant-size overrun that no amount of review catches.

#include <stddef.h>
#include <string.h>

/* Four shapes, one cause. A POINTER TO A LOCATION WITH NO RECORD OF
   HOW MUCH ROOM IS THERE.
   1. STACK OVERFLOW   writes past an automatic object; can
                       overwrite a return address.
   2. HEAP OVERFLOW   writes past an allocated block; corrupts
                       whatever is next.
   3. OFF-BY-ONE      writes one element too far: the same bug
                       with a luckier outcome.
   4. FORMAT STRING   lets data be read as instructions.          */

typedef struct { char  *p; size_t len; size_t cap; } textbuf;

static int textbuf_reserve(textbuf *b, size_t extra);
static int textbuf_put(textbuf *b, const char *s);

/* RULE 1: every buffer carries its capacity, in a structure, so the
   compiler can see both. Then the missing length is not expressible. */
static int textbuf_put(textbuf *b, const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    /* RULE 2: the check is in the SAME EXPRESSION as the write, so
       no later edit can reorder it away. A check in a preceding
       statement is a check somebody can delete. */
    if (n >= b->cap - b->len) return textbuf_reserve(b, n + 1);
    memcpy(b->p + b->len, s, n + 1);     /* n + 1 for the NUL       */
    b->len += n;
    return 1;
}

static int textbuf_reserve(textbuf *b, size_t extra)
{
    if (extra > SIZE_MAX - b->len) return 0;
    size_t need = b->len + extra;
    if (need <= b->cap) return 1;
    size_t cap = b->cap ? b->cap : 64;
    while (cap < need) { if (cap > SIZE_MAX / 2) return 0; cap *= 2; }
    char *g = realloc(b->p, cap);
    if (!g) return 0;
    b->p = g; b->cap = cap;
    return 1;
}

/* RULE 3: the bounded formatted form, and CHECK THE RETURN VALUE -
   it reports the length that WOULD have been written, which is the
   only way to detect truncation without a second pass. */
static void safe_snprintf(char *out, size_t cap, const char *fmt, ...)
{
    va_list ap;
    va_start(ap, fmt);
    int want = vsnprintf(out, cap, fmt, ap);
    va_end(ap);
    if (want < 0 || (size_t)want >= cap) {
        /* Truncated. Say so, or refuse - never continue silently. */
        out[0] = '';
    }
}

/* RULE 4: NEVER LET A FORMAT STRING BE DATA. This is the fourth
   shape and the cheapest to eliminate. */
static void log_line(const char *user_supplied)
{
    /* WRONG: user_supplied is read as a format string. */
    /* fprintf(stderr, user_supplied); */

    /* RIGHT: it is an ARGUMENT. The format is a literal. */
    fprintf(stderr, "log: %s
", user_supplied);
}

/* THE THREE FAILURES.
   1. A LENGTH THAT LIVES SOMEWHERE ELSE - in a comment, or in a
      variable three functions away - means the check is against the
      WRONG VALUE, and nothing can tell you.
   2. A CHECK NOT ADJACENT TO THE WRITE, which a later edit can
      reorder away without anyone noticing.
   3. A STRING OPERATION ON A BUFFER THAT IS NOT TERMINATED, which
      reads past the end looking for a zero. The same read as the
      truncation trap, but now on a security boundary. */

/* Turn the compiler's own diagnostics on and treat them as errors.
   They catch a constant-size overrun that no amount of review finds:
     -Wall -Wextra -Werror
     -Wstringop-overflow -Wstringop-overread    (constant-size cases)
     -D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2                        (with optimisation)
   ASan catches the rest, at run time, exactly once. */

int main(void) { return 0; }
/* Two disciplines: ADOPT ONE LENGTH-CARRYING BUFFER TYPE and make it
   the only kind you write, so the missing length is not expressible;
   and TURN ON THE COMPILER'S BUFFER DIAGNOSTICS as errors, because
   the compiler sees a constant-size overrun that no review catches. */

/* THE ARITHMETIC UNDERNEATH EVERY OVERFLOW HERE: a buffer of size n
   holds n-1 characters plus the terminator, so writing n characters
   plus a terminator needs n+1 bytes. */
enum { CAP = 8 };
static char dest[CAP];          /* 7 characters max                */

/* THE TRAP: strncpy does NOT terminate when the source fills the
   destination. This is the behaviour that produced a dedicated
   warning in every compiler, and it is still the mechanism behind
   "works on my machine, garbage in the log file". */
static void the_trap(void)
{
    const char *src = "12345678";        /* 8 chars, no room for NUL */
    strncpy(dest, src, sizeof dest);     /* dest has NO terminator   */
    /* strlen(dest) reads past the end. Every later string call does
       too. It may print correctly, which is what makes it survive
       testing. */
}

/* THE HELPER. Write it once and route every copy through it, so the
   "refuse rather than truncate" policy exists in exactly one place
   instead of being re-derived at forty call sites. */
typedef struct { char *p; size_t len, cap; } textbuf;

static int tb_reserve(textbuf *b, size_t extra)
{
    if (extra > SIZE_MAX - b->len) return 0;
    size_t need = b->len + extra + 1;      /* +1 for the terminator */
    if (need <= b->cap) return 1;
    size_t cap = b->cap ? b->cap : 64;
    while (cap < need) {
        if (cap > SIZE_MAX / 2) return 0;
        cap *= 2;
    }
    char *g = realloc(b->p, cap);
    if (!g) return 0;
    b->p = g; b->cap = cap;
    return 1;
}

static int tb_put(textbuf *b, const char *s)
{
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    if (!tb_reserve(b, n)) return 0;
    memcpy(b->p + b->len, s, n + 1);        /* reserve runs FIRST   */
    b->len += n;
    return 1;
}

/* TRUNCATION IS WORSE THAN FAILURE. A path that names a different
   file is not an error - it is a successful write to the wrong
   place. */
static void truncation_hazard(void)
{
    char path[16];
    /* snprintf truncates to 15 chars and terminates. No error, no
       warning, and the result is a valid path to something else. */
    snprintf(path, sizeof path, "/var/lib/verylong/%s", "app.db");
    printf("%s\n", path);   /* /var/lib/verylon - the wrong file */
}

/* THE COMPILER'S OWN DIAGNOSTICS catch a constant-size overrun that
   no review finds. Turn them on and treat them as errors:
     -Wall -Wextra -Werror
     -Wstringop-overflow -Wstringop-overread   (constant-size cases)
     -D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2                      (with optimisation)  */

10.2 Input, injection and the trust boundary

Everything crossing into a C program is hostile until proven otherwise, and the three places it enters — arguments, files, network — each have a specific discipline.

Three entries, three disciplines

A command-line argument is a string of arbitrary bytes, possibly with no terminating newline and possibly longer than any buffer you assumed. A file is bytes whose format you do not control, and which may be enormous, truncated, or not what its name says. A network message is a length you must read before you can trust any of it, from a peer that may send a length larger than the memory you have. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Read a length before you allocate on it, and refuse anything above a stated maximum — an unbounded read from a peer is a denial-of-service with no effort. Validate every value into a typed variable, so that the rest of the program works with an integer that is known to be in range rather than with a string. Use a parameterised query for every database access, because the prepared form is the only one where the value cannot change the statement. And Treat a parse failure as an error, never as a default, because a default applied to invalid input is a program that silently does the wrong thing. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. Building a query or a path by concatenation, where a quote in the data ends the string. Trusting a length read from the wire, which allocates whatever the peer said. And A directory traversal from an untrusted name, which is the same mechanism one level up. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Parse into a struct with an explicit validity flag, so that "parsed successfully" is a fact the type system can carry. And Put the validation in one function per input kind, so it is testable and there is exactly one place to look when a field is added.

/* THREE ENTRIES, THREE DISCIPLINES.
   ARGUMENT   arbitrary bytes, possibly no newline, possibly longer
              than any buffer you assumed.
   FILE       bytes whose format you do not control; enormous,
              truncated, or not what its name says.
   NETWORK    a length you must read BEFORE you can trust any of it,
              from a peer that may claim more than you have.      */

/* RULE 1: READ A LENGTH BEFORE YOU ALLOCATE ON IT, and refuse
   anything above a stated maximum. An unbounded read from a peer is
   a denial of service with no effort required. */
#define MAX_FRAME (1u << 20)

static int read_frame(int fd, unsigned char **out, size_t *out_len)
{
    unsigned char hdr[4];
    size_t got = 0;
    while (got < 4) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, hdr + got, 4 - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0)  return -1;
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    uint32_t len;
    memcpy(&len, hdr, 4);
    if (len > MAX_FRAME) return -1;      /* DO NOT TRUST IT        */

    unsigned char *buf = malloc((size_t)len + 1);
    if (!buf) return -1;
    for (got = 0; got < len; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, buf + got, (size_t)len - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; free(buf); return -1; }
        if (k == 0)  { free(buf); return -1; }   /* short frame    */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    buf[len] = '';
    *out = buf; *out_len = len;
    return 0;
}

/* RULE 2: VALIDATE INTO A TYPED VARIABLE, so the rest of the
   program works with a value known to be in range. */
typedef struct { unsigned port; bool tls; char host[256]; } endpoint;
typedef struct { endpoint e; bool valid; } endpoint_result;

static endpoint_result parse_endpoint(const char *s)
{
    endpoint_result r = { .valid = false };
    const char *colon = strrchr(s, ':');
    if (!colon || colon == s) return r;
    size_t hlen = (size_t)(colon - s);
    if (hlen >= sizeof r.e.host) return r;

    char *end;
    long p = strtol(colon + 1, &end, 10);
    if (end == colon + 1 || *end != '') return r;
    if (p < 1 || p > 65535) return r;

    memcpy(r.e.host, s, hlen);
    r.e.host[hlen] = '';
    r.e.port = (unsigned)p;
    r.e.tls  = (hlen > 4 && strncmp(r.e.host, "tls:", 4) == 0);
    r.valid  = true;                    /* SUCCESS IS A FACT     */
    return r;
}

/* RULE 3: A PARAMETERISED QUERY, always. The prepared form is the
   only one where the value cannot change the statement. */
static void find_user(db *db, const char *name)
{
    /* WRONG: a quote in the name ends the string.
       db_query(db, "SELECT id FROM u WHERE n = '" + name + "'");
       RIGHT: the value cannot become part of the statement. */
    stmt *st = db_prepare(db, "SELECT id FROM u WHERE n = ?");
    db_bind_text(st, 1, name, -1);
    while (db_step(st) == ROW) emit(db_column_int(st, 0));
    db_final(st);
}

/* RULE 4: A PARSE FAILURE IS AN ERROR, NEVER A DEFAULT. A default
   applied to invalid input is a program that silently does the
   wrong thing. */
static unsigned parse_port_or_fail(const char *s)
{
    char *end;
    long p = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s || *end != '' || p < 1 || p > 65535)
        die("port must be an integer between 1 and 65535");
    return (unsigned)p;                  /* NOT a default         */
}  /* Two disciplines: PARSE INTO A STRUCT WITH AN EXPLICIT VALIDITY
     FLAG, so "parsed successfully" is a fact the type system can
     carry rather than a convention; and PUT THE VALIDATION IN ONE
     FUNCTION PER INPUT KIND, so it is testable and there is exactly
     one place to look when a field is added. */

/* Read a length BEFORE you allocate on it, and refuse anything
   above a stated maximum. An unbounded read from a peer is a denial
   of service with one line of client code. */
#define MAX_FRAME (1u << 20)

static int read_frame(int fd, unsigned char **out, size_t *out_len)
{
    unsigned char hdr[4];
    for (size_t got = 0; got < 4; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, hdr + got, 4 - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; return -1; }
        if (k == 0) return -1;
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    uint32_t len;
    memcpy(&len, hdr, 4);
    if (len > MAX_FRAME) return -1;      /* NEVER TRUST IT        */

    unsigned char *buf = malloc((size_t)len + 1);
    if (!buf) return -1;
    for (size_t got = 0; got < len; ) {
        ssize_t k = read(fd, buf + got, len - got);
        if (k < 0) { if (errno == EINTR) continue; free(buf); return -1; }
        if (k == 0) { free(buf); return -1; }   /* short frame     */
        got += (size_t)k;
    }
    buf[len] = '\0';                    /* so it is a safe string */
    *out = buf; *out_len = len;
    return 0;
}

/* VALIDATE INTO A TYPED VARIABLE, so the rest of the program works
   with a value known to be in range rather than with a string.
   Success is a FACT in the type, not a convention. */
typedef struct { unsigned port; bool tls; char host[256]; } endpoint;
typedef struct { endpoint e; bool valid; } endpoint_result;

static endpoint_result parse_endpoint(const char *s)
{
    endpoint_result r = { .valid = false };
    const char *colon = strrchr(s, ':');
    if (!colon || colon == s) return r;
    size_t hlen = (size_t)(colon - s);
    if (hlen >= sizeof r.e.host) return r;

    char *end;
    long p = strtol(colon + 1, &end, 10);
    if (end == colon + 1 || *end != '\0') return r;
    if (p < 1 || p > 65535) return r;

    memcpy(r.e.host, s, hlen);
    r.e.host[hlen] = '\0';
    r.e.port = (unsigned)p;
    r.valid  = true;                    /* the caller now KNOWS   */
    return r;
}

/* A PARAMETERISED QUERY, always. The prepared form is the only one
   where the value cannot become part of the statement. */
static void find_user(db *db, const char *name)
{
    /* WRONG: a quote in the name ends the string literal.
       db_query(db, "SELECT id FROM u WHERE n = '" + name + "'");
       RIGHT: the value cannot become part of the statement. */
    stmt *st = db_prepare(db, "SELECT id FROM u WHERE n = ?");
    db_bind_text(st, 1, name, -1);
    while (db_step(st) == ROW) emit(db_column_int(st, 0));
    db_final(st);
}

/* A PARSE FAILURE IS AN ERROR, NEVER A DEFAULT. A default applied
   to invalid input is a program that silently does the wrong thing. */
static unsigned parse_port_or_fail(const char *s)
{
    char *end;
    long p = strtol(s, &end, 10);
    if (end == s || *end != '\0' || p < 1 || p > 65535)
        die("port must be an integer between 1 and 65535");
    return (unsigned)p;                  /* NOT a default         */
}

10.3 Reducing the attack surface you did not write

Most of a C program's reachable surface is code it did not write — the library, the dependencies, the platform — and the most effective hardening is choosing not to expose it.

Four reductions, in order of effect

Not linking a feature removes its code from the binary entirely, and code that is not in the binary cannot be exploited. Not linking a dependency does the same for every bug in it, which is why a small dependency graph is a security property and not just a maintenance one. Reducing the privileges of the process removes the impact of whatever is found, and it is the single most effective control available to a program that does not need to be root. And Making the stack non-executable removes the most common exploitation technique, and is a linker flag on most platforms. Then the four things to follow, and each is a requirement. Link the allocator, the threading and the network layers explicitly, so the linker records the symbols you use — a program that starts from a constructor should still avoid pulling in what it does not need. Drop privileges as early as possible, and re-acquire them for the one operation that needs them, because a program that needs root for one call has a root window for its whole run. Turn on every hardening flag the platform offers, and read what each one costs rather than accepting the defaults. And Keep the reachable network surface minimal, because every listening socket is a place an untrusted packet can arrive. Then the three things that go wrong, and each is a mechanism. A dependency with a known advisory that nobody tracked, which is the mechanism behind most compromised software. A program running as root because one thing needs it, which multiplies the impact of any bug by the size of the machine. And A binary built with the default hardening, which on most toolchains means stack protection off in the release configuration. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Review the dependency list the way you review code, because each entry is code you shipped without reading. And Make the least-privilege version the one that is easy to run, because a hardening measure nobody uses is not a hardening measure.

/* MOST OF THE REACHABLE SURFACE IS CODE YOU DID NOT WRITE: the
   library, the dependencies, the platform. The four reductions,
   in order of effect:

   1. NOT LINKING A FEATURE removes its code from the binary, and
      code that is not in the binary cannot be exploited.
   2. NOT LINKING A DEPENDENCY does the same for every bug in it.
      A small dependency graph is a SECURITY property, not just a
      maintenance one.
   3. REDUCING THE PROCESS PRIVILEGE removes the impact of whatever
      is found. The single most effective control available to a
      program that does not need to be root.
   4. A NON-EXECUTABLE STACK removes the most common exploitation
      technique. It is a linker flag on most platforms.          */

/* RULE 1: LINK EXPLICITLY, so the linker records what you use. */
cc -o svc svc.c -lpthread -lm -ldl   /* and nothing else */
/* Some toolchains will still pull in more than you asked for
   because a constructor is registered by a library you do want.
   Check the result:  ldd svc   or   size svc  or the map file. */

/* RULE 2: DROP PRIVILEGES AS EARLY AS POSSIBLE, and re-acquire for
   the one operation that needs them. A program that needs root for
   one call has a root WINDOW for its whole run - which is the
   window an attacker wants. */
static int drop_privileges(uid_t want)
{
    gid_t g = getgid();
    if (setgroups(1, &g) != 0) return -1;   /* BEFORE setgid       */
    if (setgid(g) != 0) return -1;          /* setgid is permanent */
    if (setuid(want) != 0) return -1;       /* after the group     */
    /* Verify: getuid() must now report the dropped user. A program
       that assumes the call succeeded is a program that runs as
       root on a system where the call failed. */
    if (getuid() != want) { perror("privilege drop failed"); return -1; }
    return 0;
}

/* RULE 3: TURN ON EVERY HARDENING FLAG, and READ WHAT IT COSTS. */
cc -O2 -g    -fstack-protector-strong    /* canaries on the stack          */
   -D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2         /* catches constant-size overflow */
   -fPIE -pie                  /* position-independent executable */
   -Wl,-z,relro,-z,now         /* full RELRO: no lazy binding    */
   -Wl,-z,noexecstack          /* non-executable stack           */
   -fsanitize=undefined        /* in the test and canary builds */
   svc.c -o svc
/* Each of these costs something: stack protection a little
   throughput, full RELRO a little startup time, position
   independence a little on some loaders. Read the costs rather
   than accepting the defaults, because a flag nobody understands is
   a flag nobody will notice when it stops working.              */

/* RULE 4: KEEP THE LISTENING SURFACE MINIMAL. Every socket is a
   place an untrusted packet can arrive. */
struct addrinfo hints = { .ai_family = AF_UNSPEC, .ai_socktype = SOCK_STREAM };
/* Bind to a specific interface, not to every one: ai_passive with a
   NULL node binds all interfaces, which on a machine with both a
   public and a private address publishes a service you meant to
   keep internal. */

/* THE THREE FAILURES.
   1. A DEPENDENCY WITH A KNOWN ADVISORY NOBODY TRACKED - the
      mechanism behind most compromised software.
   2. A PROGRAM RUNNING AS ROOT BECAUSE ONE THING NEEDS IT, which
      multiplies the impact of any bug by the size of the machine.
   3. A BINARY BUILT WITH THE DEFAULT HARDENING, which on most
      toolchains means stack protection is off in the release
      configuration because the default build type is debug.       */

/* Two disciplines. REVIEW THE DEPENDENCY LIST THE WAY YOU REVIEW
   CODE, because each entry is code you shipped without reading.
   And MAKE THE LEAST-PRIVILEGE VERSION THE ONE THAT IS EASY TO
   RUN, because a hardening measure nobody uses is not a hardening
   measure - it is a line in a document. */

/* MOST OF THE REACHABLE SURFACE IS CODE YOU DID NOT WRITE: the
   library, the dependencies, the platform. The four reductions, in
   order of effect:

   1. NOT LINKING A FEATURE removes its code from the binary, and
      code that is not in the binary cannot be exploited.
   2. NOT LINKING A DEPENDENCY does the same for every bug in it. A
      small dependency graph is a SECURITY property, not just a
      maintenance one.
   3. REDUCING THE PROCESS PRIVILEGE removes the impact of whatever
      is found - the single most effective control available to a
      program that does not need to be root.
   4. A NON-EXECUTABLE STACK removes the most common exploitation
      technique. It is a linker flag on most platforms.            */

static int drop_privileges(uid_t want)
{
    gid_t g = getgid();
    if (setgroups(1, &g) != 0) return -1;   /* BEFORE setgid       */
    if (setgid(g) != 0) return -1;          /* setgid is permanent */
    if (setuid(want) != 0) return -1;       /* after the group     */
    /* Verify. A program that assumes the call succeeded is a
       program that runs as root on a system where it failed. */
    if (getuid() != want) { perror("privilege drop failed"); return -1; }
    return 0;
}

/* EVERY HARDENING FLAG, AND WHAT IT COSTS. A flag nobody
   understands is a flag nobody will notice when it stops working. */
void build_hardened(void)
{
    /* -fstack-protector-strong   canaries on the stack      */
    /* -D_FORTIFY_SOURCE=2        catches constant-size overflow */
    /* -fPIE -pie                 position-independent exe   */
    /* -Wl,-z,relro,-z,now        full RELRO: no lazy binding */
    /* -Wl,-z,noexecstack         non-executable stack       */
    /* -fsanitize=undefined       in the test and canary builds */
    /* Each costs something: stack protection a little throughput,
       full RELRO a little startup time, PIE a little on some
       loaders. Read the costs rather than accepting defaults.   */
}

/* KEEP THE LISTENING SURFACE MINIMAL. Every socket is a place an
   untrusted packet can arrive. Bind to a specific interface rather
   than to every one: ai_passive with a NULL node binds all
   interfaces, which on a machine with both a public and a private
   address publishes a service you meant to keep internal. */

/* THE THREE FAILURES:
   1. A DEPENDENCY WITH A KNOWN ADVISORY NOBODY TRACKED - the
      mechanism behind most compromised software.
   2. A PROGRAM RUNNING AS ROOT BECAUSE ONE THING NEEDS IT - which
      multiplies the impact of any bug by the size of the machine.
   3. A BINARY BUILT WITH DEFAULT HARDENING, which on most
      toolchains means stack protection is OFF in the release
      configuration because the default build type is debug. */

/* TWO PRACTICES. REVIEW THE DEPENDENCY LIST THE WAY YOU REVIEW
   CODE, because each entry is code you shipped without reading. And
   MAKE THE LEAST-PRIVILEGE VERSION THE ONE THAT IS EASY TO RUN,
   because a hardening measure nobody uses is not a hardening
   measure - it is a line in a document. */

10.4 A review checklist that finds C defects

A C review that looks for the right things finds real defects in an afternoon; one that reads for style finds none of them, because the interesting defects are absences.

What to read for, in order

Start with every pointer, asking who owns it and who frees it. Then every buffer, asking where the length is. Then every allocation, asking what happens when it fails and what happens on each early return after it. Then every cast, asking what it is asserting. Then every arithmetic expression on sizes, asking whether it can overflow. Then every boundary check, asking whether it is adjacent to the operation it protects. Then every use of a raw numeric literal, asking whether a named constant would have carried the reason. Then every comment that claims an invariant, asking whether the code enforces it. Then the error paths, asking whether each one leaves the program in a state a caller could reason about. Then the release configuration, asking whether the build that ships has the same code as the build that was tested. Then the public interface, asking whether every contract is written where a caller will read it. Then the tests, asking what they would fail on. Then the dependencies, asking when each was last looked at. Then the one thing that will hurt most, asking whether you would rather know now. Then the naming, because it is last on purpose: consistent and boring naming is worth having and it is not a defect. Then the follow-up, asking what is written down that a reader will need and that the code does not say — because a defect nobody writes down is a defect that recurs. Then the remaining risk, asking whether it is acceptable and who decided. Then the two habits that keep it honest, and each is a discipline. Review the interface before the implementation, because the interface is what cannot be changed cheaply and everything else is an implementation detail of it. And Make the compiler the first reviewer, with the warning set as errors, because it finds a class of defect that a person reading for ownership will consistently miss.

/* WHAT TO READ FOR, IN ORDER. The interesting defects are
   ABSENCES, so a review that reads for style finds none of them.

   1.  Every pointer: who owns it, who frees it.
   2.  Every buffer: where the length is.
   3.  Every allocation: what happens when it fails, and what
       happens on each early return AFTER it.
   4.  Every cast: what is it asserting?
   5.  Every arithmetic expression on sizes: can it overflow?
   6.  Every boundary check: is it adjacent to what it protects?
   7.  Every raw numeric literal: would a named constant have
       carried the reason?
   8.  Every comment claiming an invariant: does the code enforce it?
   9.  Every error path: does it leave the program in a state a
       caller could reason about?
   10. The release configuration: is the build that ships the build
       that was tested?
   11. The public interface: is every contract written where a
       caller will read it?
   12. The tests: what would they fail on?
   13. The dependencies: when was each last looked at?
   14. The one thing that will hurt most: would you rather know now?
   15. The naming: LAST, on purpose. Consistent boring naming is
       worth having and it is not a defect.                      */

/* What a review of this shape finds that a style review does not:

     ptr = malloc(n);                    <- no NULL check
     if (x) { ... }                      <- no comment, no owner
     snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "%s", u); <- unchecked truncation
     return a[i];                        <- bounds come from where?
     int total = count * sizeof *p;      <- can count * sizeof overflow?
     assert(n < 10);                     <- enforced in release? NO */

/* Two habits that keep it honest.

   REVIEW THE INTERFACE BEFORE THE IMPLEMENTATION. The interface
   is what cannot be changed cheaply, and everything else is an
   implementation detail of it. Reading the implementation first
   means you have already accepted the interface by the time you
   look at it.

   MAKE THE COMPILER THE FIRST REVIEWER. It finds a class of defect
   that a person reading for ownership consistently misses: the
   missing return, the sign comparison, the uninitialised field,
   the format mismatch. Warnings as errors means those never reach
   a human at all.                                              */

/* WHAT TO READ FOR, IN ORDER. The interesting defects are ABSENCES,
   so a review that reads for style finds none of them.

   1.  Every pointer: who owns it, who frees it.
   2.  Every buffer: where the length is.
   3.  Every allocation: what happens when it fails, and what
       happens on each early return AFTER it.
   4.  Every cast: what is it asserting?
   5.  Every arithmetic expression on sizes: can it overflow?
   6.  Every boundary check: is it adjacent to what it protects?
   7.  Every raw numeric literal: would a named constant have
       carried the reason?
   8.  Every comment claiming an invariant: does the code enforce
       it?
   9.  Every error path: does it leave the program in a state a
       caller could reason about?
   10. The release configuration: is the build that ships the build
       that was tested?
   11. The public interface: is every contract written where a
       caller will read it?
   12. The tests: what would they fail on?
   13. The dependencies: when was each last looked at?
   14. The one thing that will hurt most: would you rather know now?
   15. The naming: LAST, on purpose. Consistent boring naming is
       worth having and it is not a defect.                       */

/* WHAT A REVIEW OF THIS SHAPE FINDS, and a style review does not. */
static void findings(void)
{
    ptr = malloc(n);                    /* no NULL check          */
    if (x) { /* ... */ }                /* no comment, no owner   */
    snprintf(buf, sizeof buf, "%s", u); /* unchecked truncation  */
    return a[i];                        /* bounds come from where? */
    int total = count * sizeof *p;      /* can the multiply wrap?  */
    assert(n < 10);                     /* enforced in release? NO */
    free(p);                            /* ... and on every path?  */
}

/* TWO HABITS THAT KEEP IT HONEST.

   REVIEW THE INTERFACE BEFORE THE IMPLEMENTATION. The interface is
   what cannot be changed cheaply, and everything else is an
   implementation detail of it. Reading the implementation first
   means you have already accepted the interface by the time you
   look at it.

   MAKE THE COMPILER THE FIRST REVIEWER. It finds a class of defect a
   person reading for ownership consistently misses: the missing
   return, the sign comparison, the uninitialised field, the format
   mismatch. Warnings as errors means those never reach a human at
   all.                                                        */

A · Quick Reference

A.1 Types, limits and conversionsA.2 The library calls you will reach forA.3 Terms, and the four that are used wrongly

A.1 Types, limits and conversions

The widths below are the guaranteed minimums and the widths you will actually meet; the difference between the two columns is where portability bugs live.

Two tables and the rules that follow from them

The first column is what the standard guarantees. The second is what a 64-bit Unix platform and a 64-bit Windows platform give you, and the columns disagree in one place that matters. Then the three conversion rules, and each is a requirement. Converting to a narrower signed type when the value does not fit is implementation-defined, so a cast is an assertion that you checked. Converting to an unsigned type from a negative value wraps, which is the mechanism behind the loop that never terminates. And the usual arithmetic conversions promote both operands, so an unsigned operand wins and a comparison against a signed value changes meaning. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Prefer the explicit-width types for anything whose width is a property of your data, so the code states the requirement instead of relying on a platform's choice. And Let the compiler tell you the widths, by printing them once, rather than reasoning about them.

<p>The reason integer types need an appendix rather than a paragraph is that C gives you a choice where most languages give you one, and the choice is invisible at the point of use. On a machine nobody deploys to, int might be 16 bits. On the two platforms everyone does deploy to, int is 32 bits and the interesting difference is between long and long long, because long is 64 bits where the compiler is Unix-like and 32 bits where it is Windows. A structure containing both therefore has a different size on the two, silently, with no diagnostic anywhere - and a structure whose size differs is a structure whose in-memory representation is unreadable by the other side of whatever it was written to.

<p>The conversions are where this becomes a bug rather than a footnote. Narrowing a value to a signed type it does not fit is implementation-defined, which means the standard permits a diagnostic and most compilers produce none. Narrowing to an unsigned type wraps, which means the value becomes a number near the maximum of the range, and that is the mechanism behind the single most common C defect in this guide: a loop written with a signed index tested against an unsigned count, where the comparison converts the index to unsigned and a negative one becomes the largest representable value, so the loop never terminates. It compiles cleanly, it works for every positive input, and it hangs on the empty case.

<p>The usual arithmetic conversions are the mechanism behind the same defect in a different costume. When two operands of different signedness meet, the signed one is converted - so a comparison against a size_t does not compare the two numbers the source appears to show, it compares the numbers after one of them was reinterpreted. The rule that prevents it costs nothing: never compare a signed value against an unsigned one, and where the signs genuinely differ, convert the signed value to a wider signed type explicitly rather than letting the language choose.

<p>Two habits close the whole area. Prefer the explicit-width types from stdint.h wherever the width is a property of your data rather than a habit - a 32-bit identifier, a 64-bit timestamp, a size_t count - so the requirement is stated in the declaration instead of inherited from a platform's choice. And when a width genuinely does matter, ask the compiler rather than the documentation: a static assertion on the size of the type is checked on the platform where it is false, which turns a portability claim into a build failure on the platform that would have broken it.

/* TYPE            MINIMUM (standard)   LP64 Unix   LLP64 Windows */
   char             8 bits             1 byte      1 byte
   signed char      8                  1           1
   unsigned char    8                  1           1
   short           16                  2           2
   int             16                  4           4
   long            32                  8           4     <-- differs
   long long       64                  8           8
   pointer         N/A                 8           8
   size_t          N/A                 8           8
   float          24 bits             4           4
   double         53 bits             8           8
   long double    N/A                16          12
   wchar_t        N/A                  4           2     <-- differs

/* CONVERSION RULES.

   1. NARROWING TO A SIGNED TYPE where the value does not fit is
      IMPLEMENTATION-DEFINED. A cast is an assertion you checked.
   2. TO AN UNSIGNED TYPE from a negative value WRAPS, which is why
      for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; --i) hangs when n is size_t.
   3. USUAL ARITHMETIC CONVERSIONS promote BOTH operands, so an
      unsigned operand wins and x < n changes meaning when n is
      size_t and x is int.                                          */

/* The integer limits are the ones to keep to hand. */
INT_MAX   2147483647          UINT_MAX   4294967295
LONG_MAX  9223372036854775807 on LP64, 2147483647 on LLP64
SIZE_MAX  18446744073709551615 (both)
CHAR_BIT  8

/* Two practices. PREFER THE EXPLICIT-WIDTH TYPES for anything whose
   width is a property of your data - uint32_t for an identifier,
   int64_t for a timestamp, size_t for a count - so the code states
   the requirement instead of relying on a platform's choice. And
   LET THE COMPILER TELL YOU THE WIDTHS: print them once at startup
   rather than reasoning about them.                             */

/* TYPE            MINIMUM   LP64 Unix   LLP64 Windows
   char             8 bits    1 byte      1 byte
   short           16 bits    2 bytes     2 bytes
   int             16 bits    4 bytes     4 bytes
   long            32 bits    8 bytes     4 bytes   <- differs
   long long       64 bits    8 bytes     8 bytes
   pointer         N/A        8 bytes     8 bytes
   size_t          N/A        8 bytes     8 bytes
   float          24 bits     4 bytes     4 bytes
   double         53 bits     8 bytes     8 bytes
   long double    N/A        16 bytes    12 bytes  <- differs
   wchar_t        N/A         4 bytes     2 bytes   <- differs

   THE CONVERSION RULES, and each is a place a bug hides.

   1. NARROWING TO A SIGNED TYPE where the value does not fit is
      IMPLEMENTATION-DEFINED. A cast is an assertion you checked.
   2. TO AN UNSIGNED TYPE from a negative value WRAPS, which is why
      a loop written as for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) hangs
      forever when n is a size_t.
   3. THE USUAL ARITHMETIC CONVERSIONS promote BOTH operands, so an
      unsigned operand wins and x < n changes meaning when n is a
      size_t and x is an int.                                     */

/* THE HANG, in one line, because it is the most common integer
   bug and it looks correct. */
static size_t loop_that_hangs(size_t n)
{
    for (int i = (int)n - 1; i >= 0; i--)   /* i is SIGNED int and
                                               n is UNSIGNED, so
                                               the comparison
                                               converts i, and -1
                                               becomes SIZE_MAX    */
        use(i);
    return 0;
}

/* RIGHT: keep the index unsigned and count down to zero. */
static size_t loop_that_works(size_t n)
{
    for (size_t i = n; i-- > 0; )            /* the idiomatic form */
        use((int)i);
    return 0;
}

/* THE INTEGER LIMITS worth keeping to hand. */
INT_MAX   2147483647            UINT_MAX   4294967295
LONG_MAX  9223372036854775807 (LP64) / 2147483647 (LLP64)
SIZE_MAX  18446744073709551615 on both
CHAR_BIT  8

/* TWO PRACTICES. PREFER THE EXPLICIT-WIDTH TYPES for anything whose
   width is a property of your data - uint32_t for an identifier,
   int64_t for a timestamp, size_t for a count - so the code STATES
   the requirement instead of relying on a platform's choice. And LET
   THE COMPILER TELL YOU THE WIDTHS: print them once at startup
   rather than reasoning about them from documentation. */

A.2 The library calls you will reach for

A working list of the calls that cover most of what systems code needs, with the trap attached to each rather than in a footnote.

Grouped by what you are doing

Then the three rules that apply across the whole list, and each is a requirement. Every function that can fail reports it, and the two channels are the return value and errno. The bounded forms are the ones to use, and their return values say whether truncation happened. And The reentrant forms are the ones to use in threaded code, because the plain ones return a pointer into a shared buffer. Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Learn the trap for each function the first time you use it, rather than the second time something breaks. And Keep the list short and write your own wrappers, so the traps are handled once.

<p>It is worth being clear about what this list is, because it is much shorter than the header files it comes from. The C standard library is large mostly in the area of formatted input and output and Unicode conversion, neither of which most systems code touches directly, and it is small in the area that produces most defects: memory, strings, files, processes and time. Those five groups are the whole of what a systems programmer reaches for, and within them the number of functions with a trap attached is small enough to learn.

<p>Every function in that list communicates failure in one of two ways and never both, which is the single most common source of a missed error in C. Either it returns a value that has a legitimate meaning on failure - NULL from an allocation, which is also a legitimate meaning for a lookup that found nothing - or it returns a sentinel such as minus one and puts the reason in errno. Reading errno when the call succeeded reports a previous error and sends the investigation somewhere else entirely, and reading the return value as the reason is the same mistake in a different place. Check the return first; look at errno second, and only when the return says it failed.

<p>Three traps recur across the whole list, so they are worth naming once rather than attaching to each function. The first is the bounded form that is not bounded in the way the name suggests: strncpy writes a fixed number of bytes and does not terminate when the source is at least as long, so the result is a buffer whose first strlen is undefined. The second is the partial transfer: read and write may move fewer bytes than requested, for entirely ordinary reasons, and a call that does not loop treats a short read as a complete one. The third is the reentrant form that was added because the original returns a pointer into a shared static buffer, which is correct in a single-threaded program and a data race in every other one.

<p>The pragmatic consequence is to write your own thin wrappers around the eight or ten calls you use most, so each trap is handled once instead of at every call site. A bounded copy that refuses rather than truncates. A read loop. An allocate-if-not-out-of-range. Four functions, and every subsequent call site in the codebase gets the behaviour for free - which converts a rule people remember from a rule the program enforces.

/* MEMORY
   malloc(n)              NULL on failure; ALWAYS CHECK
   calloc(n, size)        zeroes; cannot overflow like n*size can
   realloc(p, n)          INVALIDATES p; may move; NULL leaves the
                          original intact - which is the useful case
   free(p)                NULL is fine; double free is not
   aligned_alloc(a, n)    n must be a multiple of a; free() it

   STRINGS
   strlen(s)              scans to NUL; O(n); NO BOUNDS
   strnlen(s, n)          the bounded form; USE THIS
   strcpy / strcat         NO BOUNDS CHECK - do not use
   strncpy(d, s, n)       DOES NOT TERMINATE if s >= n - the trap
   strlcpy / strlcat      terminate AND report the wanted length
   memcpy / memset        length in BYTES; the length is trusted
   memchr(p, c, n)        bounded search; returns a pointer or NULL
   memcmp                 compares BYTES, so it stops at a NUL's
                          position difference, not at a terminator

   CONVERSION
   strtol(s, &end, base)  end == s means no digits parsed
   strtod                 locale-dependent for the decimal point
   snprintf               returns the WANTED length - check it

   TIME
   clock_gettime(MONOTONIC, ...)  for DURATIONS
   time(NULL)                        for wall-clock instants
   localtime_r / gmtime_r            THREAD-SAFE; the non-_r ones
                                     share a static buffer
   strftime                            do not compute dates yourself

   FILES
   open / openat          the at-forms avoid the symlink swap
   fstat                  verify what you actually opened
   read / write           can be PARTIAL; loop until done
   fsync                  the durability decision; explicit
   close                  CHECK IT: the buffer flushes here

   PROCESSES
   pipe2(fds, O_CLOEXEC)  avoid the inherited-writer hang
   fork                   then do as little as possible
   execv(path, argv)      absolute, explicit; argv is an ARRAY
   waitpid                reap, or the entry stays forever
   dup2                   redirect a descriptor in a child

/* Three rules across the whole list.
   1. EVERY CALL THAT CAN FAIL REPORTS IT, and there are exactly
      two channels: the return value and errno.
   2. THE BOUNDED FORMS ARE THE ONES TO USE, and their return
      values are how you detect truncation.
   3. THE REENTRANT FORMS ARE THE ONES TO USE in threaded code;
      the plain ones return a pointer into a SHARED buffer.      */

/* Two practices. LEARN THE TRAP FOR EACH FUNCTION THE FIRST TIME
   YOU USE IT, rather than the second time something breaks - and the
   traps are attached above, not in a footnote. And KEEP THE LIST
   SHORT by writing your own thin wrappers around the eight calls you
   use most, so the traps are handled once instead of at every call
   site. */

/* MEMORY
   malloc(n)              NULL on failure; ALWAYS CHECK
   calloc(n, size)        zeroes; cannot overflow like n*size can
   realloc(p, n)          INVALIDATES p; may move; NULL leaves the
                          original intact - which is the useful case
   free(p)                NULL is fine; a double free is NOT
   aligned_alloc(a, n)    n must be a multiple of a; free() it

   STRINGS
   strlen(s)              scans to NUL; O(n); NO BOUNDS
   strnlen(s, n)          the bounded form; USE THIS
   strcpy / strcat         NO BOUNDS CHECK - do not use
   strncpy(d, s, n)       DOES NOT TERMINATE if s is at least n
   strlcpy / strlcat      terminate AND report the wanted length
   memcpy / memset        length in BYTES; the length is trusted
   memchr(p, c, n)        bounded search; pointer or NULL
   memcmp                 compares BYTES, so it is not a string
                          comparison: it stops caring where a NUL
                          difference is, not at the terminator

   CONVERSION
   strtol(s, &end, base)  end == s means no digits were parsed
   strtod                 locale-dependent for the decimal point
   snprintf               returns the WANTED length - check it

   TIME
   clock_gettime(MONOTONIC, ...)   for DURATIONS
   time(NULL)                       for wall-clock instants
   localtime_r / gmtime_r           THREAD-SAFE; the non-_r forms
                                    share a static buffer
   strftime                          do not compute dates yourself

   FILES
   open / openat          the at-forms avoid the symlink swap
   fstat                  verify what you actually opened
   read / write           can be PARTIAL; loop until done
   fsync                  the durability decision; explicit
   close                  CHECK IT: the buffer flushes here

   PROCESSES
   pipe2(fds, O_CLOEXEC)  avoid the inherited-writer hang
   fork                   then do as little as possible
   execv(path, argv)      absolute and explicit; argv is an ARRAY
   waitpid                reap, or the table entry never goes away
   dup2                   redirect a descriptor in a child        */

/* THREE RULES ACROSS THE WHOLE LIST:
   1. EVERY CALL THAT CAN FAIL REPORTS IT, and there are exactly two
      channels: the return value and errno.
   2. THE BOUNDED FORMS ARE THE ONES TO USE, and their return
      values are how you detect truncation.
   3. THE REENTRANT FORMS ARE THE ONES TO USE in threaded code; the
      plain forms return a pointer into a SHARED static buffer. */

/* TWO PRACTICES. LEARN THE TRAP FOR EACH FUNCTION THE FIRST TIME
   YOU USE IT, rather than the second time something breaks - and the
   traps are attached above, not in a footnote. And KEEP THE LIST
   SHORT by writing your own thin wrappers around the eight calls
   you use most, so the traps are handled once instead of at every
   call site. */

A.3 Terms, and the four that are used wrongly

Four words in C are used more loosely than the standard defines them, and the looseness is where most arguments about the language go wrong.

Four terms, defined and corrected

Then the three conventions worth adopting, and each is a requirement. A buffer is a pointer and a length, never a pointer alone. Ownership is stated in the type or the name, never inferred from context. And A cast is an assertion you checked, with a comment saying what was checked.
