Then the two practices, and each is a discipline. Use the standard's word in a comment, so that a reader knows the term means what the language means. And When a comment says the word, check that the code satisfies the definition, because the comment is a claim and the code is the fact.

<p>Four terms in this guide are used loosely in ordinary conversation and precisely in the specification, and the difference is not pedantry in each case - it is the difference between a rule you can follow and a rule you cannot. "String" is the clearest: precisely, a null-terminated sequence of char, which means a NUL cannot appear in the content. Used loosely, it means any sequence of characters, including arbitrary binary data. Every string function is safe on the first meaning and unsafe on the second, and the gap between them is where a file read as text and then truncated at the first zero byte comes from.

<p>"Undefined behaviour" is the one that causes the most argument, because it is regularly confused with "unspecified", which is a much milder condition. Unspecified means any of a defined set of results is valid and the program remains meaningful - two compilers may legitimately order two independent evaluations differently, and both are correct. Undefined means the standard imposes no requirement at all: the program may do anything, including produce a value no conforming machine could produce, and a compiler is permitted to assume the situation cannot arise and delete the code that would have detected it. That last clause is why an undefined-behaviour bug is not merely wrong but unrepairable by inspection - the code that would have caught it may no longer exist in the binary.

<p>"Array" is the third, and it matters because of what happens to one. Precisely, an array is an object whose size is part of its type. It decays to a pointer to its first element in almost every context, which is why passing an array to a function passes a pointer and loses the length. So a comment saying "pass an array" is hiding exactly the information that gets forgotten, and an interface that says "pass a buffer" is the same problem stated more clearly.

<p>"Null pointer" is the fourth, and the confusion is between two states that behave differently in the one place you can test. Precisely, the null pointer is not the address of any object, and it is distinct from every non-null address. Used loosely, "points to nothing" also describes a pointer to an object that has been freed - which is not null, cannot be detected with a comparison, and is the dangling case. Only the first can be tested for, and code that sets a pointer to NULL after freeing it is doing something specific: turning the second failure mode into a harmless one on the paths that check.

<p>The practical discipline that follows is short. Use the specification's word in a comment, so that whoever reads it knows the term means what the language means. And when a comment uses one of these four terms, treat the comment as a claim and the code as the fact, and check that the code satisfies the claim - because a comment that asserts a buffer is null-terminated is documentation until someone reads the allocation.

/* 1. "ARRAY". Used loosely for two different things.

   PRECISE: an object of array type, whose SIZE is part of its type
   and which therefore decays to a pointer to its first element
   almost everywhere.

   LOOSE: any contiguous run of things, including the interior of a
   buffer and the result of a pointer arithmetic expression.

   The distinction matters because "pass an array" means "pass a
   POINTER and separately pass the length", and a comment that says
   "pass an array" hides the thing that is most often forgotten.   */

/* 2. "STRING". Used loosely for three different things.

   PRECISE: a null-terminated sequence of char, where the
   terminator is part of the data and a NUL therefore cannot appear
   in the content.

   LOOSE: any sequence of characters, including arbitrary binary
   bytes, a network packet, and a buffer with an explicit length.

   The distinction matters because every string function is safe on
   the first meaning and unsafe on the other two.                    */

/* 3. "NULL POINTER". Used loosely for two different things.

   PRECISE: a pointer value that is not the address of any object -
   the null pointer constant, which is distinct from every
   non-null address.

   LOOSE: "points to nothing", which is also true of a pointer that
   points to an object that has been freed, and which is the
   dangling-pointer case. THE TWO ARE NOT EQUIVALENT and only the
   first can be tested for.                                        */

/* 4. "UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR". Used loosely to mean "unspecified".

   PRECISE: the standard imposes NO REQUIREMENT. The program may do
   anything at all, including produce an answer that no possible
   machine could produce, and the compiler may assume it cannot
   happen and delete the code that would have caught it.

   LOOSE: "we do not specify what this does", which is the much
   milder UNSPECIFIED BEHAVIOUR - where any of a defined set of
   results is valid and the program remains meaningful.

   The distinction is the whole reason some "it works on my machine"
   bugs cannot be reasoned about: undefined behaviour is not a bug,
   it is a permission, and a permission can be exercised.          */

/* Three conventions worth adopting.
   1. A BUFFER IS A POINTER AND A LENGTH, never a pointer alone.
   2. OWNERSHIP IS STATED in the type or the name, never inferred
      from context.
   3. A CAST IS AN ASSERTION YOU CHECKED, with a comment saying
      what was checked.                                             */

/* Two practices. USE THE STANDARD'S WORD IN A COMMENT, so a reader
   knows the term means what the language means. And WHEN A COMMENT
   USES THE WORD, check the code satisfies the definition - because
   the comment is a claim and the code is the fact. */

/* 1. "ARRAY". Used loosely for two different things.

   PRECISE: an object of array type, whose SIZE is part of its type
   and which therefore decays to a pointer to its first element in
   almost every context.

   LOOSE: any contiguous run of things, including the interior of a
   buffer and the result of pointer arithmetic.

   The distinction matters because "pass an array" means "pass a
   POINTER and separately pass the length", and a comment that says
   "pass an array" hides the thing that is most often forgotten.   */

/* 2. "STRING". Used loosely for three different things.

   PRECISE: a null-terminated sequence of char, where the terminator
   is PART OF THE DATA and a NUL therefore cannot appear in the
   content.

   LOOSE: any sequence of characters, including arbitrary binary
   bytes, a network packet, and a buffer with an explicit length.

   The distinction matters because every string function is safe on
   the first meaning and unsafe on the other two.                    */

/* 3. "NULL POINTER". Used loosely for two different things.

   PRECISE: a pointer value that is not the address of any object -
   the null pointer constant, which is DISTINCT from every non-null
   address.

   LOOSE: "points to nothing", which is also true of a pointer to an
   object that has been freed, which is the DANGLING case. THE TWO
   ARE NOT EQUIVALENT, and only the first can be tested for.        */

/* 4. "UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR". Used loosely to mean "unspecified".

   PRECISE: the standard imposes NO REQUIREMENT. The program may do
   anything at all, including produce an answer no possible machine
   could produce, and the compiler may assume it cannot happen and
   delete the code that would have caught it.

   LOOSE: "we do not specify what this does", which is the much
   milder UNSPECIFIED BEHAVIOUR - where any of a defined set of
   results is valid and the program remains meaningful.

   The distinction is the whole reason some "it works on my machine"
   bugs cannot be reasoned about: undefined behaviour is not a bug,
   it is a PERMISSION, and a permission can be exercised.          */

/* THREE CONVENTIONS WORTH ADOPTING.
   1. A BUFFER IS A POINTER AND A LENGTH, never a pointer alone.
   2. OWNERSHIP IS STATED in the type or the name, never inferred
      from context.
   3. A CAST IS AN ASSERTION YOU CHECKED, with a comment saying
      what was checked.                                             */

/* TWO PRACTICES. USE THE SPECIFICATION'S WORD in a comment, so a
   reader knows the term means what the language means. And WHEN A
   COMMENT USES THE WORD, check the code satisfies the definition -
   because the comment is a claim and the code is the fact. */
